The name is not struck out, the objection is entered in a margin in a clerk's own name and initialled, **and the objection does not suspend the renewal.** There is no scene. **Tomas stands four feet away with a bell half a mile off and does not move.** Renn tells him at the sixth hour that there is not a bell in this town that says anything about a wagon, and Tomas says out loud, in front of the ward, that the reason is not law and not courage: a bell cannot be made to say a thing it was not bound to say, anything else would be a version, and there is already a version on the ferry road. Absalom Teage gives his reason out loud unasked. **Crake comes down the lane without a black case and says the truest sentence anybody in the commission has said all month: a ward that publishes a procedure can make an office legible and cannot make an office stop; the Crown's paper became good enough to be worth reading, the reading is done by a clerk in a building, the clerk is right, and the child goes; and careful is not the same as effective, and they are all about to find out how different those are on the twenty-eighth.** He says he is going to write the word *clean* on a page about a morning on which he had thirty-seven houses and a child. **Tomas spends the whole round writing down what the ward actually did, house by house, in three columns headed — not *position*, not *answer* — *first thing, second thing, third thing*, fifty-one lines, checkable at any door** — including 108, including 133, and including the fact that Mrs Corris's own name in the column is half of the single worst fact of the day and that nobody noticed. Perry returns the paper he wrote to her on Day 15, and he does not take it back, and Mercy Slee enters that the reason he did not is *that he has learned the one thing this month, which is that a man who takes a thing back off a person who is frightened has had it twice.* Oren records three answers in three columns and states, in the watch's answer, that the word *standing* in that office means, in this ward, that a person stood in a lane and was heard. **Tamsin's route is not used and she states four reasons in public, the fourth of which is *I cannot hear it*, and about nine people hear the difference — **and then she publishes the fact of it herself, on purpose, in the same lane**, which breaks the Chapter 29 rule that nobody in Kells was to be told. **This is deliberate and is recorded as a canon change.**** The Crown's answer is nailed at half past the eleventh hour and is the shortest notice the commission has ever put on that board: the ward's practice is **a matter for the district and no longer for the local court**; the renewal is **posted again for the twenty-eighth day**; and, in a second paragraph in the small hand, **the district will consider the renewal of the ward's own line, of the possession of the ground in which it is laid, and of the persons under seventeen in the Low Ward.** The Crown has spent nine days building a procedure and has found a cheaper one: the ward's own unasked question, asked out loud, in the Crown's hand, in five words. And at the last hour Serjeant Teale reads a fourth paragraph, which is a question and not a notice: **the district will accept any answer** to whether the ward intends to remove the persons under seventeen from the ward before the next renewal and **to whom**, will certify it, and **a certified answer will be a return.** Nobody answers. Tomas's four things are on a fresh slate on the bench: the route fragment, the three column-headings, the copy of the schedule co-held with Mercy Slee, and the custody receipt with his correction in the margin. **The trial handbell is in Crown custody in a district building, bound and unspent, never rung. The fixed carrier is in the watch store in its frame in a padded cradle, and the joint receipt on its file expired at the fourth hour of Day 10 and there has still never been a custody review. The half-barrel is empty. The door has three seals on it and all three are still on.**

## Chapter 0041 — What a Page Is For

Day 22, full day, Tomas, with Nell and Mercy. Hask's six men are back at the low end of the turn **with the crowbar in the ground four inches**, and **Renn's gate has still not come up** and the Crown cart is still across the culvert under a works order the Crown's own papers carry as current. A **printed extract of Collis's return** comes up on the morning post and is read at the ward board: **thirty-seven doors, the form exhibited entire, twenty-two objections in margins in a clerk's own name and initialled, nine of them recording the answer *I am not permitted to say*, one person removed at the fifth hour and thirty minutes, and the *Witnessed by* column filled in two lines and not cleared between rounds** — with Collis's four sentences about a form, a round, and a surveyor who no longer knows what a form is for. **Mrs Corris says the second number, and Nell reads both and refuses to let the lane have either without the other.** Tomas hands his own **fifty-one-line page to Mercy Slee** and keeps nothing. He is asked about the market lane four times and refuses four times; at the fifth ask, by **Absalom Teage** in a lane with about sixty people, he gives the same reason he gave on the twentieth and will not improve it, and says he is not going to give anybody a version. Then **Teage begins asking one question at each door — what did you do on the twenty-first, and who saw you do it — gets to nineteen doors by the second hour of the afternoon, and writes his own answer at 114: *Nothing. Stood. Two hours. I have a boy of nine.*** Tomas watches from his own gate and does not help. **The Crown's answer is not a notice but a second-series supplementary return** to every household before the close of the twenty-sixth, with the reason field the ward demanded, a witness field, no signature, and a certification. Mercy Slee names it: it is the three questions in a Crown hand with the argument taken out. **The chapter's sentence, entered by Oren in the gate book: a procedure can make an office legible and cannot make an office stop — and the page is not the town's instrument, it is what the town has instead of a reason.**

## Chapter 0042 — The Name in the Column

Day 23, full day, **Nell** with Mercy Slee and **Mrs Wilde of 108**. Nell wants the Wilde household at the board; Mercy refuses her the argument and then gives it back — that it is a good argument and it is about *her*, and she has been letting it be about her for nine days. The alignment is named and **not spent**: *I have got something and I am not going to use it.* At 108, Mrs Wilde is on the floor of a front room with neighbours and is described before she speaks: she is not grief, she is a person waiting to find out whether she is going to be told. She refuses for nine seconds, then sets the terms: **she will read the question at the side of the clerk's table with her back to the lane, she asks to be named before she stands, and she names her own price in advance** — a file on house 108, forever, and being the one who is looked at. Her grounds: **eleven letters to Fourmile in two years, four answers, three saying the return stands, one saying a return is not amended except at a renewal, and a daughter of ten in a wagon because of a name her mother-in-law wrote before the daughter was born.** She quotes Tamsin's refusal to ring at the next one back as the reason she has chosen which version of herself gets written down. Nell publishes the **second column question** in a household's own words — *is there a name in the Witnessed by column on my line; is that person living; whose hand is it in* — and knocks it at **eleven doors** with no slate, because a slate is a notice. The finding is that **the never-cleared column is a practice and not one household's bad luck, and that it exists on lines the Crown has never acted on** — including a house at 44 that has never been marked and never visited, carrying a dead elder sister's name. A Crown officer comes to the ward board to ask whether a **list of households intending to remove children** exists, and **Nell has the word *none* entered in three hands**; he says he did not want to be the one asking it and will not write that down either.

## Chapter 0043 — The District

Day 24, full day, **Oren**, with Tomas in the evening. A district form at the second hour requires keeper **names for seven days commencing with the twenty-first, a day already elapsed.** Oren neither obeys nor refuses: he accepts the hours, the places and every bell, declines the names, and adds a **second reason in his own hand — that he knows who sounded the four bells in the market lane on the nineteenth, has not said it in any book, and enters it so that he will not hold a private file. He names nobody.** He also enters his own Day 10 conduct, his Day 17 signature and the eleven names, so that if an office ever says the watch withheld, it will find it on a page and not in an assembly. At the seventh hour he walks the register down to the north gate himself and **permits the district's rider to read it on the parapet in a lane in front of about nine people, at the watch captain's own risk and with his reason entered first**; the rider reads all twelve entries of the nineteenth and the word *none* twelve times and says a Crown man in Fourmile is going to want a name. In the evening Oren does the arithmetic he has avoided: **the four papers of the fortnight all end in the word *names*, and he supplied every one of them** — the eleven, the twelve *none*s, the refusal, and the *none* entered under the girl of sixteen. He says he has built a town out of names without entering a ward, and that he has done it with his own hand. The district's return comes back on its own horse and **names, before the register has arrived, the officer who will read it: District Officer Marten Cope, of the district renewal office at Fourmile**, and Oren reads the name out loud, has Jerm enter it, and says the sentence of the day: *it has been a desk for eleven years and there is a name in it, and I have only just worked out that never was a comfort.*

## Chapter 0044 — The Name in the Margin

Day 24, evening, **Tomas** with **Tamsin Rook** at the low drain gate and afterwards in his own yard. **His four days of silence end and he says the market lane out loud to her first**, including that she could not tell whether he rang it once or four times, because she felt it in four feet of stone and cannot hear a note and will not hear one again. She tells him the whole of the same fact from her end and neither of them can fix the other's: **a signal that goes further than its keeper can govern is not a fault in the signal, and a keeper who cannot hear where it went is the same problem from the other side, and two people who are extremely good at this cannot do the work for each other and have to go on.** **He does not read the trial handbell's custody receipt to her and the chapter does not have him do it.** He gives her the leaf and goes up the hill; afterwards, alone in his own yard with a lamp, he takes out the **custody receipt — his margin correction, *Object received bound. No answer recorded at taking. Maker has not certified the binding*, and a single initial under it that is not a name and is not agreement** — and says out loud, to nobody, that an initial in a margin is a man saying *I have seen this* and going back to his desk, and that the only difference between that and agreement is that one of them has to come back to the door. **He writes the request** — a leaf off the day-book with a heading, three numbered lines (the answer may be no; no reason required or asked for; he may not know in advance), a finding on the other side of the fold, and a witness line — and **gives it to Jerm** and does not press. She reads it in about a second and a half, does not read the other side, tells him she is not going to answer tonight, and says she will not be dignified about it. **The chapter does not say what she has chosen.** She goes up the towpath and does not come back down it. In the yard he sets the plain carrier out in the open, looks at it, and realises the claim lives in four pieces of paper and that on the twenty-seventh it will have to stop carrying it. **He hears, or does not hear, and could not afterwards have said which, a sound once off the river, out in the dark, where there is no route and no door; and there is nothing there when he listens.**

## Chapter 0045 — The Ward's Answer

Day 25, morning into evening, **Nell** with Oren and Mercy Slee. Nell finally asks her two people: **Mercy Slee, who agrees on conditions, and Mrs Wilde of 108**, who refuses for nine seconds and then takes the whole of the arrangement back from Nell in reverse — *sit me at the side, and put my name first, and I want it entered that I asked what it cost before I said yes.* The market court sits at the ninth hour with about **two hundred and thirty people**. **Oren Pike certifies the room and refuses to certify the contents**, for the first time in eleven years, and gives the reason in the open. Nell reads the ward's answer: three columns, each separately certifiable, the three not certifiable together, and **the third column empty, because a certification is a witness and a witness is a person and a third column is not a person** — and when the room counts seven, she answers that an empty column can be counted two ways and both counts are true, and a sentence is the one thing a Crown office has never had to check. **Mrs Wilde reads her own question at the side of the table with her back to the lane, once, and sits down, and the room goes absolutely silent.** **Three supplementary returns are on the board and Nell has three house numbers copied onto the back of the pinned schedule: 114, 133 and 121**, of which **121 was written on the afternoon of the twenty-fourth** and the chapter dates no other; Chapter 41 has two households answering by the morning of the twenty-third, which is what makes three. The first column has one household and the second twenty-five and the third is empty. Between about thirty and forty households then come one at a time over five hours and say one of three things themselves. **Mrs Corris of 133 puts her hand up in a full court and takes the Crown surveyor's certification, says why, and says out loud that she will not be asked about it; the ward does not treat it as betrayal, and she names that herself.** **Tomas is in the court for five hours, says nothing, rings nothing, and is not asked once**, and Mercy Slee tells him so and asks him to write *nobody asked, nobody turned round* as a fact and not a lesson. **The district's reply is an instruction to Crown Officer Ambrose Rudd to attend at the ninth hour on the twenty-eight and produce four things** — the full return entire, both series of supplementary returns with the house numbers, and the authority to ascertain what works of metal are in the ground of the Vale Bellhouse yard — plus a fourth line under the fold requiring the watch to say whether any bell is bound to a claim and by whom, **not accepting *none* as an answer and not accepting it as a refusal.**

## Chapter 0046 — The Measure

Day 26, full day, **Tomas** with **Iven Sore** and **Sabin Fell**. The Measure sits on its own notice. Sabin is not in the room and nobody mentions it. Sore takes the bell item first and states his limitation before opening: the Measure has not seen the bell since the nineteenth, has not heard it and does not propose to, and everything it says is said from a record and not from an ear. **He is right, and about something that is not the trial handbell.** He reads four clauses of craft law: a hearth stock is not a traceable source; a bell with no certificate of stock cannot be made certifiable afterwards; a Crown declaration does not touch the making; **but a bell made on declared ground out of an untraceable stock is a bell that exists, is in use, is carried on a public route, and whose maker cannot show where the metal came from, and that is a hazard to the public created by the declaration and not by the maker** — and he says he did not see clause four on the nineteenth and that it was in front of him. The four houses want the licence; **Sore is one of the four and says he is right and is right about a different thing.** Tomas does not defend himself, refuses to use Sabin's absent kindness as a lever, and says in an open room that **he does not know what a bell is for.** The Measure enters the narrow finding: **a professional bar on the plain carrier**, not on the licence, with the four houses' request refused; the Measure will not certify, inventory, attend with a spade, or supply the commission with anything. And **in the room Sabin asked not to be in, Tomas decides to put the Measure's road map on the record in writing** — that the map is a craft map of refusals and not a schedule of non-response, and that the district is asked to enter that. It goes on the minute book and a copy goes to the market court. **Sabin finds out within three hours, comes up the lane at dusk, and is entitled to be angry and is** — the argument is that Tomas answered for the craft's record without asking the man whose life's work it is, and the sentence he leaves across forty feet of cobbles is: *I was in a hurry, and being in a hurry felt like duty, and it was not duty, it was the other thing.* He asks for the copy **not to read, to put in his own drawer and to know it exists**, which is the only thing he is entitled to. **At the door, alone, Sore repeats his Chapter 14 position word for word — he still will not say he did not sign it — and puts the one question he can support, which is a question and which Tomas cannot use: did the Crown withdraw a commission the Bellwrights' Measure granted, or did it withdraw a name?** The chapter records that Sore also warned him on the morning of the nineteenth and that the Measure will not put a man's survival and a professional finding in the same place in his head.

## Chapter 0047 — The Line Under the Yard

Day 27, full day, **Tomas** with **Oren** and **Jerm**. The authority to open the yard exists because **Oren enclosed a fair copy of the watch gate book's entries for the fifteenth with his return of the seventeenth**, and his own three sentences about a damp line under a bellmaker's floor are why a district sent a schedule. The three decide separately — Oren that the watch will attend, admit nobody, not obstruct, and not take part in the finding; Jerm that he will write out in advance what he expects to see at each hour; **Tomas that the floor is opened in daylight in front of the ward, because a record of an event made in an empty yard is the Crown's account of his yard** — and he prices the cost before the act, naming the bench-books, the forty people, and the **Crown lock register that echoes a name in a book of his.** **Oren refuses, in the lane in front of Collis, the district's requirement that the watch say whether any bell is bound to a claim and by whom**, and Collis refuses to certify the refusal, in her own return. The **Crown wax is broken at the Crown's own request**; the floor comes up at the fourth hour; the books come out into daylight; at about the seventh hour the line is lying open in a trench four feet deep and nine feet long **and does not go toward the millrace — it turns under the front of the shop and goes out under the wall facing the north road.** A bearing is taken and **read out in a lane in front of about forty people.** **Nobody says what it means. Oren does not write it. Jerm enters that he did not write it and why.** The floor is laid again and a bar of the Bellwrights' own ash is laid across the damp strip. **Crake applies in writing for the millrace bridge bell** — a properly made paper with a keeper, a limit and a return date — and **Oren grants it in the lane in six lines before about thirty people, naming Jerm, limiting it to the north gate for one morning, and entering his own reason: that a watch which tells the Crown it has no instrument is handing the Crown a reason to invent one.** Collis's objection is entered before the grant, not after. **The district copyist rides out at about the eleventh hour having never in nineteen years put a bearing in a finding, and tells Tomas at the gate, and Tomas writes four words: *He said it to me. He wanted it said.***

## Chapter 0048 — First Light

Day 28, dawn into morning, **Tomas** with the ward. **Before light, Gib Hask's six men take ninety feet of Crown backfill out of the low-turn culvert with a bar and a spade**, put it on the ward side and take none of it away; he comes to Tomas's gate and says he has done it for Renn's gate and not for the round, and Tomas tells him that sixty men at a flood gate is going to help somebody fill in a Crown form, and Hask says write it down. **Renn's gate comes up at about ten past five, hard, on the first pull, and about sixty people watch.** In the yard Tomas **withdraws the water claim** — the ward board's copy and Renn's oilskin-board line — and **burns both in the ash-pit in the dark**, because a paper you keep is a paper somebody finds in nine years; Renn holds his sentence and agrees in advance to be contradicted by his own bell. **Wenna Cray withdraws in writing — her own record is stamped the fifth hour and she comes down the lane at about a quarter to six — in her own words, before the bell is sounded and without knowing whether it will be**, and her ground is that her four clauses were the ward board and two doors, and that a Crown officer with a spade asked her yesterday which bell was bound and she would not say and he did not need to, and that a duty she cannot stand behind is a signature. **The count is one.** Tomas rings once at about twenty minutes to the sixth hour and the words are on the page entire: the known facts, the unknown facts, his own duty, **that the bell is barred by the Measure on the twenty-sixth and he is ringing it anyway and nobody made him**, the keeper, the count of one, his own name, and that it is too bright and it repeats within eight feet of a wall and he cannot make it stop. **It tells nobody what to do.** Jerm writes the bar beside the hour because the maker asked him to. **Jerm sounds the millrace bridge bell twice at the north gate at the sixth hour under the lawful grant**, and the two sounds are ninety seconds apart. The Crown comes up the west road and the half-closure is a bar, a chain and a man with a lamp; **a wagon goes over the bank at the mill narrows and takes the top four of Alis Marrow's mother's steps into the water.** The ward does four things in four columns: **nine moved, twenty-one stayed, eleven children in eight other people's doors, four doors refused.** **Foreman Crane asks Tamsin Rook at a quarter past five, in his own words, and she walks her whole route and rings once at the landing door and stands there while he says his own words in his own voice, and she does not ring again, and does not ring past the low drain gate, and cannot hear it and asks nobody.** Nell gets **thirty-one children off the landing in forty minutes** from a slate she has had since the fourteenth day. Oren holds the north gate from the sixth hour to the ninth and does not put two men in a lane. **House 114: Absalom Teage produces his own Crown return of the twenty-second in his own hand, the district's file says the boy was delivered on the twenty-sixth, the list says he is here, the cart did not come, and a Crown officer of twenty-six will not contradict his own district in a lane.** Tomas is four streets away and hears about it afterwards and does not improve it. **Rudd attends at the ninth hour with the four things, finds that the third was discharged the day before by a Crown surveyor who was not asked to, and reads out a district finding that the ward's bells are unaccounted for. Tomas stands up and certifies his own words as the Crown's evidence against himself, asks for the bar to stand beside the hour, and has the district's sentence read three times by a copyist with a cough.** The chapter ends on Crake's own answer to the two-line form, filed that afternoon and not seen in Kells until the twenty-ninth: the ward co-operated, no violence was committed on any Crown officer, and the bells are unaccounted for, which is a different question and is not disorder, *and I have put the two on the same piece of paper because the office has given me one piece of paper.*

## Chapter 0049 — What Burns

Day 28, morning into evening, **Tomas** with Oren and Nell. At the tenth hour **Rudd produces a fifth schedule**: the workshop's records are Crown material within the L-4 matter, are to be taken and copied, and **the originals are to be destroyed in the yard in daylight in the presence of the ward with the hour entered by a copyist.** Rudd says four words about it in a lane and they are on the page: *It is a form. It is a schedule. It is right.* Four Crown officers take **six people** between the tenth hour and the second hour of the afternoon, and **the watch's only part is that Oren writes the record of each arrest in his own hand — the ground, the hour, the place, the officer, the name, and whether the person was told and whether a reason was given — and gives each of them a copy, because Rudd asks him for it and says he could produce six arrests without asking a question of the watch.** The six and their grounds: **Gib Hask** under the Crown works order, for ninety feet of backfill taken out at night; **Mrs Wilde of 108** as the household of record on a line in the *Witnessed by* column the district cannot produce a living witness to; **Wenna Cray**, entered by the watch captain on the seventeenth for public standing and on the twenty-eighth for withdrawing a duty in public; **Ivo Prentice**, entered on the same list for a petition and for stating in public that the Crown's cart was still across the culvert; **Louisa Tace of 44**, a house never marked and never visited, entered because she put the column question at her own door; and **Absalom Teage of 114**, whose own return saved his son three hours earlier. **House 108 is marked again and no child is taken, and Collis asks the household to countersign the objection and not the answer, and the household countersigns the objection** — a surveyor's name in the column beside a dead woman's, for the first time. The six names are read out in the afternoon and **all six came off the district's amended return of the seventeenth in Oren's own hand**, and Oren writes the two-paragraph statement he has never taken out. At the half past two the Crown party goes up the lane with about a hundred and forty people behind them; Tomas gives up the L-4 papers himself, naming each one aloud; **the copyist comes to the bench-books, and one of them is for Reckoning 619, and the district copyist says out loud that he has never had a schedule with a year in it in it before.** **The bench-book for Reckoning 619 is burned in the ash-pit in three goes at the third hour, in daylight, in front of about a hundred and forty people, with the hour and the page numbers entered.** **Tomas rings nothing and asks nobody**, and gives Mercy Slee the reason in a lane — *a bell cannot stop a fire, and a bellmaker who thinks it can has stopped being a maker* — and writes the day's three-column record in thirty-one lines and gives it away. **He tears a leaf out of the running bench-book and gives it to Bevin Sarr, who has refused three times to be a witness and is on no list, and Sarr's answer is: *not one word to anybody, and if anybody comes and asks me, I have never had it.*** A Crown officer's armful of L-4 papers goes down on the bench on top of the watch's open duplicate register and **Sarr lifts the page clear and hands it to Nell Fenn**, and it is the surviving register fragment. The yard is sealed at the fifth hour with a district wax and **Oren's watch mark under it** — nineteen words, and no opinion. The millrace bridge bell comes off the gate bracket and goes back up the hill and is sounded once on the way by Jerm, unasked. The trial handbell is produced unwrapped in the market court and identified by Tomas, who produces his own receipt in a lane, and Collis puts it in her return unasked. **Tomas is taken at the sixth hour and asks for one thing, which is to see his custody receipt, and reads the margin aloud in front of about sixty people, and the copyist's initial is still not agreement.** The last thing out of the yard was a page.

## Chapter 0050 — Before Dawn

Day 33, before dawn, **Tomas**. Days 29 to 32 are compressed in the opening, in the order written, with the hours: taken to a district building at Fourmile on the night of the twenty-eighth; **Mrs Wilde released on the thirtieth** because the district cannot hold a household of record on a witness column for a second day longer without a witness and Collis refuses to certify her line again, **and the other five before the district on the same day and released together on the thirty-first**; five men and one woman released without charge on the thirty-first on a single sheet in **Crake's own hand**, Tomas released the same day and in Kells by the second; **Crake relieved of the commission on the thirtieth for recording disorder and co-operation on one paper** and moved to a works office, and nobody in Kells will ever know; **the Crown cart off the culvert on the thirty-first at about the eleventh hour** and the backfill back in on the **thirty-second** under a works order signed by a foreman who had been signing them for nineteen years, uncompacted and unchecked; **Foreman Brann's gang's book entry on the thirty-second that the ground part of the Day 8 plan came up with the works order from Fourmile on a folded third sheet in a hand that is not a road hand**, with the nineteenth's entries still illegible; and **Nell Fenn spending the nine *not returned* and six *accompanying* lines on the thirtieth as a checkable question by the north post, the question going up on the thirty-first, and the district's two-paragraph reply on the thirty-second** — it will answer any line in an officer's own name, it regrets calling a schedule non-response, and it notes that the respondent is a person of sixteen of the household of line twenty-two and does not propose to ask why — **pinned on the ward board beside the pinned schedule, and no household on any of the fifteen lines has yet come to ask.** At about the third hour of the morning of the thirty-third a **custody review** is held at the watch house, on Oren's four-word note, because **District Officer Marten Cope wrote on the second that the district cannot answer whether any bell in this ward is joined to any line, wire or ground and needs the only instrument in the ward whose manner of working is recorded** — which is the reason Oren arranged in Chapter 3 and the last chance to make it. **Tamsin Rook unwraps her own bell and sets it on the cloth; Tomas reads the wear from a distance and does not touch it; nothing is rung.** The comparison shows **a technique and not a person**: the courier bell's crown re-cut inside by a chisel about a sixteenth of an inch with a **riffle file** mark at four points, and the fixed carrier's coupling cut nearly through with a copper jumper across it, both at two removes from a Crown pattern, both ordinary work, both nameless — and the service ticket's ***answered in office, L-4 ward roll, per local practice*** with the mark **OFF. 411** is read out again with **no person behind it and none this month.** Collis's return **withdraws the district's finding that the ward's bells are unaccounted for and makes no finding against the watch or any person.** **Oren releases the same fixed stripped carrier to Tomas in its mounting frame, bolts released only for transport, the frame in its reinforced padded cradle, no clapper and none to be furnished, four clauses and a fifth that Tomas asks for and Ferrow writes: that the receipt does not make the holder a keeper of any route and that no answer given to any signal on or by the object may be entered as the answer of a household of this ward.** **Tamsin gives back the Day 24 request with one line on the outside of the fold — no, no reason, and you did not press me once — and travels with him on a slate Mercy Slee wrote, which neither of them wrote first, which puts a leash on both of them, and which she read to each of them separately**, and she says out loud in a lane that **a warning does not require her to obey him.** The Vale Bellhouse sign hangs crooked over a locked door with the district's wax on it and the watch captain's mark under it. On the river road she rings the handbell **once** at about the seventh hour, waits, and gets an answer **from Ocklebridge, a village of about two hundred that has not yet heard the Crown's order**, and does not ring again. **Nobody in this volume asks her for a reason.** His day-book ends on the two questions: who taught a Crown office to turn one clerk's answer into a whole settlement's consent, and where are the names being sent when they are registered.

---

## Volume 01 close — what the audit changed on the page, and where

Recorded so that no summary of a summary is read as evidence. Full record: `reviews/volume-01/volume-audit.md`.

- **Chapters 4, 11, 23, 33, 35, 36, 37, 38, 40 — fourteen lines carrying weekday names removed, across nine chapters.** Six were replaced with the numbered day the page already fixes (the fifteenth for the trench, the twenty-first for the round, the nineteenth for Marda Hodler's move, the twentieth for the market day); four had no derivable date and were made unanchored instead (*market day*, *at the landing*, *from the start of next week*, *at the next one either*). **No weekday is named in any chapter of Volume 01.**
- **Chapters 31, 39, 40, 42, 45, 46, 50 — eight ages removed that the narrator or a passing line had no right to supply:** Foreman Brann at `ch31:17` and `ch46:189`; Mrs Corris at `ch39:75`, `ch40:21`, `ch40:106` and `ch50:169`; Mrs Wilde at `ch45:155`; and the household of forty-four at `ch42:175`. **Mrs Corris is sixty-five on her own hand and in her own mouth only** (`ch39:97`, `ch45:9`, `ch45:137`), and Mercy Slee's *about forty* in five chapters is recorded and not removed.
- **Chapter 33 — the Crown clapper is no longer named in a character's mouth.** *carried by a clapper nobody in this ward put in* replaces *carried by a Crown clapper*, and `state/batch-0004-summary.md` was corrected with it.
- **Chapter 48's ringing now has one hour. It had three.** Cray's written record (`ch48:59`) and the narration (`ch48:75`) said *twenty minutes to the sixth hour*; the district's finding (`ch48:211`), Tomas's written certification (`ch48:227`) and the closing reflection (`ch48:161`) said *twenty minutes to five*. **Jerm's watch-book entry at `ch48:91` is stamped the fifth hour, which is the arbiter**, and the three lines were aligned to the sixth hour. This is the volume's climax and its central document, and the two figures were a full hour apart.
- **Chapter 48's Day 28 clock, as this summary now records it, and as the chapter gives it:** Hask's men from about the fourth hour of the morning; Hask at the gate about a quarter past four; **Renn's gate at about ten past five**; the claim off the frame about half past four; **Cray's withdrawal down the lane at about a quarter to six, her own record stamped the fifth hour**; **the ringing at about twenty minutes to the sixth hour**; Jerm at the north gate at the sixth hour; the Crown on the west road about twenty past six; the wagon at the mill narrows about the seventh hour and in Kells about a quarter past seven; house 114 at the eighth hour and twenty minutes; Rudd and the court at the ninth hour; the fifth schedule at the tenth hour. **This summary previously said twenty past five for the gate, a quarter past five for Cray, and twenty minutes to five for the ringing. The first two were state errors and the chapter was right; the third was not an error at all but a figure the chapter itself gave, and the audit settled the chapter (see finding 10a) and then settled this summary to match.**
- **Four month names remain on the page** — March at `ch12:73` and `ch15:87,89`, June at `ch15:89`, February at `ch17:49` — and are recorded rather than removed. **This is the one place Volume 01 does not fully satisfy the rule as written.**
- **One ambiguity recorded and not resolved:** Chapter 36's notice names Mercy Slee as the first person to put the column question, and Chapter 36 later puts Sena Dunn of 121 at the counter asking it on the same day. Both are the chapter's.

---

# Volume 02 — *The Unpaid Names* — chapter summaries

## Chapter 0051 — The Book and the Roll

Day 34, before the light into **the fourth hour of the afternoon**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, on the towpath north out of Kells from the low turn to the second mile. She walks in front of the barrow with her shoulder against the shaft and does not pull and does not offer. The frame stays in the cradle and she counts the four bolts twice and says nothing about the counting. Her own bag is on her hip and the bell is in it and she does not take it out. **She asks three carriers about paper and not about a child, and gets the batch's working distinction out of them.** A carrier's book: the day, the thing, from where, to where, how many miles, the carrier's name — and **three things a carrier may not write: the owner, a debt, and a price**, and *people are not parcels and always were not*, and a clerk with a list is not a carrier and is not bound by the rule that keeps a person out of a book. **A woman with a bolt of cloth describes a clerk who came down the road with a list and a copyist, read names at four doors, asked nobody anything, and the list filled in anyway — and came back nine days later with the same list, and at the third door a woman said *that is not us any more, we have been at the low end of the water since the flood*, and he said *thank you*, and the copyist scratched something out and wrote something.** An old man at a gate says it before she asks: *a road full of people who will tell you things is a road you can get anything out of by not asking.* **She says her reason out loud and it is not about Tomas:** a boy of eight called Jory Fenn, taken in the round that came before a Crown notice was put up in his ward, nobody has him, and the only paper about him is a line on a schedule and a piece of a household list with a fourth line cut away. She will not be the woman in a room with a slate again, because a courier who stops walking and starts asking stops being the road, and no list of the road exists anywhere. **The leash is used for the first time:** at the top of the rise he asks her to take him on the flat fork to the ferry; she says no, gives the ground in her own book and not to him — a Crown object on a carrier's road puts the object in a carrier's book — and **he does not ask again that day.** They come into Ocklebridge at the fourth hour of the afternoon, **with the frame in the cradle and the bolts made fast and nothing rung.** End: she tells him at the top of the rise that she is not going to tell Marta Vell about the schedule, and that she was going to be there when he did it badly rather than somewhere afterwards telling him how he did it, and **that telling her and being there are different and she has given him the second one nine times this month and not the first one once.**

## Chapter 0052 — What Marta Vell Is Not Told

Day 35, **about the seventh hour** to **the fourth hour of the afternoon**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, at Ocklebridge. **He leaves the barrow and the cradle in the grass at the top of the lane, out of the square, not carried in, and makes the four bolts fast at the place it was set down and writes the hour and the fact.** Marta Vell rings the water bell at about the seventh hour, four strokes, unhurried. He gets nine words into telling her and she says, before he has finished the first sentence, that she has a Crown bell in a field at the top of her lane, and that he has told her the interesting part in the first breath, and that she would like him to go up the lane now and will not be cross about it. **She is right, and it is not temper.** He goes to the first gate with his back to her and offers to say all of it and go away. He tells her: the morning of the thirty-third, four rings and a woman who named what she wanted repeated; the schedule in a building in Fourmile; the line that says the water bell of Ocklebridge is a bell of that ward; **nothing in the field where the keeper goes, because the form has no field for a keeper of a water bell**; and that nobody asked her and nobody has told her. She says: *How long have you known that.* He says since the twenty-eighth. She says: *And it is the thirty-fifth today. And you are telling me it at four in the morning at the top of my lane because it is on your way, and if you had not been on your way I would be standing here this morning not knowing that for another year, and that is not a reason to tell me, that is a reason to not care.* **He tells her he read his own fifth clause for two days as a reason not to tell her, and that it is a bar on entering an answer for her and not a bar on telling her a fact, and that he put them together because he did not want to be the man who had to come down the lane.** She tells him to go up the lane. He goes, and writes in his book: **a receipt is not a permission** — and under it, that a permission is not a reason, and that a man who leans on a rail for two days will lean on a person next. He comes back at the ninth hour. **She will not look at anything and will not read anything.** He asks her instead to write down, in her own hand, with the hour, what she does every morning and who has ever asked her to ring it. **She does it on a slate on her own step in about eleven minutes, turns it round and holds it against her own body so he cannot see it, and at one point stops and says she has to get a thing straight or the whole of it is wrong.** She will not give it back for a week and will read it herself in about nine days, and the week is hers to ask for and she has asked for it. She gives him one sentence off it: a form that has no field in it for the only person in the entry is a form that is not about the person. **The first name written on the page is not his and is not going to be, and he may ask in nine days and will not be answered before.** **At the fourth hour of the afternoon a letter comes up the road in a ward board's grey cover**: a bundle of Crown papers left at the Kells ward board by a district rider on the second, read by Mercy Slee on the third, forwarded up the road in a ward bag with the bellmaker's name still on the bag list. **The letter is unsigned** — a household of six, a form that asks for the house, a man who used to live there and does not, six certified returns with his writing and a district stamp, the district writing *forwarded* on the wrapper the second time, and a request not to have their name on it. On the outside, **in a hand that is not Nell Fenn's**, four lines from Mercy Slee: *do not answer them*; you do not have to do anything with this; I have not been thanked and I am not going to be.

## Chapter 0053 — Six Years of One Hand

Day 36, **about the eighth hour** to **about the seventh hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a hamlet two miles past Ocklebridge, in a lane. **He leaves the barrow at the lane mouth with the cradle down in the hedge and the bolts made fast and writes the hour and the fact.** The lane has nine houses and is called nothing on any paper he has; the letter had said *in this lane* four times without once naming it. **At the third door a woman lets him read four paragraphs standing in the lane and says it is the worst offer anybody has made at this door in two years.** **Maud Prine, who does the writing in a house of six, reads her own unsigned letter and says it is her hand and that she did not sign it and did not write the *forwarded*.** Five people are in the room and the door stays open. **He does what he did in Kells for a month — asks them apart, separately, so they cannot hear each other — and it does not work, and Maud Prine stops him and gives the reason: they have been asked separately for six years, by a form, and there is no room in that house where the answer of that house lives.** He shuts his book and asks for the form. **The form is a supplementary return and it is a good one:** four lines under *reason, in the household's own words*, a box for *the name of a person present at the answering*, and the small print *a statement returned before the closing hour of the period stated above will be certified*. **The heap on the table is six years of it.** He reads twenty minutes and is not left alone with it. **In the box is the same name in the same young man's hand on all six, and five different dates in that one hand, and the downstrokes of the d and the g have the same hesitation in all of them, and it is not a copy and not a traced thing.** The mechanism is then given whole and it is entirely lawful: the district takes the return in, stamps it, **sends it back to the house to keep**, and next year a man comes with the next form and the last certified answer and a line reading **confirmed by the household**. The brother-in-law has not been in the house for six years. **Then Maud Prine names the man who comes: a man who fills in forms for people who cannot write, four times a year, who is not Crown, who does not charge, who is not cruel, and whose name she will not give and why — *if you go up that lane with his name, the next person in this lane who cannot write will find that the man has stopped coming.*** **Matthew will not thank him, says so, and is right**: a man came to a lane of six houses with a book, asked one question, and the asking was for him. **Maud Prine gives him the six-year-old certified answer out of her own drawer and out of her own hand, with a condition: the paper still in the door must go back on the fourth day, and this one is a record and that one is an instrument.** He reads the giving back twice and she corrects him twice. **He does not ask the household to stand in anything, and he does not ask for the signer's name and is not entitled to it.** He writes the day's cost in his own book at the top of the lane: he asked them apart because it is the only instrument he knows, and **the last line on the current year's form is a different hand — a careful hand, and it is not one of the six — and he asks no questions about it and puts it in the book so he cannot leave it out.**

## Chapter 0054 — The Map With the Corners Cut

Day 38, **the fourth hour of the afternoon** is the only clock time in the chapter. **Tamsin Rook's POV**, a coaching inn four miles on, with a yard and a coach office in a lean-to. **The leash is used again and it is Tomas who asks and is refused:** he asks to come in, she says no before he has finished, and the reason she gives him is that if he is in the shop it is a man asking about a bell and if she is in the shop alone it is a woman asking about paper, and she is not going to stand in a street being a third thing a clerk has to write down. **Ruben Sallow sells road sheets at a farthing and says the panels are not printed, which is not true, and has a new plate, and the girl's in the shop says he will not show anybody the plate because he showed a Crown man once and the Crown man wrote a number down.** She buys four and then five more. **The fifth panel is gone**: the same rectangle, smooth and uncreased, the paper the same age and the same colour, the ink a slightly different black, and the two column rules the other four panels have are not. **It is not wear, because wear is random and does not care what a thing is for** — and this is the same professional reading a courier applies to a bell, and she traced a theft once by looking at the inside of a crown. **Somebody has taken out the part of the map that says where an answer goes.** She does not accuse him. She asks **who orders a road sheet printed and what are they paid by — by the sheet, or by the year** — and he works out in four seconds that she is not asking him a question at all: *they buy by the sheet, the district has bought that sheet from that printer every year since the year I came in here, and the number has not changed in six years and the number has not gone up with the price, and that is not my business*; and, later, *they're not the same number and nobody has ever asked me.* **Tomas reads a map on a barrow and sees a road map, and looks at the back at the roads, and looks at the tide table while she holds the fourth panel, and says that a district has thought of something naming the day a return goes out, and only when she puts her thumb on the fifth one does he say *there is a gap*.** She records in her own words that she saw it at the counter and he saw it afterwards, on a barrow, four minutes late, having looked at the tide table. **On the front, in a column of eleven paid tradesmen's advertisements, is AMOS TREWIN, SMITH, past the county line, and his four lines are *METAL SOLD WITH A RECORD / Bellmetal, fittings, wire, and honest work / Ask for the book*.** The day-book entry for the crossing of the county stone is four lines and the fourth is: the bar is on the bell and not on the licence, the bell is in a sealed yard four days' walk behind them, and the line stops at the stone and not one yard further.

## Chapter 0055 — A Bell Somebody Else Made

Day 39, **about the seventh hour** to **about the seventh hour of the evening**, all day, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the smithy past the county line. **The smith says the two things he does and the two he will not do before he is asked:** he is not a master of the Bellwrights' Measure and cannot certify anything at all, and **he will not cast anything — not a handbell, not a wheel bell, not a strap, not a staple for a gate — and has not cast a bell in nineteen years and there is no fire in this shop that could do it.** He also says he will sell metal and keep a book, and that the two are the same thing and people do not believe it, and that he has kept a book every day for nineteen years because a woman told him in a room what his metal did and did not raise her voice. **He recognises the notice on his own signboard** and knows what is in the frame, and quotes the craft law *before* Tomas knows he has heard Sabin Fell say it — *a bell that cannot be traced is not a bell of unknown origin* — and then says he heard it at a gate in the rain in this county about a month ago and has never been able to get it out or get it checked, and Tomas gives him Sabin Fell's name and Trewin says he has said it and has not got it from you. **The bar is a real instrument and the smith is unbothered by it:** the notice says the thing may be produced at the request of any person and there is not one line in it that stops him selling metal. **Tomas is allowed to read the book and reads it standing up all afternoon and writes nothing down.** Nine columns: day, thing, what it was made of, weight, price in, price out, buyer, where it was to, and **a ninth, the where-from — the place the metal came out of, the date, and the man he had it from.** **Sabin Fell's rule arrives from a stranger who has never heard of the Bellwrights' Measure: a bell is traceable when the metal of it can be shown to have come from a source that keeps a record of what it sold and to whom.** Trewin makes him say it again with a word changed, and then gives the practical form: *ask his supplier, and the supplier shows him the ninth column, and the supplier's supplier shows him his, and there is a chain of shops going back as far as anybody can remember, and the chain is made of men who wrote a day down.* **At the end Tomas asks to buy a page — the households column, nine years of who bought metal and where it went — and is refused, and the reason is given whole: the eighth and ninth columns together make a list of houses, the book is the only copy, and it cannot be made again out of a shop a hundred and forty years old.** He is told he has about one chance in four and that the reason is not the law and not Tomas and it is that Trewin has never read a piece of paper in his life that was not a bill. He says he will come back at about the sixth hour on the fortieth with a reason in writing. **The price is eleven and six the hundredweight for metal with a book against it, against four and six out of a hearth, and the difference is not the metal.** The smith's other professional opinion is that a Crown dealer is not expensive but *available*, and that nobody in a county with a bar on a bell is going to ask a Crown store what the metal is for. **Mara's trail appears once and does not confirm itself:** a loose leaf between two pages of the book, folded in four, not pasted, not sewn, two lines copied off a Crown form in a copying hand and five words under them — **I have not taken this**, which is five words and not four — and a copying-wax mark the size of a thumbnail. **He cannot tell whether it is his sister's hand or a copyist's, he puts it back where it was, writes nothing else, and does not tell Tamsin Rook, and the reason he gives himself in the book is that the last time he put a page of somebody's in her hands it was his own and he chose who got it, and she does not need his sister's wax.** The day's last four lines: *nothing, no metal, no casting, a supplier, a price, a county stone, a ninth column* — and that **Sabin Fell and Amos Trewin have said the same sentence about the same column and neither knows the other exists and he has no right in either trade.**

## Chapter 0056 — The Name on the Stock Book

Day 40, **about the sixth hour** to **the sixth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the smithy and then the road. He goes back at about the sixth hour with a four-line reason in writing, including *nothing here is Crown property and nothing here is mine either*, and Trewin reads it standing in his own doorway and says it is the first reason anybody has ever given him for wanting a page of a book, and **does not sell him the page.** He does the other thing unasked, and shows him the **eighth column, four years back, in the spring: the district sent a cart round the county, bought gate iron and bar and handcart loads, paid with a document out of a store in Fourmile with a date and a place of purchase on it, and the order was in the name of no man but a place** — *the district, for the ward of —*, four wards, four streets under each, one sheet, one clerk's careful hand, copied into Trewin's book and taken back, and Trewin is entitled to have let him. **Tomas gets it wrong and Trewin corrects him, at length and without heat: nobody has the book, nobody ever has, he is not frightened of it, and the district does not need it, because the district made its own** — a clerk came down the road four years ago with a list and a copyist and did not stop at one house, read names at four doors, asked nobody anything, and filled the list in anyway, and the list is Crown paper, and Tomas's face has the look of a man who has found a door and there is no door. **That is the same clerk the woman with a bolt of cloth described on the thirty-fourth, four days and a day's walk behind them, and neither of them has any idea the other exists.** At about the tenth hour the sentence arrives and he writes it out eleven times: **the names are not the point; a household which has ever bought a thing is a household an office can find, because a trade already keeps, for its own reasons and in its own hand, the exact three things an office needs — who lives where, who buys, and who signed; the Crown's roll is not built out of stolen paper, it is built out of the ordinary paper a trade keeps, and the whole of the apparatus works because two entirely honest trades keep the same three facts in the same two kinds of hand; a room with a plan in it has a door and a door can be watched and shut, and a column and a stamp have no door and only need somebody to be competent.** At the fourth hour of the afternoon he says out loud in a doorway that **this is a worse thing than a villain**, and Tamsin Rook, who has been on a gate thirty feet away reading his shoulder for nine minutes, tells him that a man being dramatic about a column in a lane is the first half of how a column works, and that if anybody had heard him they would have told the shopkeeper, who does not need a Crown office to be frightening. **He apologises to himself in the same breath and is right and she is right.** At about the sixth hour of the evening he writes a method on a page and gives it to her to read over his shoulder without a word, and she reads it in about nine minutes and says *yes*, and he asks her the one question: **if a trade's paper and an office's paper are the same paper, what is the one question a person can put to a clerk that the clerk has to answer in his own name?** Her answer: **you have been asking offices who is on a list; a name is a thing you can be refused, and being refused a name looks like the office protecting somebody; ask them what the document is — is the thing you keep a record of what I said, or is it a record of what somebody else said about what I said — and then make him put the answer in a document with his name at the bottom of it, because a thing a clerk holds is not a fact about anything, and a clerk who has written down what it is has said a thing, and a thing somebody has said can be looked at by the next man and by the person you hand it to.** And: *You have had it since the ninth day of the month, in a market court in Kells, from a girl of sixteen, and you have been trying to make people answer for themselves instead of making a document say what it is. You have been doing it the way a bellmaker does it. **There is no person. There is a column.*** Under it, the page he will not be able to read at: **nobody taught it; it only had to be convenient, and then it had to be printed, and then it had to be stamped** — and that a habit is worse than a man, because a man can be refused and a habit cannot, and a man can be watched and a habit is watched by nobody, because everybody is inside it. **The last line in the book for the fortieth is about his own receipt:** the third clause lets any person request the object, he asked for that clause at about the fifth hour of the morning of the thirty-third because he was right, it is a door with no lock on it that he hung himself, and **he is writing down the night before that it will be used, so that on the morning after he cannot stand up in a yard and say a man told them it would happen.**

## Chapter 0057 — Somebody Took the Cradle

The evening of the fortieth into the forty-first. Arrival at about **the eighth hour of the evening of the fortieth day**; Tomas goes in at **the ninth hour** and is asleep by ten; the request at **about the second hour**; **the fourth hour**; **the fifth hour**; and they reach Fourmile at **the second hour of the afternoon of the forty-first day**. **Tamsin Rook's POV.** The yard has four vehicles in it. She counts the four bolts twice and the ostler counts them too, without being asked, because she said *four* and pointed. She asks the ostler for the yard book and works out, walking the length of the yard, that nobody has ever been asked to use it as a witness to anything, and tells him she wants a line in it at the second hour with a requester's refusal in it, and he says he will leave the book out and be there and **will not hold a Crown bell for anybody and would rather say so now than at two in the morning.** **A man comes on foot and alone at about the second hour and requests the object in the frame be produced, and the request is one side, dated the fortieth, timed the second hour, in a hand Tamsin cannot place, asking that the object be produced for the purpose of looking at it — and it has no name on it.** She does not refuse it and says so in front of witnesses. **She asks for his name for the book and is refused; asks who is standing behind him and is told nobody; and writes the refusals in that order and says the order is the whole of the fairness in it and offers to write it in another order if he prefers.** She asks what he intends to do with it, and he says *I am going to ask it to tell me what it is.* **She reads the fourth and fifth clauses entire, out loud, in the yard, with about eleven people in it.** **The object is produced in its cradle on the barrow, the bolts made fast, not taken out of the cradle, not rung, and it is looked at for a quarter of an hour without being touched and without going round the other side.** The yard book has eleven lines and records the request, the two refusals, the production, the reading of the clauses, the quarter of an hour, the departure, and the eleven people. The man tells her, standing in the yard, that **the fifth clause is the one that stopped him and the fourth did him nothing, and that a clause saying the holder may not say what it means is worth nothing at two in the morning unless somebody gets to the point of asking him to say it — and then: you have made it cheap; you have made a thing that costs a man one sheet of paper and a walk, and in about a year there will be a man who does not want the object but wants a third of this receipt, and by then this will happen on an ordinary day and nobody will stand in a yard about it. She says yes. He says he is not going to say she was wrong, and wants it entered if there is anybody to enter it with, and that he thinks she was right and that it has cost her a night's sleep and will cost more.** **She did not wake him, and has never given a reason, and nobody has ever asked her for one.** He reads the page twice and says the fifth clause is what stopped it, and she gives him the finding: **a request is only checkable if you can tell in advance what the person is going to do, and you cannot tell that from a sheet of paper, and the only way anybody has found to check a request is to make the requester say out loud what he wants to do in a room with other people in it, and then write it down while he is saying it — and that is all a fifth clause is.** He says he had been carrying that clause for seven days as half a thing and calling it the whole of it. He does not say *I told you so*, and **she makes him enter that he did not tell her and that she did not ask, and says that if he had told her on the thirty-third it would have been a man telling a woman she was slow.** She says, to a wagon wheel: *I am very good at this. I have been good at this for eleven years and I have never once enjoyed it and I have never told anybody that. Being good at it is not the same as liking it, and that is why I have got a bag on my belt and not a slate.* The frame never leaves the cradle, the bolts are made fast at the place it was set down, **nothing is rung, nothing is broken, and the object is exactly as it was on the thirty-third: the same object, in the same frame, with no clapper in it and no clapper to come.**

## Chapter 0058 — An Office With an Address

Day 42, **about the ninth hour** to **about the sixth hour**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the Crown district renewal office at Fourmile: a street with three shops, a door painted four times, the district's number on a plate by a wire with a knocker on it. **She stays forty feet off on a wall because if he goes in alone he is a man asking about a bell, and she says it as her own decision out loud, before he can use any part of it.** **He does not get past the first desk.** The clerk will not take the sheet, puts a hand on it, asks who sent him, and then: *there is a file in this building with that name in it, and it is not mine, and I am not going to tell you what is in it, and I would like it understood that I did not tell you and that you did not ask.* He goes and stands somewhere else and comes back with **Marten Cope**. Cope will not have the frame carried up a staircase. The room has a chair on the other side of the table for a person who is being answered, and Tomas notices the chair. **Cope sets the order of the day out loud before he does any of it**: three answers in writing in his own name within the hour, then the refusal in writing in his own name with a reason, **handed over with the answers because a refusal handed over with the answers is a fact about this office and a refusal handed over on its own is an insult**, and then: if you argue with me about any of the four, I will stop, and not as a tactic. Tomas does not argue with any of the four. **The three answers, in his own hand, the first with the date and the hour on it and the second in the same hand an hour later, and only the second of the three printing an hour on its face — the tenth hour, with the first and the third carrying their hours only on Tomas Vale's four-column page, at the ninth hour and the twelfth hour:** *the Ocklebridge water bell* is entered on the district's sheet of water bells for the north river with **the keeper's field empty, and the reason the field is empty is that the form has four fields and none of them fits a water bell, so the field is not left empty by a person, it is left empty by a form** — and he has entered a marginal note asking for an instrument, in his own name. *A document in which one hand signs as the answer of more than one household* — **four such returns in one file in this building over eleven years from four districts, in four different receiving clerks' hands, all four bearing the same hand at the signature line, and he describes the hand: it leans forward, the descender of the g hesitates and comes back, the downstroke of a d and of a g stop and start in the same place, and it is the same in four counties and four receiving clerks' hands, and that is the part that convinced him, because a clerk's hand is never the same twice and a signer's hand is the same every time** — and he refuses to describe the households, because a document in a file is not a person and the road to a person becoming a document is a question put in a corridor. *Does this office transmit any schedule, roll or list of names* — **it transmits nothing, the copy stays in the building, and he has put the question to the district twice in writing, at the district's own request once, and had no answer to either, and records the dates of his own two letters, and will not speculate in a document because a document is a thing people believe.** **The refusal:** that this office's instruments require a return, that **a roll is not a return**, that to answer the fourth properly he would have to write down in a document that goes to a district office that the largest instrument the office holds on one route is not one of the things its own forms recognise; that **the office has no instrument for a roll, none, and he asked for one in the ninth year and was refused and did not ask again, and that that is the whole of his own part in it**; and that **he is not yet willing to say it in a document that leaves the building, and he is not going to give a braver reason than that.** Cope then says, unasked, that the letter of the thirty-second was a bad letter and he would write it again, that the custody review was his, that **the withdrawal of the finding of the twenty-eighth was not his and was a surveyor's and he will not stand in another man's clean water**, and that **the withdrawal is not a win: it is a document that says his district was wrong about a set of bells, there are about nine hundred bells in this district, and nobody has asked for it.** And: *I want the next question I am asked to be the right one. A question a person can check in an hour is worth nine questions a person cannot check at all.* **Tomas asks for one thing more:** that the refusal be **filed where a person can ask for it** — and Cope files it in the office's **book of refusals**, about two hundred and forty entries in eleven years, in which nobody has ever before asked, and records that he is the officer who stopped leaving the requester's name out of that book, four years ago, for a reason he cannot account for and has never asked about. **Tomas writes the four out in four columns with the hour against each and the answer or refusal in its own column, and does not write *the district answered three of four questions*, because a sentence like that is a response, and a response is what this has been about for six days, and nobody merged them, including him.** And on the last line, nine words: **being right is not the beginning of an exchange.** In the passage he tells her he is not going to be able to answer the fourth and is not going to be the man who makes a district officer say it. **She says both of them have to ask households, and that the rule on the slate is about to come out of her mouth at a door in a way he will not like, and that he will not carry her question for her and she will not put his name at a door, and that in about nine days one of them is going to be refused and the answer will be no and the person who asked may not ask again that day — and that this is Mercy Slee's doing and she has told Mercy so and Mercy said she knew and would have it in the ward's book.**

## Chapter 0059 — Nine Households, One Signature

Day 43, **the fourth hour** to **the seventh hour of the evening**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, nine doors between a sluice and a bridge along about a mile and a half of towpath and two lanes off it, done in the order the water goes. **Her bell is in her bag the whole time and is the heaviest thing she carried.** **The three questions, the same words nine times, not improved:** *has any person ever put their name to a paper for this house — a Crown form, or a return, or a certificate — without anybody asking them to*; *is the person who put their name there living in this house now*; *do you want that written down, and may I write your no as a no and not as nothing*. **The third one is Nell Fenn's, and it is not her words and not her idea, and Tamsin worked out at a low drain gate that the whole of what she had seen in that market court was a refusal being kept in a column of its own, and it is the only thing she knows that works.** **The leash is used and it is the third time:** at the seventh door Tomas asks her to take the ninth — *it is one hundred and forty yards and the same three questions and my name on them and not yours* — and **she says no once**, and the reason is that *a man who cannot get to a door alone will find a woman, and a woman with a road is always available, and that is how one person's answer ends up in a document with two names on it and one keeper in the box*, and that she has been the second name on a piece of paper four times in eleven years and one of them was about a child and she has never got it back. **The two noes in this scene are Hesk Rann's own answers, at the seventh door, to the first and the second of her three questions; Tomas asks once, does not ask again, and says so aloud — *I am not asking twice*** — **and she makes him write it down.** **He asks once and does not ask again, and she makes him write it down, and in his own book he writes the real reason: he did not want to go to a door by himself, and that is his whole flaw and it is the reason the market lane happened and the reason the archive burned.** She finishes the one she is at and he goes and stands forty yards off, and at about the sixth hour he leaves the barrow in a field with the cradle down and the bolts made fast. **Eight of the nine refuse, and one answers** — a man at a green door who has had every form for eleven years and has never once been asked anything and wants it written down with his name on it and does not care what happens to him after today; she reads it back and he makes her change one word. **She puts the slate in the water at the sluice at about the fifth hour** so that nothing she has written can be looked at over her shoulder, and writes: *nobody's name is in my bag, nobody's number is in my bag.* **The seventh door is a form-writer, and he names himself before she asks the second question: Hesk Rann.** He says no to the first question and then, in a different voice, tells her unasked, and asks for it to be noticed that he is telling her and not writing it: a man two counties over signed a form for his family six years back because his sister-in-law asked him and he was the only one of the six who could write, and **Rann has taken it to him twice a year ever since, and the man does not know he is signing, and asked once in the second year whether the paper wanted him to say something, and was told no — it wants the answer you gave last year, and there is a line to confirm it on** — and cannot remember agreeing to any of it and last called the paper *a thing about a gate*. **Rann then says the rest and it is the batch's engine: there is a woman four doors up who cannot write and two more between there and the bridge and about forty in a year; he has been asked by nobody, not once in nine years; the form has one line for a name and he is the one in the room; he has signed about nine hundred; every one is a true answer from a true household; and every one is in a form that says the answer of this house; and he has never lied to anybody about anything.** He says he is telling her so that there is one true sentence about him in a book before the rest of it starts, and that in about four days a man with a Crown bell is coming up the road. **He will not sign her refusal and asks her to write down that he asked her to write down that he would rather be one of her eight and that she did not.** She gets the hand the correct way — by asking a question whose answer is a hand — and he admits he wrote Amos Trewin's wife a bill in the spring and was paid nine and six, and writes his refusal on the back of a form out of his own satchel, in the same hand he uses for a household and for a tradesman's bill: forward slope, hesitating descender, same pause before g. **The same hand is in three documents in three counties and in two kinds of paper, and the two kinds are a Crown form and a tradesman's bill, and no law anywhere says a man may not be the hand on both.** At about the seventh hour of the evening she tells him nine, eight refusals in eight hands, one answer in a ninth, and one hand on a Crown form in a lane two days back, on a smith's bill past the county line, and on nine hundred doors along this water. He says: *then it is not a conspiracy, it is a man.* She says: *it is a man and about nine hundred doors, and the man is the door, and the doors are a habit, and the habit is the thing.* **And she gives him the thing he will not like, and has had since the seventh door: the man has been to that family in the lane two days back, twice a year, for six years, and the household told him the man has not been in that house for six years, and it is all true at once, and she has not got a way to hold a thing being all true at once, and she has had three hours on a parapet.** She says *downstream* and stops and waits to see whether he is going to say it or she is going to have to. He is quiet for the length of a cart going past.

## Chapter 0060 — The Road To Miremarket

Day 44, **about the ninth hour** to **about the seventh hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the towpath above a ferry, the decision made in the open at a landing stage at about eleven miles. **He does not decide for nine hours, and in the nine hours he writes four columns, and there is not one word in the four columns that is his own opinion, and it is the first page of his in a month made that way.** He writes the question out first, and asks **two people the same three questions in the same words, and adds nothing to it for either, and writes down what each said and it takes eleven minutes.** **Abel Rundle, the ferry-man, answers yes inside about four seconds**: the coach up the bank runs twice a week, touches at Miremarket if the water is kind, takes a barrow, and the fare is the fare and he does not carry anybody who says they will pay at the far end; and he will carry a paper, on a day, for nothing, and will not read it, and there are four on this reach and two who will take a paper anywhere on this river for a shilling. **Hanna Grieve, who keeps the landing tally and has kept it for about thirty years, answers the first half and then stops dead on the second and says five words: a Crown bell in a Crown coach** — the coach is on a Crown contract, a Crown warrant and a list, the driver keeps a list of what he takes on because he is not allowed to take on what he cannot write down, and if a Crown office ever wanted to know where that object had been on a particular day there would be a page in a building in Fourmile with this stage on it, **a stage keeps a tide and a coach keeps nothing at all** — and tells him he may ask once more today and then may not. **He writes both whole at the head of the stage and neither improved**, and under column three, the road, not decided, he writes the decision, which is that **the coach is not refused by anybody, it is unavailable, because one person at a landing stage who has kept a tally for thirty years says a Crown object does not go in a Crown coach, and I believed her, and the reason I believed her is that she is describing a page appearing on a table in a building in Fourmile, and I have been in that building for an afternoon and I have read what a district officer will and will not put in a document** — and that **the four counties do not share a bellwright, do not share a market day, do not share an office, there are four county stones on this road since Kells, and the only thing they have between them is a person who once signed something on behalf of a house and has been signing ever since, and he is not in a room and is not in a document and is asleep above a sluice with a satchel on a hook.** In the same column he puts **two things that are not about a boat**: that he stood in a district office on the forty-second for the best afternoon he has had since he left Kells and **deliberately did not ask the district one word about the fifteen lines, and would not, because a person putting that question is a household putting it about its own line, and no household has come to the board, and it is not his, and the day he stops noticing that is a day somebody notices the line**; and that **the Day 24 request is not re-opened, he has not pressed her once in eleven days, and he is not going to open it now because he has a boat and a date and a signature and a man at a sluice and the one thing he could want today is for somebody to come with him, and he is not going to be the man who asks her for a reason to get on a boat.** Column four is hers, in her own hand, given face down, **five words** — *As far as it goes* is five words — and the whole of the slate. **He did not ask her whether she would come, and column four says he did not ask her, and the reason is that he was afraid of the answer, and if she had said no at a stage in front of two people he would have had to be a man about it in public for the first time in this volume, and there is no crowd here and no board and no Crown officer and the only people watching are a ferry-man and a woman who cannot hear him, and that is exactly why it would have cost nothing,
