and that is exactly why I did not do it.** They load at the fourth hour of the afternoon, frame in the cradle on the boards, **bolts made fast, cradle kept under it, barrow on the barge with the wheels chocked, and nothing is rung.** The date is not his: **the tide turns at about half past four, the boat goes on the tide, and the chapter gives the date as the forty-fifth at the top of the light, and there is no argument available with a tide.** At about the seventh hour of the evening, on his way up the path, **the ferry-man says, in passing, to nobody in particular: there are rolls at Miremarket that answer for people who never went** — and four paces further up the path, to the path, without turning round: **that's the Answer Rolls, that's all they're called down there.** He does not say who asked him, or when, or what a roll was, or what the *never went* meant, or whether he believed it, and nobody on that stage could have told afterwards whether the two words were a name for a thing or a thing a man had heard of somewhere and repeated because it sounded like a name. **He is over the lip of the bank and gone before either of them can ask a second question.** The tide was what it was.


## Chapter 0061 — The Boat

Day 45 into Day 46, **the second hour** to **about the eleventh hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the water. **He has never been a passenger since he was nineteen and does not know how to be one, and a barrow is a thing you own and a boat is a thing you are given a place in.** The object is in its padded cradle on the boards with the bolts made fast, and **the cradle is the thing the receipt says may not leave the carriage, and a boat's deck is not a carriage, and he has to work out in front of a ferry-man what the carriage is.** He works it out wrongly the first time and is corrected by **Abel Rundle**, the man he paid, and **the correction is in the record** and goes into the book afterwards. He does not tell the ferry-man what is in the frame and does not lie about it: **it is Crown property and I am carrying it under a receipt and the receipt says who may ask for it and you are a person and not only an officer, and you may ask for it now or in four days and I will produce it either way, and I would rather you asked now.** Rundle does not ask. He books the frame's place anyway and says one thing about the receipt that Tomas writes down. **The emotional change is that a man who has spent a month insisting other people handle the paper is now the paper, and he learns what his own fifth clause feels like from the outside and does not improve it.** He gains a passage and a debt to a man who did not ask. **The frame never leaves the cradle, the bolts are made fast at every place it is set down, and nothing is rung.**

## Chapter 0062 — The Fifth Panel On Water

Day 46 into Day 47, morning into the evening, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, the river and a landing. She wants to know whether a river road has a map and whether its fifth panel is gone. **A river does not print maps, every map she can buy is printed inland, and the ones inland have a fifth panel cut out of all of them, and the panel she wants is the one that names an office.** The resistance is a person: **the only person aboard who has bought a road sheet in eleven years is a man who bought his in a town two hundred miles up the river and has carried it as a decoration**, and he will not sell it and will not be asked to, and a second copy costs a day's walk or a shilling she does not have. **She does not steal the sheet and does not ask twice. She writes down what she can see of it from the other side of a table without asking to see it, and puts the description on a slate she fished out of a sluice two days ago and dried, and gives the slate away to a person on the boat, does not ask for it back, and says why.** The finding: **the cut is in the plate, so it is on every sheet printed from that plate in every year since, and a thing removed from a plate does not have a date; a thing removed from a copy has a date, and this has no date, which is worse.** And on water the routes are not roads, they are **tides and landings and stopping places, and a river's return does not go to an office by road at all.** She gains a description in her own hand, a copy of a sheet from a different year, **and a person on that boat who has now been told what she is reading and has not been asked to help.** **This is not the Bellwrights' Measure's road map, and its fifty-three withdrawals are not on any map and must never be conflated with a count on one. Her bell is not rung and nobody asks her for a reason.** The landing puts them in a town with a district office in it, and she says out loud that it is not going to be her who goes in.

## Chapter 0063 — The Name On The Return

Day 48, **the fourth hour** to **about the sixth hour**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a district office in a town — **and the seventh hour of the afternoon is Day 48's last hour and is not printed in this chapter; it is given retrospectively in Chapter 69 as the hour he recorded the hour at Helling.** **A second district, the same four questions, the same form.** The office is a different office and the questions are the wrong questions, because **he has learned to ask a building what a document is and has not learned that two buildings can hold the same document.** The form has a field for a person and a field for an hour and **no field for a reason, and he did not write a reason, and a district is entitled to a reason** — the deputy's argument is that a box for a reason means the office decides about the reason, and a person who writes a reason in a box has handed the office a reason to be frightened, and a frightened office does one of two things and neither of them is answer the question. **On a return in this second district, at the signature line, is the same hand** — the same forward slope, the same hesitating descender, the same d and g stopping in the same place — **and this district has never been asked about it and has eleven years of it too.** And on a schedule at the back of the file, **an auditor's return, quoted approvingly, and the approval is signed.** He does not accuse and does not build a case. He asks for the approving return **copied for him, in the office's own hand, with the hour on it**, is refused a copy, and **is given the return itself to read where it sits, which is a different thing and a better one** — the deputy's reason being that the office has no instrument for copying a return of a practice and that copying it on a sheet of his own would be making a return in this office of a document that is not one. **The name of the auditor who signed it is on that return, and Tomas writes it down: Pell Rill. It is the first time that name is on any page in this novel, and the man is not in the chapter, is not described, and is not spoken of by anybody.** **And then, on the way out, he asks the one question the chapter was built to ask: what is the schedule for this district — is it a record of what the households said, or a record of what somebody in this building said about what the households said.** Deputy **Ilma Carrow (I. C., no age) looks at him for a moment, gets a sheet, writes for about eleven minutes, gives it to him, and signs it *this office, this day, about the sixth hour*.** It says **it is a record of what this office holds to be the answer of a household and not a record of what any household said, because no household in this district has ever said anything to this office in a room with a person in it; where a household has sent a return it is a good record; where it has not and the year closes the line is carried — and she does not know what it is carried from, has asked twice, has never found the standing entry, has been four years in the post and has never asked the district.** He reads it at the counter and again on the steps with the frame in the cradle behind him in the yard and the bolts made fast. **It is the only one of his sheets not written by a man who was being careful, and the chapter's own numeral for it is the fifth sheet he has. It is a document and it is on him, and it is the tenth of the ten non-bell documents, and it is recorded in `state/batch-0007-summary.md` item 3.** He gains a name, an approving return, a second district's file and a deputy's written answer, and **he does not gain a household, a name in a household, or anything he may act on.** Then two rooms over a shop off the market square, the barrow in the yard behind it, the bolts made fast at the place it was set down, the frame in its cradle all night, nothing rung, the receipt whole — and about a hundred yards from the office a lane he did not go up. **OFF. 411 is not on this page. The Crown clapper is not on this page. The Silent Muster is not spoken.**

## Chapter 0064 — The House They Left

Day 49, **the fourth hour** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, the town and a lane off it. A household of four living in two rooms over a yard with a house standing empty two streets away and their name still on the door. **Nobody wants to know and the ones who do know are the ones who cannot be told.** The household is not being evicted, is not in arrears and has broken nothing; **the reason is a column on a return, and the person whose name is in the column is one of the four, does not live there, is alive, and is the same person who signed.** The town has already found a good answer — *they are not our sort any more* — **which is a sentence and a sentence gets merged.** The finding: **what a person is registered as decides what may be done to the people who live with them, and the doing is lawful, and the doing is not done to the person who is registered.** A household that bought a bell once, or signed a return once, has a relative on a return, is a household an office can find; **the office's finding has a consequence at a door, and the consequence is a lease, a rent, and a removal to two rooms over a yard, ordered in a form and carried out by a landlord who is not a villain and who is owed money.** She persuades nobody, uses no one's name, **gives up the fact of being a courier on the first door by putting the bag down where it can be seen**, and asks the same three questions she asked at nine doors two days earlier, in the same words — **and asks them of the landlord, which nobody in the volume has thought of doing.** He has moved them himself, he was asked by the district on a form any landlord may send, he got a good answer in nine days on the district's own paper with a date on it, and **the answer is that the address is in the district, the office does not hold a record of the persons at the address, and the office is not able to say who is at the address.** He keeps the letter in a drawer, will not let her have it, will not read it to her, and will tell her what it says. She is refused, **and the refusal is reasonable, because he wants to be left alone and he is owed four months' rent by people who are not the ones who are leaving.** She gains a fact that is checkable and a landlord's name and no cooperation. **Her bell is not rung. Her hearing is not discussed. No age for anybody in the household or the landlord.** She tells Tomas, at the top of a lane, not to ask the landlord whether a paper was sent, and to ask instead **what a district office is, when a landlord writes in and asks who is at an address, and what it is that the office then knows.**

## Chapter 0065 — The Office Used Properly

Day 50, **the fifth hour in the morning** to **the fifth hour of the afternoon**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the Fourmile renewal office, and the second visit. **He arrives with a demand and the office is entitled to notice, and the notice is a form, and the form is a Crown form, and asking an office to answer a question properly means putting a Crown form into a Crown office, which is the machine working.** **District Officer Marten Cope refuses the demand in about eleven seconds and is right:** a demand is not an instrument in this kingdom and if there were one he would want to know about it, because he would want to refuse it. Then Form nine of the second series, with a box for the person, a box for the hour, and a box for what the office has done, **and no box for a reason**, and the argument about why there should not be one, given at the counter and written down in Tomas's own book and not in Cope's. **Cope fills the form in himself, gives the carbon without being asked — the only copy outside the building — and then takes a chair out from under the desk and sets it on the wrong side of the counter**, because in eleven years nobody has ever sat in it and asked him for a *shelf*. **The book of refusals: a quarto, about two hundred and forty entries, eleven years, numbered, dated, each with a ground, and it has never been open to anybody.** He reads it aloud for two hours with Tomas beside him. At the **one hundred and ninetieth**, in a clerk's hand, dated **a year back from the twenty-sixth**: *Requested: a record of the making of a bell, to show the metal of it. Ground entered: declined; another district's business. The person asking is entered and is of this district.* **Copie has never seen the person who asked; the name is entered and is of this district and is not in the book; and a ground that is a district is a place and not a person, and the office cannot tell you who asked or what they wanted, and the office is not lying in the book.** At the **two hundred and seventeenth**, in Cope's own hand, dated the forty-sixth: the Ocklebridge water bell, *ground entered: no instrument is provided for a water bell*, **and that is the district telling this office what a form is, so a bell rung every morning for thirty years by a woman nobody has told anything about has been answered, in writing, by a desk, in the negative, with a date on it.** He says he is not sorry and will not take it out, because **a negative is not a thing somebody did, and that is the whole of it.** Then the file of four returns: **four districts that do not share a bellwright, a market day, a parish, an office or a post, and the four returns share one hand — which is the only thing they share and the only thing he can put in a sentence, and he has counted it this morning in a room with somebody in it for the first time in nine years.** Then Tomas's fifth question, not one of the four, the question Tamsin Rook gave him on a road: **what is the book of refusals?** Cope calls it unfair and correct and answers in his own name, with the hour, and gives the whole of it except one piece he has not got: **it is a record of what this office has refused to supply, it is not a record of what the district has declined to supply, about eleven of the two hundred and forty are in a clerk's hand and are not refusals of this office at all, and in about four years there will not be one of them left and then it will be a clean book, and it will be a clean book that is not true.** He walks him to the door, which he did not do last time, and says: **last time you asked a man for a door; today you asked a building to be a building, and it was, and I have been a building for a day, and I was a man last time and it was restful.** And he says the withdrawal of the district's finding of the twenty-eighth **is not a win, is not his, and nobody has asked for it and about four hundred people could.** On the steps Tomas writes eleven minutes in the flat hand: **a door is a thing you walk through and a shelf is a thing you can be shown; he has spent a month asking for doors; he got not one thing he asked for; and the one hundred and ninetieth entry is a smith's question, asked in a form, in this county, in his own lifetime, and it would be answered by a stock book four miles up the road.** **And he has not asked Cope about the L-4 lines and is not going to; that is a household's question and he has no standing in it.**

## Chapter 0066 — Asking For A Bell In The Open

Day 51, **about the seventh hour of the morning** to **the fourth hour of the afternoon**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, the county stone, Amos Trewin's smithy, and a room over a saddler's shop in **Bramden**, a market town of about eleven hundred with a stone landing and a three-day market running the fifty-first to the fifty-third. She stands on the stone doing arithmetic: **the thirty-fifth plus nine days is the forty-fourth, the forty-fourth is a day she watched him spend, and she has said nothing for seven days because the term was hers, a term that runs out has run out, and she is not going to be a woman pointing at a man's arithmetic in front of a smith's yard.** In the smithy she asks the question wrong — *is there any law against a man buying a bell* — and **Trewin corrects her in front of six people: there is no law against a man buying a bell; there is a bar on a bell, in the county she has just walked out of, and it is on the bell and not on the man; and the half nobody in her county is telling her is that a bar follows a bell out of a county in about four months with no court in it, and the paper will say the opposite and the shop will say otherwise.** He refuses to take her up to the master, and the reason is not the Crown: **he has eleven customers who cannot write and a stranger's question in the shop means every one of them is standing where a Crown matter is discussed, and I am refusing you because you are a question and the two do not go in the same room.** In the room over the saddler's, with about eleven people, she **reads the custody receipt in all five clauses out loud, and then reads the Measure's finding of the twenty-sixth in four lines, and says that the man with the barrow is not in the room and she asked his question for him and is not pretending otherwise.** **Rowan Ketch**, master, no age, on circuit for the market, says the four clauses are the best piece of paper he has ever seen drawn against his own trade and would like to say so. Then she asks her own question, in her own name, and he answers it out loud to the room and to the man with the lasts whose face has gone: **a man may buy a bell he has not make; there is no standing, no parish, no district, no years, no entry; a bell may be certified by a master where the making is complete, and the making is five things — the mould, the pattern, the mark, the date and the hand — and a master may certify a bell he did not make, and the great majority of the certificates he has signed in thirty years are on bells he did not make; a bell without one of the five may never afterwards be made certifiable; and the metal is traced by a chain of where-from in books, and if the chain is broken the bell is as good as a story.** And then the half that makes it no use to a man: **traceable is not the same as good; a bell with five on it can be stood in front of later; it is only worth the making to you and never once to him.** He writes it out, signs, times, and there is no second copy. **She writes the question out beneath it, with her own name, her own hour, the room and the number of persons, and signs it and gives it to him — and he tells her, before she has it back, what that costs: a question with a name and an hour and a room in it is findable, and it is a complete record, and that is the only danger in it and it is not a cage, because a cage is a thing somebody else wrote.** She says she signed it **so that it would not be his** — because in a month every room has looked at the door when a master said yes, and everybody knows what happened to a bell in a yard once. **And then the chapter says out loud that she has just built herself a fifth clause, in about four seconds, about somebody else, and that a cage is a thing somebody else wrote and this is a thing she wrote, and she may unwrite it by not signing the next one.** She learns that the Measure's bar **binds this county and not the next, which is the only reason a bell can be bought at all.** She gains a room, a smith, a master, a price and a stock. **The bell is not cast in this chapter and no certificate issues in it. Trewin is not a master and cannot certify anything. Tomas's plain carrier is barred and in a sealed yard in Kells and is not on this road.**

## Chapter 0067 — A Bell Somebody Else Made, And A Bell He Rang

Day 52, **about the fifth hour of the morning** to **the fourth hour of the afternoon**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the smithy and the room over the saddler's shop, full day. **He writes the letter to Marta Vell first, before he can talk himself out of it, and tears the leaf out of the book.** It is the whole of the debt in his own hand: **you gave me nine days, the ninth was the forty-fourth, I did not come, the term was yours and a term that runs out has run out, I am not asking you for the name, I am asking you one question and it is a short one — does the nine days still stand — and if the answer is no then it is no and I will not come again and I will not write again and you will not have to say no twice**; and that he did not write on the forty-fifth because on the forty-fifth he found out he was going to want something, **and a man who wants something should not be the one to reopen it.** At the seventh hour Trewin opens the ninth column and lets him read it with his hands behind his back. **The bell is a plain carrier of fourteen pounds two ounces from Roald Byre's hearth at the eleventh mile, cast in the spring, standing in a crate since: no wax in the crown, no dressed groove, nothing but the mark and a day.** Trewin's trade speech: **nothing is ever wrong with a bell; a bell is a lump of bronze with a note in it; every bell anybody ever threw away was a lump of bronze with a note in it that somebody did not like.** The mark gets you a man; **a date gets you the year, and the year gets you the caster's book, and the caster's book has the where-from in it, and the caster's supplier's book has it underneath that** — and the chain stops at a Crown store with a Crown document and a day, because a Crown store keeps no book of where it got it. **The price is one pound fourteen shillings, of which the metal is three shillings and fourpence at Trewin's own price out, and the rest is the making, and he will not be talked under it.** He offers the barrow. **He cannot sell it: the cradle is bolted to the barrow's bed, the receipt says the frame does not leave the cradle for the carriage, and if the barrow goes then at some point in the next four days a Crown object has nowhere to be set down. You have written a paper that will not let you pay for a bell.** He then offers a promise: a page a year, in his own hand, with the hour, saying where the bell is and what it has been rung for and by whom. **Trewin refuses, and the reason is the volume's argument made by a man who has read none of it: a man who writes to his smith every year about a bell is a man whose bell has a keeper, a keeper is a mouth, and a mouth is for a claim — and the whole of what is good about that bell is that there is nobody in it, no wax, no groove, no claim, no keeper, no name but the punch and the day. A page a year would make a plain carrier into a kept bell, and everything in that book would be true, and that is the worst of it.** He says he would rather not have either — a man is not a good keeper of a page of paper. And then the money comes from the wrong side of the room: **Tamsin Rook counts one pound fourteen shillings on the anvil bench out of a purse that has been flat for about eleven years — nine coppers a month off the towpath for about four years, a carrier's day-book for eleven years that has her name in it as a place a thing was left, and about eleven parcel fees — and she has never bought anything in eleven years, and a person can only find out what she is saving for when somebody finally needs something.** He says no; she says **you do not get to say that**; he gives one sentence for refusing her in front of six people; she puts it down anyway. Trewin's whole response is to ask her where she got it. **Nobody in the shop looks at the bell.** Then the loose leaf, **opened once, deliberately, in front of Trewin, on the bench**: two lines copied off a Crown form, five words, *I have not taken this*, and the wax. **Trewin's only finding is that it is a thumb and not a flat copyist's press, a small thumb, a person who was not being paid to be copying, and he will not say which and neither will Tomas; his other finding is that he has never seen a page in that state of dust in a customer's hands, and that the leaf was folded in four by somebody who thought it was worth four folds, and that it has been in that shop since before his name was over the door.** He tells him to put it back the same way, **because the crease is the thing.** **It does not confirm itself. It goes back. Tomas tells nobody, including Tamsin Rook.** Then Bramden: the letter is posted at the tenth hour, a Crown receipt for eleven pence, and a rider tells him the boat goes down at the eleventh and does not wait and **nobody on it is reading your post.** In the circuit room, **Rowan Ketch says in front of six people that yesterday was the law and today he has to find out whether he can use it, and asks: say where the last one came from.** And Tomas answers it, in public, in full: he is a bellmaker, he made it, he bought the metal in the ordinary way and did not ask, **nobody in that transaction lied to him**, it has no certificate and cannot have one, the Measure found on the twenty-sixth, he may not ring it in a public place in this county, **the bar is on the bell and not on his licence**, and it is in a sealed yard in Kells and he has not stood inside it since the twenty-eighth. **The man with the lasts is sorry for him and Tomas refuses the kindness and asks to be asked a question instead, and is asked one — why is your bar not past the stone with you — and answers that a bar is on a bell and not on a man, and that a bar follows a bell in the trade in about four months, and that he said that out loud in a room so that it is on a thing somebody heard him say.** Ketch's answer to all of it: **the right answer is worth nothing, that is what nobody tells you and every man finds out alone, there is nothing on this side of it and nothing on the other, and I asked you in front of six people so that a room can be held to it.** Then the certificate, four lines, his own hand, the hour, the five things with **the join at the mark**, the tracing good as far as it goes and no further, **six witness names written by six people of the town, one of them by his own hand**, and **no second copy, because she asked for the one and there would have been two and a district office if he had done it as usual.** Ketch warns that a certificate is worth nothing to the holder and is only the thing a room remembers for about a year, and asks **what he is going to do with it**; Tomas has not worked it out; Ketch says **do not ring it**; Tomas says he is going to ring it; Ketch says that is his business and not the company's. Tomas tells him the sentence about traceable bells **is a quotation and you did not know I had been in the room — she was.** He loses the thing he wanted: the anger. At about the fourth hour of the afternoon, in the room over the draper's, **he rings the bell once, on a leather, on a sill, with the door shut, and half the sound goes out of the window and cannot be got back.** She tells him she can feel it in the sill, the floor and the wall and cannot hear it, and that this is not nothing, and that she is not going to let him make it a thing about the fourth of last month. The day-book entry is in the flat hand, in four columns, and the price, and the day-book has the river-post receipt in the inside of his coat.

## Chapter 0068 — Whose Mouth

Day 53, **the fourth hour** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, a market and a landing. She has been asked three times before to stand next to something and said no three times. **At about the eighth hour of the morning, in the room over the draper's, he asks her to stand where a market of about nine hundred can see her beside the bell. She says no, once, in one word, and gives him a place instead of a want, and says there is a difference and he is the only man in this kingdom who could not tell it, and that if he asks a second time she will say the same word and then be gone for an hour.** **That is the fifth use of the leash, counting Mercy Slee's writing of the slate on the thirty-first, and it is worked, and he does not ask again that day.** Her reason, in full: **if she stands next to a bell in that square, the square decides what the two of them are in about four seconds and it will be right, and afterwards people will say the bell was rung by two people and one of them could not hear it, and that is a sentence about her, said at the low end of the square within a day and in Fourmile inside a month — and she would have said yes if he had asked her for that instead, and she wants both halves in the book or neither.** She takes a slate — **the back of a fishmonger's slate, the price side left showing** — and draws **four columns**, and **uses two of them and leaves two empty all afternoon, and tells the fishmonger why: a man from Kells keeps four columns and the third is for a thing somebody refused and the fourth for a thing nobody could answer, and there was no such thing in that square and she was not going to invent one, and if she filled it she would be as bad as the man she was writing down.** He had written four sentences and asked whether the third was too much; she told him the third was too much; and she told him why he must say nothing: **the minute you open your mouth you are the man who says what it is for, and the square will agree with you because you are the one with the bell, and it is easier — and then the square decides, and you will find out what the square thinks a bell with no claim in it is for, and that is worth more to you than anything you have said to me in a month, and you will not be able to complain about it afterwards, and that is the price.** At the top of the hour he **rings it once**, and it is not words: a single stroke about two seconds, and nine hundred people hear the same thing, and about a second later there are about nine hundred different things in nine hundred people. **That is the finding, and it is a bad finding and it is the true one, and she writes it down before anything else.** Column one is his words, given at the kerb and read back to him once: *this bell has no claim in it and no keeper in it and no name in it but the caster's mark and the day of the casting; it says only what I say while it is moving; I am not a keeper of anything; I am not speaking for any household in this town or in any other; I have no household to speak for; I rang it because I have bought it and I have not yet found out what it is for.* Column two is four named bystanders, ruled apart, a name and an hour at head and foot: **Mab Quiller, a chandler, that he has said it is a warning and not of what, and that a warning is the oldest thing there is and a market day is the last place on earth for it; Harl Gant, a carter, that it is for the river, because a bell on a wharf with nothing in it and one stroke on it is a wharf thing; Wil Trotter, a dyer, that it is for nothing, he said himself it is not for anything yet, and I have been in this square thirty years and I do not think nine hundred people will hear one again; and Nesta Bray, with a barrow, who would not give a sentence until asked the fourth time, and who said: whatever he says it says; a bell is a mouth and a mouth belongs to the person whose claim is in it, and there is no claim in this one, so it belongs to whoever is standing there when it stops.** A **fifth voice, a cutler with a stall, said it twice, and the second time he said it to her, and she did not write it down** — because the four had given her a sentence in their own words and had stood there while she wrote it, and the fifth had given her a sentence for a stall and said it twice to a crowd, and one of those is a record and the other is a performance. She reads the slate back, one at a time; **Quiller wanted his rewritten to say *of the river, at the low end* and she would not let it; Trotter wanted his to say *nothing, nothing, forever* and, on hearing the first version, said that is not what I said, the difference is everything, because the first is a thing I am claiming and the second is a thing I am saying.** **Nobody in about nine hundred people asks her for a reason, and nobody puts a hand on the slate, and it is a man saying four sentences about himself and a bell with nothing in it that does that, and it goes in the other column, which is that about eleven people said out loud that they had seen the woman write, and it is the first time anything she has done has been noticed by anybody, and she was ten feet off.** And the finding she writes under it: **a note is a thing one person hears and a column is a thing nine hundred people can read, and a person who has lost the first one is not the worse person for reading, and everybody in every parish in this kingdom has a book.** At the landing she turns down a parcel, notices that the leash does not touch it and never has, and says the thing she has said four times since the boat, once, to a river: **the road is not for him and will not be after him; a name on a roll is not a place; a roll is not a road and no clerk has ever walked one; a road needs a person who turns up at the door and says her name out loud and lets the door decide; and the day somebody offers her a road that runs through him she will say no in about four seconds and will not be asked to explain.** He comes down at the eighth hour of the evening and asks her to price it. **She prices it at fourpence**, and it is not the price of the square: **nobody in that square can say one thing about that bell that is not a thing they were told, and the four sentences he said are the only source, and one of them said he does not know what it is for — everything else is a rumour with a child in it and none of it is his and none of it is anybody's, and that is fourpence a day forever, and it is the only kind of mouth that can be used on a new thing every single morning of its life.** And she says the price is not about her hearing, and that she has watched him use it about nine times by not using it. The slate goes on a trestle at the top of the landing with the price side up, **the fishmonger moves it to the front of his pitch, and by the next morning it is against a barrel of herrings, and on the fifth it is gone, and nobody ever established who took it, and she did not ask, because she had written in the margin that anybody might read it and nobody had to give anything to anybody to read it, and a thing you put where anybody may take it is a thing you have not given to anybody, and that is the difference between a slate and a leash and it is the only difference there has ever been.**

## Chapter 0069 — The Approving Return

Day 54, **the eighth hour of the morning** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the town. He has not got the approving return; he has got his own four-column page recording that he read it where it sat at Helling on the forty-eighth at about the seventh hour of the afternoon, and a deputy's name and hour at the foot of the entry. He reads his own handwriting and it says: **every word of it is correct, there is no sentence in it that is wrong, and it took a woman on a market square two days ago to show him where the thing is, and the thing is that it is not false.** His entry for the day says: **a thing may be a machine and still be correct; nobody is to tell me to read it the other way, and there is nobody here, and that is the difficulty.** The return, in its own words: where a household is returned as unable to make up its mind, the office records **one hand** against the line, the line is answered, the schedule goes on to the next house; it does not say the hand is the household's; it says the office is not required to hold a household in a room until it agrees; **a letter out, a wait, a letter back and an entry is about nine minutes of a clerk's day; if a line may be held open then every household that cannot agree is a line held open, and the cost of a line held open is a second round, and a second round is a cart and a man and a day, and a man will not come twice to one door**; the household is informed; the household may come and dispute it at any hour the office is open. **Every word of that is true, and the two things not said are that a household is informed by some means at some point, and that one hand does not know what the other four hands wanted.** He asks a man on the crane what happens in a house that cannot agree, and is told: they send somebody, and if the four still do not agree they put one of them down and go, and it is done, and there is a paper in a drawer with one of them on it and nobody in that house can tell you which, and it is not a thing that happens, it is a thing that happened, in about nine houses, and he has a mother in one of them and did not know for two years. He asks for the houses and is sent to the woman at the copper. **Ivet Slade**, who takes in washing at the far side of the square, nine lines out, about forty houses on her list, nine years at that address, **no age.** She stops with a sheet. He does not ask whether she is on one; he says he wants her to read the reason, and puts his own page on the table with the ninety words on it, and will not touch the page. **She reads it twice and says he has just done the single most useful thing anybody has done in her life about a sheet of paper, and it is no use to her whatsoever, and both are true at once and she would like both said.** She tells him what is not use: **the office is open from the ninth to the four, and it has been for about a hundred years, and the clerks go home at the four, and the office is shut on the last day of a round because the cart has gone, and no door in this town has ever been at a district office in its life.** He may write it down and **may not write her name against it**, and the reason is not fear: **every one of those forty knows exactly where I am at any hour of any day, and there is not one door in this town that has ever asked me for a piece of paper, and that is the reason I have said yes, and if my name goes in a book with a hand against it then I am a household and somebody will be able to come and find out who I live with and who does my washing and whether I pay my rates, and a person of my own is a thing that is on a roll somewhere, and I have spent nine years being a woman who is not on anything and I am not giving it up for a page in a book about other people.** She is entered in the book as **the woman at the copper**, and she makes him change a comma. Her household is five, and there is a sister in it who has not been asked a question about a return in nine years, and when the sister asked her what her own hand on a piece of paper meant, two years ago, she told her it meant that somebody had been to the house, and she has thought about that answer every week since. He then gives her the cost **both ways**: if the line is opened, a second round is a cart and a man and a day, and the five are a house that has been sent to twice, and **the thing that gets taken out of a house is never a hand in a column — it is a lease, or a rent, or a landlord, or a removal to two rooms over a yard, and the person in the column is not the person it happens to, and nobody ever asks that, and in another town four counties back it has already happened to a family and the man who ordered it was owed money and is not a villain**; and if it is not opened, the sister goes on the roll as if she had agreed to something she was never asked, forever, at no cost in money at all and at no way of finding out what it costs, **which is the reason it will not be opened by anybody, and the reason is not wickedness — it is that the second thing is free and quiet and it is on paper, and the first thing is expensive and it happens at a door.** She decides: **she is not going to open the line, and she is going to have her own answer put in, in her own hand, on her own paper, and to take it to that office herself on a morning when it is open, and to ask them to write down that the line is answered and that the hand on it is hers and not her sister's, and to say it out loud to a clerk.** She knows exactly what that is and names it: **me standing in a room with a hand in a column and saying that one is mine. And what it buys is that in nine years there is one line that a woman came in and claimed, and it is hers and it was not taken and she was not frightened. And what it costs is that my sister is on that line for the rest of her life and she will never know, and I am not going to tell her, because she is not going to ask, and because if I tell her she will spend a year being frightened about a form and I have watched that happen to two other women on this street.** **He promises not to come back — not tomorrow, not in a week, not when it goes badly, not when somebody in an office has been unkind — and gives the reason: after a decision the only thing he could want next is a consequence and she would not be able to tell the difference and neither would he — and adds, when she tells him he does not need to be that careful with her, that he has been careful with about four people in a month and every time it was the correct thing and not one time it was comfortable, and that he noticed the pattern in himself four days ago and does not like it, and would rather say so in a book than go on being careful and call it kindness. She laughs.** Then **the post comes up at about the fifth hour of the evening and there is a letter from Marta Vell, in her own hand, dated the seventh hour, four lines and a paragraph, with a word in the third line started and begun again.** It refuses: the first name on her page is not going to Kells, not down the water, not into a book with a stamp on it, and she is not going to be asked for it a second time; **she has had two days and has not been able to write until this morning and the reason is not that she was busy**; the day was hers and has gone and she is not making another one; she has a bell at about the seventh hour every morning and there is nothing in it that belongs to him or to his district; **he is not a bad man and she has thought about that for two days, and you did not write on the forty-fifth and I heard that afterwards from a carter and I would have written on the forty-fifth.** And she gives the fact: **when they entered her bell they came to the door and told her a day, and the day they told her is the day it goes in the book, and it is not the day they came; she has never seen them before or since and there was no man at her door on any day at all; it is earlier and she has not been able to find out by how much; and it is a fact about a day and not about a name, and she has said it out loud to nobody in nine years.** He copies it out entire, including the started word, and writes in his own book: **the answer is no, and it is the first refusal of a term she set, and it cost her two days and a word begun twice; the debt was mine, and I let a term of hers run out without asking her whether it still stood, and I did not do it out of carelessness — I did it because on the forty-fifth I found out I was going to want something, and that is a rule I invented on the fortieth, and it has cost me the only thing I went to Ocklebridge for, and I would do it again on about half the days and I am not going to pretend to a reader of this book that I would do it again tomorrow.** And: **the date is not one of the five, and they are two different documents, and a person who joins them has joined them because they are the same shape, and I did not come thirty miles to be a person who joins things because they are the same shape; I do not know how the five dates got there, I wrote on the fortieth that I am not going to guess, and holding a sixth fact of the same kind did not make me feel cleverer and I had expected it to.** And the only use the fact has: **the office is given a day and enters the day it is given, and the day it is given is a fact about the office and not about the thing.** He does not write to her again. At about the eighth hour of the evening he copies the four columns of the front room into the book entire, and then at about the ninth hour **a rider comes up the landing road with a letter that is not a Crown hand and not a district hand**, who will not give it to a woman at the door, who says he has not read it and would not, and that the man who gave it to him read the address off the top of a page with nine lines on it and had written none of it himself. **The wax is a private seal at about eleven pence, and on the back somebody has written in pencil *at your convenience*, and in the other half of the same upright hand, with a different pen and about nine hours old, *replies to follow*, underlined once.** Tomas does not open it that night. He writes why, and the reason is not care: **a letter is not a door and a man on circuit is not a door; it is that I can feel myself getting ready to be interested, and I know what that feels like, because it is the same feeling I had at about the ninth hour of the morning of the thirty-second in a watch house, and I went out of that building the best I had felt in a month and I had learnt absolutely nothing. If a thing makes me want to be a better man by the morning, it is not a door, it is a compliment.** And: **the woman at the copper made a decision and I read it back and shut the book and I am not going back, and the reason is that after a decision the only thing I could want next is a consequence.** And: **the approving return is still the best piece of paper I have read in a month and it is still signed by a man whose name I have had for six days and have not once been told what to think about him by anybody, and nobody in this county knows his name, and I have written it out twice now and both times I could not make it into a lie, and the second time I came nearer, and near is not far.** He writes that he is going to bed, and that if the man has written to him then tomorrow he will walk into a room with a man who has read a thing before he wrote to him, and that he has a fairly good idea that he does not know what that is like.

## Chapter 0070 — The Man Who Reads It Twice

Day 55, **the seventh hour of the morning** to the end of the evening, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a room over a haberdasher's shop in Bramden. **The name had been on three pieces of paper in six days and he had not met the man:** the return at Helling at the foot of the approval, the sheet the rider carried, and the letter itself, on a private seal costing about eleven pence. **Pell Rill** is not a big man, is not dressed like a district, is not like a company, and comes to the door before Tomas has reached the second stair, and apologises for having only one chair and offering the floor. **He was civil in a way Tomas has not been in a room with for about a month**, and Tomas is right about two things inside four minutes before Rill has finished saying them, **and after the fourth minute Tomas finds he is afraid of him, and it stays that way, and it is the first time in six weeks a room has frightened him without anybody in it being wicked.** Rill says he wrote the return, recommended the practice, stands behind every word of it, and would like to know what Tomas thinks is wrong with it; and when Tomas says, after a minute and a half of going at it nine times in four days, **nothing is wrong with it, and that is the difficulty and he is not going to pretend to have come further** — Rill says *I have made your week* and means it, **and Tomas feels the cold before he understands why, and writes that down at the fourth hour of the afternoon: he said thank you and he meant it.** Then the number, which Rill gives before the argument and which Tomas writes down and checks him on: **about eleven thousand held lines a district year; a held line is a letter out, a wait, a letter back and an entry, about nine minutes of a clerk's day; the office loses about four hours a day; two hundred working days, eight hundred hours; a clerk at eleven pence an hour, and he has that figure because he signs for it; about forty pounds a year; one clerk out of four; about two per cent of the district's answer; about three hundred and sixty pounds across nine districts — and that is the whole argument and he has never been able to improve it, and the number has never once been in a document.** Then the half that is his and not the district's, and which he has never said in a room: **what does the truth buy? It buys a household four hours of argument a day. Not attention — argument. The office writes back and says we cannot record one of you, a cart goes out, two men go into a kitchen, and four people who have never had that conversation in their lives have one now, and either they agree — in which case the line was never unable to make up its mind, they had not been asked — or they do not, and the line is still open, and there is no third. And that is the correct answer to the question the office was asked. That is why it survives. Nobody in this kingdom has thought to ask the question a different way and I have looked for nine years and have not found one, and I have been right, and being right about a professional matter is a thing I am very good at and it has never once been any use to me.** Rill then offers the schedule from his bag, **to read where it sits, no copy, never had one**, and says in advance that it is not a record and he does not call it one, so that Tomas cannot afterwards say he was misled. **The sheet has forty lines: a column of numbers, a column of dates, a column about a thumb wide with a mark in it, and a stamp upside down at the foot, and a hand at the head that is not Rill's, and nothing else. No column headed what the household said, no column headed who agreed, and no household name anywhere at all.** Tomas reads it three times and **cannot say what it is** — not because it is obscure but because it is legible, and there is nothing in it that belongs to anybody, and he has been in rooms with four hundred people and has never in his life been in front of a document that would not tell him what it was. **Rill refuses in advance to explain the column, says he has been asked that four times in nine years and has not answered it once, and would rather say no now than in an hour when he has Tomas's attention for nothing.** And then the frightening thing: **he read the schedule twice before he answered, and he says so, in a letter, in his own name, nine hours before they were in a room, and he explains why, and the explanation is ordinary professional care** — the first time he read it he could not tell whether he had read it or remembered reading it, and a man who cannot tell those two things apart should not write a letter about a document at all. Tomas thinks, and does not write it down for two days: **that is the most frightening thing anybody has done to me in a month, and the man did it out of the ordinary care a careful man takes, and he is not hiding from me, and I have spent six weeks working on the fact that everybody in this business is hiding from something, and here is a man who read a document twice and told me so, and I do not know what to do with it, and there is nothing to be careful about, and that is the danger.** At about the second hour of the afternoon Tomas says that he is not going to ask him to be a door and is asking the room to be a room, and that he has said that to one other room and Cope told him he had been the complaint and not the compliment, and that **a room of one is the hardest kind there is and he is aware of that and is doing it anyway.** Rill says he will answer anything and is not doing it because he thinks Tomas is right: **a man who is not asked keeps his own account; he has read about four hundred schedules and has never once been asked a question by a household, and he would like to be, and Tomas is not one, and he has not pretended he is.** Then the fifth question, not one of the four: **what is the document?** Rill takes a minute, writes for three minutes, and pushes it across. **It is a record. It is a record of the answer given, entered in the answering column against the line, in the order the answers were given; it is the only thing it is; it is not a household's answer, it is not a person's answer and it is not a copy of either, and the difficulty in the question is exact; a household is asked a question, the office does not obtain an answer from the household, the office obtains an answer from a person, the person is a person and not a household, the office records that it has obtained an answer, which is true, and the office records the answer, which is the person's, and treats the line as answered, which follows; there is no false statement in that; there is a great deal of true statement in it and one step across, and the step is a professional one and he has never thought it dishonest and has gone back over nine years of his own arithmetic looking for the dishonest step and there is not one; and the number is not in the record and never has been and is not in any record he keeps, it is in his head, his head is not a record, and that is the entire difference between him and a book and he would be obliged if Tomas would write that sentence down and keep it, because in about four years somebody will ask where the number is written and if he cannot say *nowhere* he will have to say *a man said it to me*, and that is a different and much worse sentence.** Then Rill asks to look at the day-book, standing up, hands behind his back, and reads one page. **Four columns, the question and the hour, the answer in its own column, the refusal in its own column, and nobody has merged them, and that is the first thing he says and he says it before anything else, and he asks to be told he has been told nothing in a way he could carry back.** Then the danger made visible: **that is the most expensive book in this county and you have written it for nothing and you have shown it to a stranger in a room and you did not ask me whether I was allowed to read it; the whole of the protection is that anybody may ask and there is no other; every person who has ever written anything down about a household has understood that inside about nine days and he has watched four of them and two stopped; you have not stopped and you have not asked me one question about whether I am a thing that ought to see it, and that is not carefulness, that is a man who has decided that being careful is what a careful person feels like.** And then: Tomas has written Rill's name in the book twice, both times with what he said, and in nine years, if anybody wants to know what Rill said on the fifty-fifth in that room, they need only find Tomas, **and there is one of you and there are not very many of me** — and he says it as the only warning he has got, about Tomas and not about the practice. Then Rill says there is a chair that has been empty all afternoon because the woman who travels with him is not in the room, and that **the question is not a household's, it is hers, and it is not Rill's to ask through Tomas and not Tomas's to answer for her** — and tells him he sent it up to a room over a draper's anyway, behind his back, and would rather he heard it from him than found it out from a rider on a road in three days. And: **you have asked me five questions today that were not yours and every one of them has been the right question, and I have enjoyed all five, and a man who enjoys five questions of that kind in one afternoon has stopped asking whether the person in front of him is the right one to ask.** The runner comes up at about the fifth hour of the afternoon. **Tamsin Rook is in the room**, reads the note twice, and says: *say that I have read it and that I say yes, and I want him to write the four lines in his own book with my name at the head of it and the hour against it, and I want him to write them exactly as I say them and I want to see them before he shuts the book.* **Rill's note told her precisely what it would cost — his own hand, his own book, this town, this date and hour, her name at the head, gone down the water with the entries, in a clerk's hall inside two days, and nobody able to take it out, including her and including Tomas — and told her she may say no, and that he will not ask again, and that the whole of what has gone wrong in his trade for nine years has been somebody being asked a question and finding out afterwards what it was for.** When Tomas comes back he says *she is going to do it*; Rill says **she asked me whether I would enter it and I said yes, and she asked me a second thing, which was whether I would enter that she had said yes, and I said no, and she thought about that for a while and then she let me enter that she had said yes, and it took her about nine seconds, and it is the only time in nine years that a person has taken the cost of a thing and protected the fact that they had taken it, and I have written down both halves and which half she spent the nine seconds on.** Rill adds that he wrote down the answer and **that a person's answer to a question, with the hour against it, is a record, and there is nothing in it anybody can be used for, because a person is not a hand and a person answers for herself.** And then the thing that frightens Tomas most: **you are carrying two leaves of paper of the same kind and you have never once put them in the same place** — the leaf folded in four in a stock book, and the leaf folded once in a book with a number on the spine — **and I believe you, and believing you does not make them the same.** Then, at about the fourth hour of the afternoon, the last question: where are the records kept. **For a schedule of this kind the entries go to a clerk's hall. Not a district office. A clerk's hall** — a building with shelves in it, one man in charge of the shelves and about nine others, who does not talk to a district officer and does not talk to anybody else either, and **is not obstructive, he is a man with eleven thousand pieces of paper and a set of correct rules about who may hold them.** And then he takes something away: **Tomas has no standing in that building, and this is not a complaint and not a refusal, and there is nothing by which a man who is not a district officer may ask a clerk's hall for anything at all, and if he goes down there he will be told so pleasantly and correctly, and Rill is not permitted to say it and is going to, because a man who arrives at a door without knowing what the door is for gets a worse answer than one who does.** **The town is two days by the river post, and he will not name it — in a letter and not in this room — because if he names it then the next thing that will happen is that Tomas will be in that town, and the thing after that is that somebody there will know Tomas went, and he would rather Tomas found it the way he has found everything else, by asking somebody a question they were not expecting.** Tomas says *then what is the use of you*, hears himself, does not take it back, and Rill calls it the fairest thing anybody has said to him in nine years: **he has given four things — a document, a reason, a number and a refusal; three are his to give and one is not, and the one that is not is the only one of the four that is any use to anybody; the other three are how he knows what he is doing, and without them he could not tell whether a practice was sound or merely something he is used to — and he recommended one that was not sound, in a year in another district, and it stood four years, and it was found by accident by a person who had no reason to be looking and had not been paid to be; and he makes a thing visible by saying he is not going to say it, about four times a year, and it has never once helped anybody and he does it because it is the only trade he has.** He walks back at about the seventh hour of the evening. She is at the window and says, before he can ask: **he wrote it down in front of me and I read it and it is a good sentence and it is not mine; I have been eleven years writing sentences at doors that people took away and I have never once had one of mine written down as being mine; I am going to be sick about it and I am not going to be, because I priced it in a smith's shop on the fifty-first and I paid it with my eyes open on purpose and you are not going to make it into something you did.** And to *you should have asked me*: **then you would have said no, and you would have been right, and it would have been the fourth time, and I have been the fourth thing that happens to you twice over now and I am not doing it twice.** Two hours of writing, in four columns, in the flat hand, nothing merged, including the part where he contradicts himself, which is in the fourth column on its own and which he read back twice and left. **The last entry of the fifty-fifth records that nothing happened: Rill is not a villain, he is a competent courteous careful auditor who agrees about two things and is right about both, and he answered every question in his own name and in writing and gave him nothing that is not his to give and one thing that is not his to give and told him in advance that he was giving it. And the thing lost: for six weeks he has had a use for being right, and every man who refused him was a man standing between him and a question he could work on, and a man who agrees with you has nothing to be angry at, nothing to work on, no reason to be careful and no lever, and he does not have one; and the number is about two per cent and it is good and he cannot answer it, because he has no answer to forty pounds a year and he has an argument about a signature, and there are four hundred people in a clerk's hall two days down the water who are owed an argument and not one of them will be helped by his argument. And: a record has to be kept somewhere; it is a shelf and a man and about eleven thousand lines; it is two days down the water; there is a town with a clerk's hall in it; the name of it has been given to him by nobody and he is not going to have it handed to him by an auditor who is being kind to him in a room on his last day in it. So: the river goes down. It takes two days. He has a barrow and a frame and a receipt with five clauses and a bell in a crate that a master certified in his own hand with the hour, and a name to look at in a room he has not found yet, and a fact about a day that nobody asked a woman for. And he is going down the water with nothing to say to anybody, which is a thing he has never once set out to do, and he has not rung the bell since the fifty-second and is not going to ring it on a river.** He shuts the book and puts it under the mattress, which he has never done, and is careful about that too and could not have said why.

## Chapter 0071 — Down The Water

Day 56 into Day 57, **the second hour of the afternoon** to **about the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the river. He does not want the post — the post goes on the second and comes up on the fourth and the second was the fifty-second — and takes a **carrier's boat** that goes when it has a load and lies up at a landing overnight, and carries barrows because barrows do not walk. A boatman asks what is in the crate. **The honest answer is that he bought a bell to say something with and he does not know what, and a man who says that on a river is a man who has already told a stranger he has nothing** — and he says it anyway, and the man writes *a bell* and tells him it is the shortest honest entry he has made in nine years, and then tells him that a bell with a keeper's name in it is a bell two men are minding and he would have had to ask. **A clerk's hall is not a district office and a river knows the difference**, and a barrow does not go into Miremarket, because a carrier's book says what a thing is and a clerk's hall has a man in it who writes down what a thing is, and two books about one barrow is a thing a carrier does not want. **MIREMARKET** comes off the wood cover of a book in a column headed for the place a load is going, and it is the first name for a place he has had out of a document in a month, and he did not have to work for it. The frame is bolted at every place it is set down; the cradle rides on the barrow's bed; the crate goes in as a **smith's consignment**, which he tells her is a lie by omission and which he does not put in the book. He tells her in advance that he is going to pay a man with a handcart two shillings and sixpence to take a barrow and a frame round a town, and a shilling for a yard and for not asking, out of five shillings. **She does not stop him, and gives him the reason he is doing it, and he is right about the reason and it is not the frame and it is not the bell: five weeks of being refused for nothing has left him entirely willing to pay a stranger to look away, because a free man is a thing a town will talk to at any hour and a man who pays is a thing a town has a price for.** The day-book entry admits that one thing has been left out of it and that writing that it is left out is not the same as having it in. They land at about the ninth hour of the evening. Nothing is rung. No clause is broken, and the fifth clause is used twice, both times as a fact about himself, and it tells him the truth and costs him a place.

## Chapter 0072 — The Column Can Be Certified And The Column Cannot Be Certified

Day 58, **the sixth hour of the morning** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the town. He pays the two men; the frame goes into a yard behind a draper's shop and is bolted; the crate goes to a smith's yard. **Ivo Trant**, man in charge of the shelves, about twenty-two years in the building, is pleasant and correct: **there is nothing in this kingdom by which a man who is not a district officer may ask a clerk's hall for anything at all**, here is a form, and there is no box on it for a reason. Tomas gives the form back and says he is not most people, and Ivo Trant says that is the first sentence anybody has said at that counter in about four years and that **not one of the nine hundred lines going into that hall this year is a complaint.** A carter in a yard says, unasked, that **the round comes twice, the money comes with it, and the money is the reason nobody here is unhappy**, and Tomas underlines it in his book. He asks his question **nine times** in a market town and gets nine true answers and can use none of them, **which is the first time in five weeks the talking has got him nothing, and he cannot tell whether the method is wrong or whether he is asking the wrong people, and he does not guess.** At about the sixth hour a clerk in the street says the two words: **the answer rolls** — a clerk's word for a set of shelves, standing for the rolls where the answers go, nothing grander, **about eleven thousand a year, the office does not leave the line open, it records one hand and the line is answered.** And then, asked properly: **you can certify anything inside a column and you cannot certify the column, and nobody has ever asked to, and the reason is not secrecy — a column is not a household and a household is not a person, and a person is the only thing in this kingdom that can be asked a question and can refuse to answer it. Everything else in here is paper. A person is not paper and there is no box for one, and that is not a design. That is just what is true.** At about the ninth hour, in a lane, a woman tells him about a book at the end of the lane and **will not give him a name and does not know whether the woman has a husband or a mother or five children.** He does not knock. He writes *Bess Marrow* in the fourth column with the hour and calls it a lead, and says that is the first one in nine days and the other three were buildings.

## Chapter 0073 — A Name At The End Of A Lane

Day 59, **the fourth hour** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, the east lane. She goes for a page and gets a room, and the reason she cannot ask is not the leash and not the hearing and not courage: **a roll is a list of places that people are not, and a page is not behind a door, and the one thing a courier may do that a clerk may not is go to a door and say her own name out loud and let the door decide.** She says in advance that the three questions are the same three she has asked at ten doors in four counties, that she will not change a word, and that the answer goes on a thing anybody may read. **Bess Marrow**, household of five, one of them a mother who cannot write, answers the first question with: **yes, eleven times, the hand on the paper is mine about nine times in ten, and what is on the paper is not what I wrote — it is a mark, in a column, against the line.** She refuses the second question and asks for the third one first. **Third: do you want that written down, and may I write your no as a no and not as nothing. Yes, and yes to both halves, and my name on it — and here is why I am not giving you the book: the day this book leaves this house it stops being the book of my house and it becomes evidence about my house, and it is the only thing in this kingdom that says what the five of us actually said, and the district has never in nine years had that, and I have got a mother who cannot write and a brother who cannot read and a house that is paid for to the end of the year.** She then lets Tamsin read the book in the room, with her looking at it, and does not let her take or copy it. **The finding: in eleven places the book and the paper the hall sent back to that house and keeps in the same drawer disagree — the day on that paper came off a roll, and she has not seen the roll and has not asked to — and in nine of the eleven the day the hall holds is a day the round was not in that street** — and Tamsin says out loud that it is what it looks like from a kitchen table and that she will not improve it into something she knows. On read-back Bess Marrow changes the third question's last three words: **write your no as a no, and stop talking.** **She does not ask whether Jory Fenn is on a roll in this town, has not asked one person, and is not going to**, and gives the reason: a woman in front of her is a person and a page is a page, and she would be doing to about eleven thousand households what she has spent eleven years refusing to do to one. Her finding, in her own hand, unimproved: **the road has not found anything. The road has made her findable.** Her bell is not rung. Nobody asks her for a reason.

## Chapter 0074 — The Clerk Who Is Not Hiding

Day 60, **the fourth hour of the morning** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a street outside the clerk's hall and a room behind it. **The round is in Miremarket** and about a hundred and forty people put their own hands into a book at a table in a yard in the open air, one at a time, and there is not one face in it that has been swallowed, and he writes the shortest line in his book about it and cannot work out what he is doing. He asks the front door **which clerk goes out on the round** — a question about the work, not a demand — and finds in nine seconds a door that is not a door; Ivo Trant tells him the hole has been there for twenty-two years, has never been thought about, and about four men in this kingdom have put a boot through it, **and that a man who says he will not ask again is worth about four men who did not say it.** Tomas then says in advance, at the counter, that he will stand in the street until the building shuts, will ask one clerk one question in his own name in the street in front of anybody there, and will not ask a clerk anything in this town again for a week. He stands there **five hours**. **Elias Nabb**, four years in the answering column and about eleven years in the hall, one child and a house, answers **what is the document** in six minutes on that office's own paper with the hour, signed in his own name, and adds two things nobody asked for: **the practice is a settlement of a difficulty, the difficulty is a household, I did not write that, and I think it is right**; and **I have never in four years been asked what the mark means, I have assumed it means what it says, I am not sure, I am not going to ask, and if I am wrong then four years of my hand is wrong.** He cannot be made to say what a column ought to be and says so in advance so nobody wastes a quarter of an hour, and the reason is that **a clerk's name in a book is the only thing in the room that can be used against him.** And then: **he is the only person in that building who has ever been asked a clerk a question**, and Elias Nabb, standing up with his coat on, says that either that is the one thing the trade has or it is a man talking to himself in a room, and that there is no difference between the two and neither of them will know which for about four years, and that he will not be asked again this week. The bolts are checked; the frame is still in its cradle; nothing is rung.

## Chapter 0075 — Four Columns Against A Column

Day 61, **the seventh hour of the morning** to **the eighth hour of the evening**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, a market of about nine hundred and a landing. She buys a slate for two pence and draws four columns and **has nothing to put in them, because Miremarket is not asking a question about a claim** — and says out loud, four times, that there is no bell rung in that square and if she fills column one she is the man who decides what a bell is for. She gets eleven named bystanders into column two and reads them back and two of them change their own words. **A man with sheepskins asks her what she is doing, and tells her before she answers that she is the only person in the square doing it and that being the only one is not a column, it is a woman with a board, and shuts his stall and does not speak to her again all day** — and she knows what he is going to say before he says it, and does not write him down. **Dory Larkin says no out loud at a cheese pitch, in about nine hundred people's hearing, to a district man with a book on a stool, who enters it and tells her it is on the record with the hour on it and anybody may come and read it** — and she says she has said no nine times. It costs them the front of the row: **a market keeper moves their pitch to the end of it, about four pounds a quarter over a season, no appeal, not your fault, and he would have said the same to anybody.** The mother says the number in the lane and Tamsin does not hear it, and writes that there is a class of thing a person in a crowd can miss entirely and it is not the loud thing. **And it changes nothing at all**: the district man tells her, without weight, that the line is closed, that he wrote no, that there is no box for a name in that book and has not been one in eleven years, and that **the record is a record of the line being answered and the line is the office's.** She gives the slate away to a woman with a stall of needles, price side up, and says why: **a slate in a market is a thing a town can read, and the only question left is whether anybody in it will fill one in.** She prices it at **fourpence, the same as in Bramden, and it has doubled, and she has been sitting on a step for an hour and cannot say why and is not going to pretend she can.** She tells him the four things in order without softening one. Her bell is not rung. Nobody asks her for a reason.

## Chapter 0076 — The Method Is Right And Does Not Work Here

Day 62, **the fifth hour of the morning** to **about the sixth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the room over the draper's and the counter of the clerk's hall. **The volume's midpoint reversal, and it is not anticipated before this chapter.** He lays out everything he has — **ten things and a slate, a receipt with five clauses, and a household's own book in a kitchen drawer** — and **ten is the number he counts and not the number there are, because four of the ten came out of one office on one morning and are one line in a book in a coat** — and **it is entirely correct and it adds up to nothing.** He asks her to come and be in the room when he puts the question in, **because the question is hers; she says no once, in one word, and gives a reason that is hers and not a grown — the sixth use of the leash, the first that costs anybody anything, and the cost is his: he goes in alone and has nobody to read it back to.** At the counter he says out loud, before anything, that he is not asking for a document, a copy, a page, a sight of a shelf or an entry, only one question, and Ivo Trant answers that anybody may ask, that it has meant that since before he came, and that it is the only thing in the building nobody has ever used, and offers him a choice of the hall or the clerk, and Tomas takes the clerk. **Bryn Pethick** answers in nine seconds, reads the other two answers side by side, says **all three men are right**, and is honest that he knew who Tomas was since the fifty-eighth and said nothing and that it was laziness and not kindness. Tomas then asks the question nobody has asked: **whose answer is on the line.** A clerk has to go and get it, and it takes eleven minutes, and the line is a household of five, asked on the sixtieth, **answered in the household's own hand and own words, and withdrawn two days later in the same hand in the same column with the hour on it** — honoured, correctly, in the best-kept column in the hall — **and the line is closed anyway**, in the fourth column, in the clerk's own hand, in a box four inches wide that is the best box on the form and has no box for a reason on it. **What a line needs is a signature and a stamp. One signature, and a stamp, and that is all. There is no household signature on the closing and there has not been a box for one on that form in about forty years.** Nobody in the room is a villain and nobody is lying and the clerk is right. The finding, in his own hand: **an answer is a person, and the roll does not need anybody's answer; a roll is not a document about consent, it is a document about a line being closed.** And on a flyleaf, nine words: *a thing that comes out of a bell a long way off arrives thinner, rounder and more certain than it went in; a roll is the same instrument, made of paper; it is worse, because nobody has to stand in a lane to ring it* — **and under it, that it is a fact and not a power, and that he has not learned where a warning lands and never has and does not now.** He fills all four columns. What he lost: the last thing he wanted, a person to be angry at who was not kind to him. And what he had been carrying: not a method but a belief that the method was his, and the belief is the part that has to go. What is left: **he can be useful to one household at a time**, and there are about eleven thousand of them and nine days. He decides he is not going to ring the certified bell in this county at all.

## Chapter 0077 — What A Name On A Roll Is For

Day 63, **the fourth hour** to **the sixth hour of the evening**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, a street behind the market and a front room.
