She says once, to nobody, and it is not a speech: **a name on a roll is a mouth that has not been opened yet.** She asks nine people and gets eight answers that are not facts. The ninth is **Reuben Skell**, four years with a name on a line, glad to be asked, in no difficulty: *no one put my name there, I did, I have done it four years, and I am living in this house.* Then he gives her the trade: **a name on a roll is a duty, and a duty is a chore, and after about four years it is a mouth** — his father was named and did not go for four years and nothing came, and *the day it costs somebody something is the day I will answer it.* **A mouth is not an opening. It faces out, and what it says is whatever the person in the house is willing to have it say, and the person in the house never once has to open anything.** She connects it to a boy of eight, is wrong twice in one afternoon in the same direction, and **he stops her four sentences before the worst thing she could do in that town and says she has built a thing that is safe and it is permanent, and those are not the same.** Then he beats her, and she has no answer: **a child of this house died of a fever, and a district cart came to the door on one of the days and offered to carry anybody that wanted to go, and nobody wanted to go, and the district was in the street because the street has houses in it that had not answered** — and he has been owed something for about a year and nobody in this kingdom has asked him what it is, and **she is asking him as though the round is the worst thing that has ever happened to that house.** The third question, the twelfth, does the work: **yes, and put the cart in it.** On read-back he changes one word: **take the kingdom out of it, because a kingdom is a word a man uses when he wants a thing to be bigger than the room it is in.** She prices it at fourpence and says she has not got fourpence's worth out of that room. She tells him her own line: **a name on a line is a mouth, a mouth is for a claim, and about nine hundred households in this town and about eleven thousand in the district behind it have become one without anybody asking them.** There is a cart in the street at the end and she cannot tell, standing in a road, whether she has been given the trade by a man who does it or told by a man who is owed something that she has been doing the wrong thing for a month. **No age is given for the dead child and none may be. Her bell is not rung. Nobody asks her for a reason.**

## Chapter 0078 — The Household That Says No Twice

Day 64, **the fourth hour of the afternoon** to **about the ninth hour**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the end of a row and a lane off a lane. **He has never been in this row and Dory Larkin has never seen him in her life, and the first thing she tells him is that he was not in this street **last night** — there were nine carts at the end of it, two men lifting a crate off one of them, and a woman with a board stood in the road for about a minute and a half and wrote something on a slate and went up the hill with it. **Sam saw the woman and told her at about the seventh hour last night, and she has had three days over the refusal and not one of them over this, and she has not slept.** She refuses him the whole of the rest of it and refuses to tell him why the carts were there: *it is not a secret, it is a thing that happened to this house about a year ago, and I have said it out loud once and it was to the woman with the board, and I am not going to say it a second time to a man I have met four minutes ago in a row, and when you go up that hill tonight you will not be able to put it in a book.* He says he is not going to ask for it and she says that is the part she has not slept and cannot get round: **a man who does not ask twice is a thing she has never had in this row and she has had about four landlords.** He stands there and it goes in the third column in one line: *she was correct and it was not a wound and I did not treat it as one, and it is the first time in five weeks that anybody has refused me in a way that was not a matter of standing.* **Then she tells him he is not here about the cart, he is here about the no, and that a woman with a board wrote down that she said no to a man with a book on a stool in nine hundred people's hearing, with her name at the head of it and the hour on it, and that she has read it four times, and it cost them the front of the row, and she wants him to say out loud that he knows it cost them.** He does: **about four pounds a quarter over a season, the market keeper said it at about the second hour of the afternoon on the sixty-first, and her mother said the number and he did not hear her mother because he was in a yard four streets off with four bolts in it, and he did not know any of it had happened until the third day, and he has wished he had heard it — and the reason he wishes it is the woman with the board, who was nine feet off and wrote it down in her own book in a lane that night and told him there is a class of thing a person in a crowd can miss entirely and it is not the loud thing. She was not wrong. He was in a yard, and nobody came and told him, and that is the whole of what he did about it.** **Second refusal: you have not asked me for anything, do not.** She is right about what he is: a man with a barrow and a thing in a yard he cannot ring and a sheet of paper with a man's hand on it, and there is not one thing in this kingdom he can do for this house, **and the last person who asked her to let him help was her landlord and he had her rent** — and it is worse because he is not a bad man: *if you were a bad man I would have said no in nine words and shut the pitch and been done.* **And she is not going to have a page. The day there is a page about my house that is not in this house then this house is a thing that is about, and I have read about nine families in this town in about four years and every one of them has got less now than they had.** He has the leash ready and **does not use it**, and writes down in his own words that the reason is that a person saying no in a reasonable form is a person who has already done the work. Then he asks the question he had not known he came for — *what would you want written down* — and she gives him **one sentence, and he writes it exactly and reads it back, and she changes one word: *asked* to *sent for***, because a cart comes and a man comes and nobody in that house has asked anybody anything. **We are five and we do not agree, and we are not going to be sent for again, and we mean it, and any hand on that line is one of the five of us and not the house.** And she tells him what happens if they refuse a second time: **nothing. Refusing is free and refusing is invisible and she has done it nine times and it is the only free thing she has ever had out of a Crown building. If we answer it takes him about nine minutes and it is done; if we do not answer it takes him the same nine minutes and it is done. He writes something in a column she has never seen and goes on to the next one, and the thing he has written is not us and it does not have our name on it, and no person has ever come to this pitch or this house or this street about that line, and a man in the hall told her *the line is answered* and would not say by whom.** **A household does not have to be brave to be left alone by an office. A household has to be inconvenient, and it takes about four years to find out, and it is the only defence anybody has found against a line.** And the mother is the reason she has not collapsed, because **the mother cannot read and the mother can count, and about two years ago the district put a rate on this pitch without saying what it was for, and the mother went in on her own and got it taken off and it took her most of a day, and she has told that story about four hundred times since, and she is the only person in that house who has ever been in that building, and she has never once asked what a mark in a column is and her daughter has never once told her there is one.** He is not allowed to be there when they say it, and the day-book's fourth column carries the finding: **on the sixty-first, in that row, a woman with a slate asked a woman at a pitch whether she had ever been put on a paper without being asked to, and he was four streets off and did not know until the third day; that question was the correct question, it made a column, and the column is what cost them the front of the row — and the question he asked at about the seventh hour this afternoon was not a better question, it was a different one, and the woman he learned the difference from is the one who told him he had not been in the row, and she did not know she was teaching him.** No age is given for anybody in that household.

## Chapter 0079 — A Man In Chair With The Answer In His Own Hand

Day 65, **the fourth hour of the morning** to **the sixth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a street, a lodging book, two rooms over a dyer's. He cannot get the name of the district officer out of the clerk's hall and finds it in eleven seconds from **the woman who lets the rooms**, who keeps a four-year lodging book in her own hand, and who supplies the batch's smallest new fact: **a lodging book is a record of who is in a town with a night and what they owe and a room, and it is exactly the same shape as the roll in the hall and neither of them has ever heard of the other.** He says at the door that he has no standing, is not asking to be believed, and wants an hour. **District Officer Hester Vowles**, about eleven years, reads both answers standing up with her hands behind her back, and **asks whose hand the second one is before she asks anything else**, and is told in the first minute, and says that is worth more than either sheet. **An office can act on a practice and cannot act on a person, and not out of caution.** She can stop a practice in her district that afternoon. A district is a ground and you cannot act on a ground. **And she corrects his arithmetic and is right: three hundred and sixty pounds a year is the nine districts that adopted the practice, there are nineteen under the county, the other ten spend it and spend more because an open line is nine minutes and a household that argues is about four hours a day, and a district that has not adopted the practice has not saved anything, it has moved the cost, from a counter to about nine hundred doors.** If all nineteen stopped it, about seven hundred and sixty pounds a year, **which is about the price of a small bridge and is not money anybody finds in a place anybody looks, and a district gets an allocation, and that is not a failure of his finding, that is the finding.** She tells him the finding of the twenty-eighth is worth nothing to him because he has no standing and because the only person who could use it is a household, **watches him not ask for it, tells him why he did not, and says that is the exact reason nothing in this county is going to change while he is alive.** Then she asks him the question nobody has asked: **what do you want me to do with these.** He cannot answer it, in about a minute and a half, and she lets it take him. **What he wanted, and says so, is a document that a person could not refuse** — and it has not been available since the thirty-second and he has known that since the fortieth and did it anyway for eleven days. Eight of the nine others wanted a document and one wanted her to be a door. He writes to her twice: a question and an answer on her own paper in the room, and a letter that costs eleven pence and asks for nothing.

## Chapter 0080 — The First Household To Say No On The Record

Day 66, **the fourth hour** to **the eleventh hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a room over the draper's and a market. **The room is his own lodging and the Larkins know it.** A disputed line is entered in a room that is not the hall at an hour the office is open and any person with business in the line may attend; a person with none is to be asked to stand outside; **nobody thought of a reason to put a man out of it, and that is how he was there.** About nine people: Ivo Trant in the chair, Elias Nabb at the counter, Hester Vowles by the window, Bryn Pethick in a corner who does not speak once, a district man with a book, the market keeper, four people off the row, and three Larkins. Nabb reads the line twice and nobody stops him the second time, and enters the household's sentence in the answering column, in their own words, unshaded and unshortened, **and then asks for a hand against the line, in about four seconds, because that is what the form is. Sam Larkin puts his hand on the counter. It is not the hand of the person who spoke the sentence. Nobody in the room sees it happen and the entry is then a complete record and it is good and it is on a shelf in a hall with a stamp at the foot of it, and the whole of what this volume is about happened inside ninety seconds and was in the correct form.** **Tamsin Rook saw it from the stair, nine feet away, and is the only person in the building who knows, and writes it on the back of her own hand and tells nobody and tells him nothing and is not asked for a reason and will not be thanked.** Then the market keeper, who came: **a second refusal is a dispute and a dispute gets a cart, and a row with two carts on it is a row with nine stalls shut, and if it is a third time he moves the whole of that row off the market and there is nowhere else in this town, and he would like it noticed that he said it in a room and nobody in the room asked him to.** The district man gives the arithmetic without weight: **a cart and a man and a day, on a day you do not choose, from the ninth hour this evening until the second hour in the morning, and the only part of it not in a book is that it will not be a whole day, because you will be at the door at the fourth hour and there is nobody left in this district who would send a cart to a house that is at its door at the fourth hour.** The cart comes at about the ninth hour, early, because the round is closing. **Four shillings of the day's takings gone, the goods stacked against a wall in a yard, and Sam's handcart does not earn — two shillings and sixpence is a good day's carrying, and a carter who misses a market is moved to the back of a board in this town.** The mother says the number once and will not say it twice: *Sam's two and six is gone and the four shillings is gone and the pitch will be shut until the second hour and I have done that sum nine times and it comes to the same figure every time. The figure is no.* And then, to nobody, in a room with nine people in it: **and it is the season.** No speech, and nobody said it was worth it and nobody said anything at all. **And Tomas Vale stood against a wall for two hours and ten minutes and did not say one word.** The finding, in his own hand: **a refusal is a record, and a record is a sentence that other people may read, and her whole practice for eleven years is to keep a refusal from being read as nothing, and in this town a refusal is read as a household that has not answered, and a household that has not answered is a line open, and a line open is a second round, and a second round is a cart and a man and a day — the two practices are the same practice in two places and neither knows the other exists, and he has never once walked eleven yards in the same lane as a woman with a slate, and that is a discovery about a street and not about a town.** And the last line of the batch: **a name read on a roll begins, in about four years, to be read as a duty, a duty is a mouth, a mouth is for a claim, and nobody has to stand in a lane to ring it.**

## Chapter 0081 — A Name Read Before It Is Needed

Day 67, **the fourth hour, bare** to **about the eighth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a market street in Miremarket, a trough at the cross, a smith's yard, the yard behind the draper's shop, and a step. He works out in about four minutes that he wants a sentence saying what a name on a roll is *for*, and then in about four minutes that he cannot have it, **because he has already asked all four people who could have said it and every answer was correct and not one of them was a sentence about what a name is for. He has run out of buildings before he runs out of questions, and that is new.** He asks nobody anything. He stands in a street and lets it happen to him. At a canvas and rope stall a woman says to the woman behind her in a queue, in the ordinary voice: *you have not sent for it — it is on the line and you have been on the line four years and nobody in that house has ever sent for it.* At about the sixth hour, at the cross, two women at a pitch of nothing in particular give his own name to each other in the ordinary way, and one of them tells the other that a man with a barrow asked Wick what the roll in the hall is, out in the street, in nine words. **Nobody is stopped, nobody is forbidden, nobody is punished for saying a name out loud, and the person who says it is usually right about who she is.** He goes up past two rooms over a dyer's with a light in the window and does not go up, and he writes that down. He looks at a bell with no claim in it in a smith's yard and does not ring it. **The finding, written at about the fourth hour of the evening: a name on a roll becomes a duty in about four years and the household reads it that way first, before any office, and it is read out of a queue by a neighbour, and the neighbour is not an agent of anything and is owed nothing. And the professional half: a duty is a mouth, a mouth is for a claim, and the claim a household makes on its own name is not a claim against the office — it is the claim that the household is the one being asked.** He counts four bolts. **He is not angry and he records that as a fact about a man with a hole where the anger was and not as a virtue, because he does not think it is one, and thinks it is worse, and refuses to write a paragraph about how noble it is to be calm in a market town.** Nothing was rung, no clause broken, and the fifth clause was used once, as a fact about who he was not allowed to be: he was not permitted to ask a woman at a rope stall a question about her sister.

## Chapter 0082 — The Household That Has Been Sent To Twice

Day 68, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the seventh hour, bare**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, a stall, a lane at the far end of a town, a doorway, a gutter. **She cannot ask, and the reason is new and it is the volume's cruelty: a household that has been sent to twice reads a third visit as a debt coming due, and a household that believes a thing is owed answers.** At about the fourth hour at a stall of nothing in particular she hears the same sentence in different words — *you have not sent for it, and it is on the line, and I am not going to be the one who says it twice* — said to a stranger this time, and she is told where to go, for nothing, by a woman who does not know her. **Mave Verrow** comes to the door without asking who is there, which is now the second door in this town to have done that. Tamsin says her own name, says she is a courier, says the three questions are the same three she has asked at ten doors in four counties and she will not change a word, and says the price before the first question: **fourpence, and it was fourpence eight days ago in a market a day up the water, and it has doubled and she cannot tell the woman why and is not going to pretend she can.** **First question, the hand:** twice, and she put it there both times, and the first time she thought she was agreeing to something, and no one in this kingdom has ever put a question to her at a door. **Second, is the person living here:** yes, and it is the second time anybody has asked that sentence in four years, and she has never once asked what the mark is. **Third question:** *Do you want that written down, and may I write your no as a no and not as nothing.* **No.** She writes it, rules it off from the other two, does not merge, puts the hour against it. **The no is about the small one.** Mave has worked out in a kitchen at about two in the morning that **a mark in a column is an answer, that a hand against a line is a hand against a question, and that the only thing a hand against a question can be is the answer to it** — and that she has never once asked what the question was, and would do it again if she asked and was told and the question was small. **Tamsin says, once, in her own mouth, that being on a list that reads as a duty is not the same as being on a list that reads as a debt, that the difference is about four years and one neighbour in a queue, and that by the time it is a debt it cannot be got back by asking — and that she cannot make it a duty again.** She does not ask the fourth thing she wants to ask, because a no is a person and a page is a page and she had promised not to do to one household what a clerk's hall does to about eleven thousand a year, and she says the price of a no out loud: **a no in this town is a household that has not answered; an open line is a second visit; a second visit is a cart and a man and a day; and no cart is coming in three days and the one after that she cannot stop.** She goes and does not come back to that door. A carter with a barrow in the lane tells her, for nothing, that the round is shut and **the third thing is not going to be a round, because there is no round left to send, so it is coming off some other piece of paper, and he does not know which and has asked two people and one of them was rude about it.** **Her finding, written in her own hand and not improved: there is no book in this town that would tell her when, and she is not allowed to ask for one, and the only thing that has ever gone to a place to find out whether a person is in it is a person on foot with a bag.**

## Chapter 0083 — The Cost Of Being Right

Day 69, **the seventh hour of the morning** to **the eleventh hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the front of the clerk's hall, a room over the draper's shop on the north side, a row of pitches. He decides in about nine minutes to say one true thing out loud, in a room, in his own name, about a household he has no standing in, **because if he does not there will be a cart in that row a fourth time, and he has not wanted to do anything in this town for two days and this is the wrong thing and he is going to do it anyway.** **Ivo Trant** at the door: *you are standing in my street again*; he has not closed the door, says so twice, and says neither of those facts is kindness; **ask me about the work.** Work: **nobody closes a line in dispute**; the schedule wants it shut, the schedule does not want, so a man goes to the door; **that is what a cart is, and it is not a punishment and it is not the office being cross; it is the only instrument there is for a line that cannot be shut on paper**, and a line in dispute can happen as often as it is in dispute. Trant tells him to ask Nabb **at this door, in front of me**, and will not help him, and warns him: **it is a satisfaction in the room and a cart in a row in about four days, and the row is in this town and the cart comes from four miles away and nobody in the room is in the row.** At about the second hour of the afternoon, on the step, in about eleven seconds, in the street, Tomas says the one sentence. **Elias Nabb** does not move for about four seconds and says: **you have said a thing in a street that you cannot take back, and there is a person in this town who is going to have to act on it, and he is me, and you have not asked me to, and that is the only reason I am still standing here.** Then a room over the draper's, a shilling for the fire, about eleven people, **nine of them not knowing they were in a hearing.** He reads the Larkins' line out; **Nabb asks him in front of everybody, in nine seconds, whether the hand that went against the line on the sixty-sixth was the hand of a person of that household, and Tomas says no, and says he was the only person in the room who could see both of those, and that he said nothing for two hours and ten minutes, and says his name is on it.** Nabb takes eleven seconds and says four things: **he is a clerk and everything he has written in four years is his and nobody else's; the practice is a settlement of a difficulty and the difficulty is a household and *I did not write that and I think it is right* and he is not going to take it back off a counter because a man has been in a room with nine people in it; you are right; and I am going to close it, and the only way there is is a man at a door** — and he enters his own part, **that a person at this address has said in a room that the record is the thing that is going to cost this household, and that the person is a man with no standing, entered because he is obliged to and not because he wished to.** Then **the market keeper stands up at the back**: it happened on the sixty-first and it is happening again today, and if it is a third time he moves the whole row off the market, and there is nowhere else in this town, and **he says he is saying it to nine pitches and to a man with a shilling's worth of fire, and one of those two is why, and he would have said it in a week if a third cart had come.** The cart comes at about the ninth hour of the evening, early, because the round is closing. **The cost, named, and not one of them a child: nine pitches off a market of about nine hundred, and nowhere else to go; Ock Randall's twenty years, to a lane; Jessa Vinch's nine years of butter bought on credit against a pitch that is not there, and the credit was the pitch; the Larkins at the end of a row that is not there; about four pounds a quarter off each of nine households over a season, and about thirty-six pounds a quarter in a year; four shillings of a day's takings; two shillings and sixpence of a handcart that did not earn; a pitch shut from the ninth hour; a carter moved to the back of a board.** **Nobody was rescued and nobody was told this was necessary and nobody was told it was anybody's fault, and he wrote it down with the hour against it because it is the only thing he has ever been able to do about a thing of this kind.** The day-book, at about the eleventh hour of the evening, third and fourth columns, the hours flat: **being right and being useful are two different tools and he has only ever been issued one; he used both of the truths he was given — a district is a ground, and a clerk's own name in a book is the only thing in the room that can be used against him — and neither of them was wrong and both of them were about offices and he needed a person; an office can act on a practice, a person can be acted on by a person, and there is no clause about that anywhere in this kingdom, which is exactly why it is the only thing in the world that works on a person; he did not build a case and did not denounce anything and did not get to stop it afterwards; nobody in that room knew they were in it; the sentence that did it is a line in a book and a mark in a column and the office's own fourth column, four inches wide, the best box on the paper, with no box for a reason on it, and the man who made the record is the man who will be read by it; a record is a sentence other people may read, and he had that in his book for three days and wrote it as a thing he had found out — it is a thing he did; a method is not a tool, and a tool has a cost on it, and his fits him and not anybody else.**

## Chapter 0084 — A Name In A Market Queue

Day 70, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, a market of about nine hundred people, a lane, a step. She did not know at about the fourth hour what she was going to do and had no idea why she did it at about the sixth. **The market is not a queue** — she is standing inside about nine thousand separate queues and nobody in any of them knows there is one. **A queue is a thing with a shape:** people in it look at each other's faces for four minutes, remember not one word, and carry away one thing, and the one thing is almost always a name. **First,** at a pitch of needles and thread, **Nan Pyle** to a woman she does not name: it is not a debt the way a shop is a debt; a shop is a line with a name on it; the name is yours and not hers and nobody's that she has borrowed, so stop looking round the queue for who you are going to owe it to; and she gives the day — **the twenty-ninth of the first month, a day she has had in her head for four years and has never written down and could not source.** **Second,** at a fish pitch at about the fifth hour, **Hilary Croy** to **Bel Amsel**: the line is at Ashlade, it is four counties, it has not been answered this year or the year before, she would not swear it was ever answered, there is nobody at that house now to answer it with, and it has gone about four years without a hand; and she says she has told them a name in a market the way a woman tells a thing in a market, and could not tell you this afternoon whether that name is a man or a house or a child, and neither could you. **Third,** at about the sixth hour, **Joss Munn** with a barrow of onions, in a line of five, to **Oda Renn** and **Peth Bram**: it is the fourth year; it is not the same fourth year as the last one; in the first year it is a thing somebody has done and in the fourth year it is a thing you are; **it is a chore, you do it because the line comes round and the line has to be answered and the answer is us**; it is not a debt, a thing that is owed is owed to somebody and there is nobody, and a thing that is answered is answered to nobody and that is fine. **Nobody in any of those lines knew that the thing they were talking about was a document, and that is not concealment, there is nothing to conceal, and it is about nine minutes a year and they will all go home and forget it, which is what makes it work and what makes it impossible to touch.** **She wrote nothing down for an hour and a half** — eleven years of being careful in public means you do not write where a person can see you writing, and a careful courier is a courier who looks busy — **and writing nothing down is the one thing her trade has never let her do, and the fourth column has been empty every time in eleven years because she has never been in a room where the thing in the fourth column was going past.** She fills it, at about the ninth hour, standing at the back, and does not improve one word of it: **the list is made in queues; about eleven thousand of us a year; a name on a roll is read as a debt roughly four years before anybody in an office knows it is a duty, and by then there is no office, no document, no mark, and no hand but a neighbour's; and nobody is to blame and there is nothing to correct, because there is nothing to correct — two women said each other's names in a market; and you cannot go back into a market and unsay a name, and a claim goes sideways, and she has spent eleven years learning to make claims and has never once had to think about what a claim does to the person who pays it; and this is the same skill, exactly, and it has taken her eleven years and a month to work out that a thing she was proud of was the thing.** Then she does the one thing her whole method reaches: **she says a name back, at ten feet, in the woman's own words, out loud, so that about nine people hear it — and Nan Pyle says *did I say that*, and is told she did, and says she has never said it twice in her life and did not know this morning it was a thing she said.** **She did not ring anything, nobody asked her anything, and she wrote that she is not going to try to stop the list and is not going to take it back in the morning, which she always does.**

## Chapter 0085 — What The District Owes A Household

Day 71, **the seventh hour of the morning** to **the eighth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the outside stair of two empty rooms over a dyer's on the north side, a landing at the end of a street, a step of the draper's shop. **The round shut at the end of the sixty-sixth and Hester Vowles left the town before the light on the sixty-seventh, and the two rooms have been standing empty since with the round's account books open on a table.** The woman who lets them, with a lamp and a lodging book of about nine hundred names in four years, says she was not sorry, would have taken the fifth night off her, and is not being kind or unkind, **and both things she has told him for nothing are the same, and that is the whole of my trade.** He goes up anyway; the second stair is loose; a cold hearth, three books open at different pages, a chair pushed back. **There is nothing to ask, and she says *well*, and that is the whole of four days of a man's life.** He finds the district officer at about the fourth hour at the end of a street, **standing up**, with a district satchel, nine sheets on a string and a pencil, for about an hour and a half, because her circuit brings her back through this town for two hours. **He says he is here about the row, and wants to know what an office owes a household it has sent to four times, and wants it in a form with her name at the bottom of it. She laughs once.** She tells him the order is wrong — **ask me what the office is for first, and you will find out that the two questions have the same answer and the answer is no**, and then he asks. **The office is for the district's answer.** Four words, correct, written on about nine hundred things in eleven years. **You have been after a person the entire time, and no office in this kingdom is ever going to be a person, and a ground cannot say a thing either.** Then she answers the original question: **nothing.** **Nothing was done to that household by the district, and nothing was done to any of those nine pitches; there is no clause in this kingdom about a man who is not an officer saying a true sentence in a room in a town where nine hundred people buy cheese, and there is not going to be one, and if there is, she will not use it; she did not send that man — a clerk in her district's hall sent him, correctly, in the correct form, out of the correct column, because a stranger put a true sentence in a room and he had four seconds and he was right; if he had not been in that room he would have closed the line on paper at about the fourth hour of the afternoon on the sixty-ninth and the row would still be in the market, and there is no name in this kingdom for what he has done and she has looked for one in about four minutes.** And the half that costs her: **she can put her name to a practice, the practice is the district's, she can stop it here, she has been carrying the paper since the sixty-ninth, it is written and numbered and it is in the satchel, and she will stop the practice in writing and nobody can stop her; and it is worth the rest of this month and every month after it and it is not worth the ninth hour of last night, and she will not tell him it puts the row back because she has not got a piece of paper that says either way, and there is no piece of paper in this kingdom that could.** **He does not ask her to put the row back, and notices about nine seconds later that he had a sentence and did not say it and can hear it.** *Will you stop this for them.* **She would have said yes, he would have got it, it would have been a paper about a practice, the practice would have stopped, and the row would still be gone.** **That is the same move, and it is the whole of the six weeks: a door is easier than a household, and a door has a name on it, and a household has to answer.** She writes the answer out standing up on a crate at the foot of the steps, on the back of the sheet from the sixty-fifth, **because there is nothing else to write on and says so out loud before she does it** — the office is for the district's answer, it is neither good nor bad, it cannot say a thing out loud because a ground cannot say a thing, **it owes that household nothing**, and stopping the practice is worth the rest of this month and every month after it and is not worth the ninth hour of last night — and signs it, and puts the hour against it, and gives it to him. **No second copy and he did not ask for one, and he wrote in his own book that he should have, which is now the second time he has written that down about two different people and has never once done anything about.** She offers him one question before she goes, tells him in advance she has a carriage on that road in about an hour and will say neither yes nor no, **and that it is not modesty, it is that there is nothing in her that a woman standing at the end of a street could not do better with a slate.** **He does not ask the tenth question.** The two rooms stay shut, nothing was rung, and the day-book at about the eighth hour of the evening records the four words, the paper, the hearing himself, the knowing he has carried since the fortieth, **that he has stopped asking offices for pieces of paper and the only thing he has got out of six weeks of asking is a thing nobody is going to hand him and a street at the end of which a woman is going to be poorer than she was on the sixtieth because he asked a question properly**, and in the fourth column the one line he has not improved.

## Chapter 0086 — The Child Who Is Sent Because A Name Is Owed

Day 72, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour, bare**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, a stone landing with a crane and about nine boats, a road up off the water. She had a day of nothing and spent it at the landing, **because there is no hour for it — there is no book in this town that would give her one and she is not allowed to ask — so she went and stood where the things arrive.** She works out three things in two hours and a half. **She cannot find the road:** she knows how to find a road, that is the whole of the trade, and there is no road to this — there is a lane, a cart, a day, and the day is on a piece of paper she has not read and is not going to be able to ask for. **She cannot be at a door:** eleven years in sixteen words, *a courier goes to a door and says her own name and lets the door decide*, **and what is about to happen is not a door deciding.** A carrier's cart comes up the road at about the seventh hour and about nine people come down the lane, and ninety seconds after it stops the talking starts in the ordinary way, and a woman at the head of the lane says it flatly: **that is the Verrows' honouring it — four years, two answers, and the third one is the small one, and she has done it right, you do not send a thing and leave the line open, that is what an open line is** — and to *you could have said no*: **I have said no for four years and I have been asked twice and I said it twice and nothing came, and now a cart is here and I am not going to be the woman who has had a line open for four years** — and to *that is not a choice anybody gives you*: **that is a choice I gave myself in about two in the morning and it was the wrong choice and I have not been told so by anybody and I am not going to be.** **The resistance that stops her asking: a household that believes it has agreed cannot be asked whether it has.** A person who has said yes cannot be asked what they said yes to, because the answer is that they said yes, and the only person who could say anything else is a man with a book on a stool who has gone to another county. **She stood there and did not ask, and it was the correct thing, and it was the worst hour of the seventy-second.** **What came down the lane was not a person being taken anywhere: the child was not being punished, is not an offender, is on nothing, is not in anybody's book this morning, and the person putting them on the cart is not wicked and has not done one wrong thing this morning or in four years.** Mave Verrow walks them down and has her arms round them for the first half and then takes her arms off, **which was the hardest thing she did in the whole forty minutes and which nobody on that landing saw as anything at all except one woman with a slate.** *Then who is it for:* **it is for the name, that is all it is for and there is nobody at the end of it; you send for it and it is done and there is nobody to be angry at and nothing to come after; I have had four years of a line open and I am not having a fifth.** **The child gets on the cart.** And **Tamsin Rook does not get on the cart** — four inches of an eleven-year rule, *the courier does not get in the cart* — and she does not claim the child, does not say the child is hers, does not say the child is on a roll, does not say where the cart is going, and does not ask one person on that landing a single question. **What she does is say a name out loud, once, in the open, at ten feet, with a landing of about nine people on it: *Mave Verrow is a person and not a house.*** **Nine words are the whole of what she did on the seventy-second and she has not improved them.** She does not know whether the name is the woman's or a child's and she does not say and she was asked by nobody. **About four of the nine heard it** — the woman at the foot of the cart, a man sitting on the shafts, and a woman going past with a pail of water who had not been going to look at anything and stopped dead in the road and put the pail down. The cart goes up the road off the water. She does not know what any of it was worth, has been trying to work it out for about an hour and a half, has not got a number and is not going to make one up. **Her finding, eleven years long, arrived in about nine seconds on a landing: she is the best reader of documents in four counties and she stood on a landing with a name in her mouth, and the whole of what she is good at was worth nothing, and the only thing that worked is the one thing she has never been trained for, and she is not going to call that a lesson and is not going to feel good about it.** In the third column, not the fourth, because it is a thing somebody said: the same sentence she has now heard three times in about five days, twice in a market and once on this landing, said in the open to another woman, by a woman who was not talking to her and not talking to a clerk, **and there is no mark and no form and no hour against it anywhere in this kingdom.** **She rang nothing. Nobody asked her anything. Nobody held anything over her. There is no speech and nobody has said it was worth it and she is not going to.**

## Chapter 0087 — A Name On A Roll Four Counties Away

Day 73, **the seventh hour of the morning** to **the eighth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a yard behind a draper's shop, a street, a counter in Miremarket's clerk's hall, a step. He wants a place and there is no such thing in this town. **Tamsin Rook gives him the town name** at the end of the yard, out of a market six days before: **Ashlade, four counties up**, said at a fish pitch to a woman in a line, that the line has not been answered this year or the year before, that there is nobody at that house now to answer it with, and that it has gone about four years without a hand. **She writes it in the third column, rules it off, and says the fourth column is for a thing nobody could answer, that she has had one in it and is not going to have two and it is not going to be a habit, and that she has not written the name down to find a place — she wrote it down because a woman said it in a market for nothing and she did not want to lose it.** He counts four bolts, makes the frame fast, and goes. **The resistance is not the door:** the door is open, the notice is nine feet inside, and he has used the notice once and got three sentences in three hands. **What he cannot do is ask, because he has asked — nine times in a street, once properly at a counter, a sheet nobody asked for, a paper on a landing — and he has four people in this town who are all right and none of whom is a door, and he ran out of buildings before he ran out of questions on the sixty-seventh and it has stopped being a discovery.** **Ivo Trant at the step:** *you have not asked me anything in four days and I have noticed and I have decided not to make anything of it*; **you can go in, you can ask, the notice means it; what you cannot do is stand here and be looked at, because a woman at the cross has been looking at him since he came round the corner and has told two people, and by the fourth hour of the afternoon this will be the third person, and in about four days it will be a thing a stranger knows about before he has opened his mouth — and nobody thinks of it, which is why the notice is the only thing in that building anybody has ever used and he has never had to defend it.** At the counter, with a chair at the wrong side of it, he asks one question and says out loud what he is not asking. **A roll in this hall is a book of answers, and it is this district's answers; a line is a line in this district or it is not a line in this hall; Ashlade is four counties up with about eleven hundred people between and there is no form that crosses eleven hundred people and there never has been; the line is on a shelf in Ashlade in a hall with different men in it and he has never seen it and will not guess at it in front of anybody, because a guess said out loud in that building is a guess somebody repeats in a fortnight.** Then the other one, unasked: **the names have to go somewhere, because a number against a line has to mean something to somebody, and if it meant nothing it would be a mark, and a mark is cheaper and he has never in four years seen a mark where there was a number — and the names go up. The answers are ours; the names are not. The answers come in on paper from a round, a clerk enters them, a clerk closes them, and the hall holds about nine hundred lines a year and about forty pounds of wages and nobody has ever asked him about the arithmetic and he has offered it to about four people. Up. To the district. And he has never been upstairs.** **The district is always shut; there is no district in that hall and there has not been in four years; what an officer does with a number between the first of a round and the last of it is between her and a building over a dyer's, and there is no form that says, and he has looked for one at three different hours on two different days and there is not one and he did not think that was anybody's secret, he thought it was that nobody has had to write it down.** He asks the sentence — **what is the document** — and the clerk answers it in the office's voice, the same sentence, faster than last time, and then says he thought about it for four days and **decided he was wrong to save the ink**, because a clerk with an office's position in his hand and no line in the office's column is keeping a thing in his head, and he has read about four hundred sheets off that counter and has never once been able to say which were in a man's head. He writes it standing up on the hall's own paper with the hour, signs it, gives it across. **No second copy; Tomas did not ask and Pethick would not offer, and he says he would like it noticed that the first time Tomas came in he asked for a copy of a line and the answer was correct then and is correct now and he has never once wished it were different.** Then the thing that changes the morning: **has anybody ever asked you that. No. And nobody has asked you what the number means. No.** **The first no meant *nobody has ever asked me* and is a thing a building says about itself. The second no meant *nobody has ever asked me, and now you have, and I know what I am going to do, and I am going to do nothing*, and it is the same word and not the same sentence.** Pethick: **I am going to do nothing, and I am right, and I would like it written down that I was asked and that I said no, and I am not writing it down, because a clerk's name in a book is the only thing in the room that can be used against him, and I have one child and a house and eleven years in this hall.** He goes out at about the sixth hour into a street where a man at a stall says *upstairs* twice to a woman. **The finding, written at about the eighth hour of the evening, first and third columns and four lines in the second:** **the sentence was never a door; he carried it thirty-three days and put it in four buildings, and a clerk said it the second time in nine seconds standing up and went back to a column and has not thought about it since; it is a true sentence about a document and that is the whole of what it is; and a man with a barrow in this town has said he has been in a clerk's hall and been told what a document is, and about four hundred people in this county will hear that by the end of the week and every one of them will hear it as a key. And the harder half: the habit is his and not theirs — he has been blaming four counties for not being a door and they were never doors, and a sentence in nine seconds is not a door, and a man with a barrow and a month of nothing to carry will make a door out of anything that is true and holds still. And the habit is a column and a stamp: he went and got a sheet of paper with a position on it in a clerk's hand with the hour against it, and it is the same shape as nine hundred lines a year with a stamp at the foot, and his has his name at the head of it, and the reason it does is that a sheet with a person's own name on it is a thing a person is read by, and he has spent thirty-three days learning what happens to a person a thing is read by.** And Ashlade: **the name is not in this building and never was; a name is not a place; a boy of eight taken six days before the day any of this started is a name in about nine thousand places in four counties, and there is no book of it anywhere in this kingdom and there never has been and there is no form for one; and the only thing that has ever gone to a place to find out whether a person is in it is a person on foot with a bag.** **He is not angry, he has been unable to be angry at anything in this town for about ten days, he has gone looking three times, and there is nothing here to be angry at: a clerk who is right, a man in charge of shelves who is right, a district officer who was right on a landing and is poorer for the asking, a woman at a fish pitch who gave him a town for nothing, and about eleven thousand of us doing nine minutes a year each, and not one of them is an agent and not one has ever been punished and there is nobody in the whole of it.** Nothing was rung, four bolts, all five clauses stand, and the fifth was not used today because he did not need to.

## Chapter 0088 — What He Says In A House Of Five

Day 74, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour, bare**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the gable with the lean-to at the end of the east lane, a kitchen, the cross in the dark. He goes with a thing to say and **does not have the book, has never carried it, and did not ask to**, and he knew that at the end of the lane and at the cross and about four hundred yards back and went anyway. **Bess Marrow** opens the door before he gets to it. *You have not brought it.* *No.* **Good — I want you to hear me say that properly, because I have thought about this for fifteen days and I have had it ready: good.** She told a woman with a slate on the fifty-ninth that the day that book leaves this house it stops being the book of her house and starts being evidence about her house, and she has not moved from that. **He does not ask her the three questions, says he has known them since the fifty-ninth, and gives her the reason when she asks: the woman who asked them is better at it than he is and is not here, and she has already answered them and he has read the answers, and he is not going to come into a kitchen and do a trade he does not know in front of a woman who cannot write and a fire — and because she was asked by him once, in a row, in a market, three days after saying no, by a man with a barrow, and Dory Larkin told him what that is worth.** She calls it **the first true thing anybody has said to me in this lane and the first time in about four years that anybody in this town has said they were not going to do a thing, and it has taken you fifteen days and a barrow.** Then the reason she has had ready for nine days: **the only thing in this county that can act on a book is a household; a clerk can certify a column, a man in a chair can stop a practice, a woman with a slate can write down what a person said; and not one of the three can go and stand in a room and say no to anything, because a person is the only thing that can be asked, and I am a person, and you have come to my door to use it.** He agrees with her mid-sentence. **The harder resistance, and it is the whole of six weeks coming back at him out of a door: he has spent six weeks telling four correct people that a person is not a shelf, and then he walked up a lane and put his hands on a person and asked her to hold a book, and she says it perfectly level — you have come to my door to ask me to be a place where your thing is kept — and she made him have it in his own mouth first, because if he had said it for her she should have shut the door and they would both have been right and she would not have got anything.** And then the thing he came to say: **he does not ask her to take the book anywhere, not to the hall, not to Fourmile, not to a room over a draper's, not out of the house for one hour; he does not ask her to show it to anybody; he does not ask her mother to look at it, and he says out loud that she cannot write, which he has never once said in a room.** He asks one question about a thing that does not exist: **if there was a line in your own name, or the house's name, or nobody's name, with a mark in a column and a day and a stamp, and nobody came to your door and nobody told you what it was and nobody has ever asked you.** *I would answer it.* **I am not frightened of a line. I have four of them in a drawer that have been open four years and I have never gone up the road and slept. I am frightened of a cart, and a cart only comes for a line that is not answered, and so I would answer it and I would be quit of it.** *Then you have come to my door to tell me the only thing I have.* **Yes. And she is right, and he walked four counties to tell a woman at the end of a lane something she worked out on the fourth day by herself, and he has nothing to carry out of the lane.** Then she asks him for **the whole of it**, in her own words, in a room, before the first question, because if she is going to be the household that is asked she is going to be asked with the whole of the thing on the table and not the half a man has decided she can carry — and because **her mother cannot write and her two brothers and her sister have not been in this kitchen in four years and are not going to be told a thing in a room by her on a working day, and he shall tell her if he does not know.** He says it. **An hour and ten minutes.** The round that came on the sixtieth and went on the sixty-third and shut at the end of the sixty-sixth with four pitches left; the notice on the wall and the nine seconds and the twenty-two years; the three men in the hall and their three answers; the district officer and the paper she gave him on a landing two days ago and that she is poorer for it since the sixtieth; the market of about nine hundred; the row, the cart on the sixty-third, the cart on the sixty-sixth, four shillings, two shillings and sixpence, four pounds a quarter, a mother on a chair who can say a number; **and the room — the shilling for the fire and about nine people who came because a door was open, and the sentence he said in it in his own name about a household he had no standing in, and the clerk with a coat on who had four seconds, and the cart that came at about the ninth hour of the evening because a line in dispute is a working day for a clerk and it comes on a day nobody chooses, and the market keeper who stood up at the back and said *then that is three*.** **He did not build a case, did not denounce anything, did not say it was necessary, and nobody in that kitchen said it was necessary.** She goes through it: **the nine pitches are off the market; no cart will come back for them; the practice has been stopped in this district in writing by a woman with a number on a piece of paper; and neither of those two things puts a pitch back at the end of a row. And the four pounds a quarter does not come back and the twenty years do not come back and the butter that was bought on credit against a pitch for nine years does not come back.** He: **I am not going to be the man who tells you it was worth it, and nobody is going to come and tell you it was, and if one does I will write down that he said it and the hour.** Then the cost, in her own words: **you have given me the whole of it and it is in this kitchen and there is nobody here who can keep a book; I have a book in a drawer that a man from another county wants to take to a building, a slate's two answers, a market of about nine hundred people and nine pitches off it, and a line of my own open four years; and every single thing that has come to this door in four years has come because somebody wanted something in it, and I have never once had anything brought to me that I did not have to be careful of afterwards; so I am going to be careful of this one as well, because a thing that is in my head and not written down is a thing I carry on my own and I am the only one in this house who can read.** And: **you are going to leave all of it here. And the reason I know you are not going to take it away is that a man who has a book in a coat does not stand in a kitchen for an hour and ten minutes and read it out to a woman who cannot write. You have told me things tonight that you did not have to tell me and about four of them have cost you something, and I have been in this lane fifteen days waiting to find out what you were after and I have got it and it is nothing you can have.** She shuts the door on about the seventh hour. The day-book at about the ninth hour, at the cross, in the dark, **written straight across without ruling anything because there was nothing in it that belonged in one column and nothing in it that belonged in another**: **the way to protect a household is to be left alone by a man with a book; he had that on the fiftieth and did not know he had it, and she is the daughter of a woman who cannot write and a woman who has been asked nine times in four years, and she is right, and he is the man with the book, and he is going to have to work out what he is instead and he has not got it.** And **the only power a household has is the power to answer, and answering is what closes a line, and a closed line is the one thing in this kingdom that stops a cart — the first thing in six weeks a person has handed to him instead of the other way round, and it is not a stage and not a power and he cannot do it for anybody.** And: **he did not ask her the three questions, she asked him why not, he gave her a true answer, and it is the first true thing anybody in that lane has had off a stranger in about four years and he has no use for it whatever.**

## Chapter 0089 — The Inheritance That Cannot Be Claimed

Day 75, **the seventh hour of the morning** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a gate on the water at the west end of Miremarket, a yard, a counting house with a long table in it, a step. He finds it because of the arithmetic. A man comes out of a counting-house door carrying a book, stops, goes back in, shuts the door, stands behind it about nine seconds, comes out again and shuts it properly, and says **four months**. **The house was Vey and Lock**, timber and freight off the water, three boats in a season, about nine men on the books, a yard with a slip in it; the senior partner **Marden Vey**, three other partners, and the counting-house clerk **Sander Fisk**, about nine years in that room. **Ivor Salter died in the winter and left about three hundred pounds, and six households in this town have a claim on it totalling about fifty-one pounds, and the money has been in that room for four months and will be there at the end of the year**, and Fisk tells it at a gate in about four minutes to a man with a barrow who has not asked him anything, **because Mr Vey is forty miles up the water and is coming back this morning on the carrier and he would rather have said it at a gate to a stranger than in a room with a door shut.** **A claim has to be shown, not proved, and a hand against a line is the only record in this kingdom that a person stood in front of somebody and said something.** Salter kept a yard-book for thirty-one years and wrote down against a day the name of any person who stood in his yard and asked him to put a thing in: **six households have a hand in it, fourteen are dead, and about nine thousand people in four counties have never heard of it, because it is a book in a yard and not a form in a hall, and he never thought of it as anything.** **And the bag cannot move, because a house may hold money for a dead man only if the house can show its own answering — you must be able to show a hand of your own before you may be entered in a book as handling a hand of somebody else's — and this firm's line is in the name of the trade and not the name of a man and has not been answered since the spring before last.** Fisk: **nobody has ever asked me why, and I have looked twice, and I am not going to look a third time unless somebody makes me; a line in a trade's name is not a line in a man's name; nobody in this firm has ever agreed what the mark means; the practice in that hall is printed and is an approving return from a district office and it is a good paper, and it says that where a household cannot make up its mind one hand is put against the line and the line is treated as answered; there are four of us and we cannot make up our minds, so a hand ought to go down about twice a year, and for four years not one has, and there is no way to make one go down that I have found, and I am not a partner and it is not my line; and you are the first person from outside this yard to ask me a question about it in nine years, and Mr Vey asked me the same question in the spring and I gave him the same answer and he did not put a hand down either.** **The leash, and it is the seventh use in this novel and the fourth time she has refused him in a month:** he asks her to be in the counting room. **No, in one word, and then a reason that is hers: a woman with a slate goes into a timber merchant's counting room in this town and by the fourth hour of the afternoon there is not one person in Miremarket who does not know it, and by the end of the week the four families that owe that firm money will know it and will not be told why — they will be told that there is a woman in it — and a firm four generations in this town does not get a quarter back for a quarter year because a woman was seen going through a door.** **He did not ask again and wrote that night that she was right for the seventh time in a month and that he is the one who keeps finding out.** **So he went in alone, and he could not read a hand**, and Fisk read him a line and he could not tell whose hand it was for about a minute and a half and did not say so for twenty minutes: **that is not a thing a man can do; I have been in this room nine years and I could not tell you whose hand that is either and I have never told anybody, and the reason is that if I cannot tell you then nobody can, and if nobody can then it does not matter, and I do not know which of those is true and I have not gone and found out.** **Marden Vey came back on the morning of the seventy-fifth and was in the counting house from about the first hour of the afternoon until about the ninth hour of the evening, which is longer than his business is.** He is not angry and says so before anybody has said anything: **he has been angry about this for four years and it has cost him four families and a yard and about a third of the carrying, and he has got the anger down to about the size of a bad morning at about two in the morning in this yard by himself, and he would have taken help if it had been offered and it was not.** The four figures, unasked: **about ninety pounds, about forty, about eleven and four, and about six.** And the second thing: **nobody is going to lend to us. A house that cannot show its own answering is a house that cannot be entered in a book as handling anybody's money, and a man with money in a town of two thousand will not lend it to us and says why in one sentence and is right in the sentence. I have tried it four times in four months and been told no four times and the reason has been the same four times and it has never once been said unkindly.** And the mark: **the mark is not what is ruining me. A mark in a column is a mark in a column and there are about eleven thousand a year in this county and none of them is a secret and none has ever cost anybody a night's sleep that I know of. What is ruining me is that the line has been open four years, and the line being open is not the mark, it is the four of us not putting a hand down, and I have known that since the spring and I have not done it.** Then the best sentence in the batch, said once, standing up, in a counting house, in his own name, about his own trade, **having said first that he has read the practice, that it is a good paper, that it is right, and that he has never been able to improve it or wanted to**: **a claim cannot be shown, and a claim that cannot be shown cannot be paid, and that is not a hardship, and I want it written down that I said that and nobody is to think I do not mean it. That rule is the reason Ivor Salter has not been robbed. He has been dead four months and there are about nine hundred people in this town who could each remember a thing he said at a gate, and a good many of them could say it better than the six who have it in his book, because a gate is where people say the true things. And not one of those nine hundred can be paid, and the reason is that this firm cannot be entered in a book, and the reason for that is four men who have never once discussed a mark in a column in eleven years. I am not going to have that in my head for a fortnight while we are open. If I bend it for six households today then I have bent it, and the next man to want it will say a merchant of this town did it for six households and it is only fair, and in about four years there will be nine hundred claims in this county that are all fair. So the six households wait, and they are owed about fifty-one pounds and they have waited four months and they will wait until the end of the year, and I am not going to tell them I cannot help them, because I can help them, in about nine seconds, by writing something down. And I am not going to. And I would like it noticed that the thing I am refusing to do is a small thing and that I am refusing it on purpose, and that nobody in this room is going to persuade me, and that I am not a villain about it and I am not frightened of it, I am just a man who has worked out what a thing is for.** He said it once and would not say it again in a different room to a different man, and **Tomas Vale wrote it down in his own book at about the ninth hour of the evening, and writing it down is not the same as having it entered anywhere, and he wrote that under it and it is the only line in the entry that is about him.** Then the mark: **the mark is not what is ruining me; the mark was put in a building about forty years ago by a man whose name I do not know, who is dead, and who was doing his job on a morning, and there was nothing anybody could do about it then and there is nothing anybody could do about it now; I have looked for the man, I asked a man in a hall in this town in the spring and he was about four years in the building and was not there then and was not there when I asked, and that is the whole of the hunt and it took me an afternoon.** And then the shape of the whole volume, said as though it were freight: **four partners, two of them nephews, one in the yard since he was a boy, and every one of the four of us reads that open line as a debt. Not as a chore. As a debt. A debt is owed to somebody, and the somebody is a hall in this town, and the four of us have looked at that line for four years and every one of us has thought, in his own words, and we have said it to each other at about nine separate times: that is the firm's, and it is in arrears, and it will want paying for, and it is a thing the firm is in for, and it is not a thing I am in for. And there is nobody in this firm to tell any of us that it is the other thing. Because it is not a debt, it is a chore, and a chore is a thing you do for yourself in your own yard on your own day, and every one of the four of us would do it for ourselves in about nine seconds, and not one of the four of us will do it for the firm, because the firm is not us. It never has been. We have been a firm for thirty-one years and we have never once in thirty-one years been able to tell each other that the firm is us. And that is the whole of my ruin and it is about eighteen words long and it is not in a hall and it is not on any paper and there is no column for it and no clerk can enter it, and you cannot certify it, and nobody is going to stop me saying it in about four years when I am not in this yard any more.** The day-book, all four columns, nothing merged: **she has refused him seven times in a month, not one of the seven cost her anything, and every one of the seven cost him, and he is not going to be grateful to her for it because she would not want him to be. A name on a roll carries every person who has ever been in the trade; a line is answered by one hand and the hand belongs to whoever is at the counter; a firm that cannot show that its line was answered cannot be entered in a book as holding anything for anybody else; and therefore a man dead four months has not been robbed and six households are owed about fifty-one pounds and none of them can be paid, and the whole reason is that four men in a yard have never once told each other that a firm is them — and that is the engine of this business and it is in a counting house and not in a hall, and it is the same shape as a name becoming a duty in a queue, and nobody has to stand in a lane to ring it in either case.** The cost, named: **about ninety pounds, about forty, about eleven and four and about six owed to him by four families, a third of the carrying, a slip, four refusals in four months, about fifty-one pounds owed to six households that will wait to the end of the year, and a yard with three boats in it in a season. Nobody in the figures is wicked and he has looked at all of them.** And the man he came into the town for: **he came into this town wanting a person to be angry at who was not kind to him, and he has got a merchant who is owed money, who is right, who is going to be ruined, and who said the best thing anybody has said to him in six weeks on his own trade, and it is true, and he cannot get at it, and the man who put the mark in the column is dead or retired or asleep, and there is nobody to be angry at at all, and he has been unable to be angry at anything in this town for ten days and this is the tenth day and he has stopped looking.** And what is coming, written down because it is the last day he can write anything down without it being a plan: **when this comes out, six households find out that a mark in a column is why they have not been paid, and a firm of nine people finds out that the same mark is why nobody will lend to it. Those two things point in the same direction, and they are about a hundred yards apart, and they do not know about each other, and there is nobody in this county who could put them together except a man with no standing and thirteen documents who has been asked not to.** Nothing was rung, four bolts, the crate untouched, all five clauses standing, and the fifth written at the gate at about the seventh hour of the morning because he has no standing to ask a merchant a question about a claim in a dead man's name.

## Chapter 0090 — A List, And A Thing Nobody Could Answer

Day 76, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the eleventh hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the room over the draper's shop, a market of about nine hundred, a stone landing, the room. He begins by ruling the four columns and then ruling them again because the first lot was not straight, and then he stands at the window for about ten minutes. **There is no list.** Not a discovery — worked out on the seventy-third and not got round to. **A name is in about nine thousand kitchens in four counties and in about nine thousand heads and there is nowhere at all that it is written down all together, and there never has been, and the whole of the arrangement is about nine minutes a year each.** He had been about to write it out, on a slate, in four columns, with the hours. **He does the sum in the margin of the second column:** about eleven thousand lines a year across this district, four years is about forty-four thousand, to find one out he has to go to the house and ask, asking costs a question and a door and a day, there are four counties, he is one man, and a barrow will not go at the speed of a question. **And he did nine** — not in a month: **in one day, in a market of about nine hundred people, on the seventieth, standing in a line behind a man with onions, and nine people said a name out loud to another person in about four hours and every one of them did it for nothing and not one of them knew they had done it.** Forty-four thousand at nine a day is a figure he wrote down and struck out and then wrote down again, **because he wanted to see it twice, and a man who strikes out a sum is a man who does not like the answer and not a man who does not believe it. And the other half: it is being made about eleven thousand a year faster than anybody is unrolling it, and there is nobody unrolling it, and there has never been anybody unrolling it, and the reason there is not is that it does not need unrolling. It is a mouth and it is open and it is not owed to anybody and nothing is asked of it and it costs about nine minutes a year each and it works.** He goes down to the market and stands at the top of it for about two hours and **asks nobody anything**, and writes that as a piece of work and not a failure and says he does not yet know whether it is one and has nobody to tell him. He watches three of the nine work, though he does not know they are three of the nine, and he could not put a county to two names and could not be certain of a first name for one, **and that is what about two hours in a market will do to a list.** He sees **Nan Pyle** at a pitch of needles and thread with a line of six, saying nothing to anybody. **Joss Munn** with a barrow of onions, talking about the price of a thing and not about a line, and who has no idea a man with a slate ever wrote a word he said near her. **And a woman at a fish pitch says a name — he catches about two words and the day — and the whole exchange takes about eleven seconds, and she says the name was not hers, it was the name of the house, and the house owed it and she was owed by it, and that she had said so at a fish pitch before and would say so again and did not mind who heard. And he did not write it down, because she had said it to a stranger and not to him, and because the moment he writes a name that a person has said in a market on a slate, that name is a thing somebody may read, and there are four columns in this world and he knows all four of them and he has not got a fifth.** He writes the sum instead: *eleven seconds*, *a queue of four*, *nobody asked*. **On the stone landing at about the ninth hour she is sitting on the bollard with her boots off and her feet in the water**, which she does and has done for eleven years and which he has never once commented on. He asks her to read it back. **That is my line and not yours.** *It is the whole thing and it is the fourth column, and I have never read a fourth column back to anybody in my life because there has only ever been one and it is about a paper and a building, and this one is about nine people, and I want it read back by the only person in this county who has read eleven thousand things back to the people who said them.* She reads all of it standing up, four minutes, nine lines, no stops and no speed-ups, and does not hand it back. **Nine.** He tells her the part after: **I am writing down a list of nine people who have said a name out loud in a market and did not know they had done it, and every one of those nine was told, by somebody, in their own words, that the name was theirs.** She hands it back: **then there are two lists and you have written one, and the reason there are two is that on the seventieth I stood ten feet off a woman at a pitch of needles and thread and said a sentence back to her in her own words, out loud, so that about nine people heard it.** *And that is not the same act as a woman saying her neighbour's name in a line.* *It is not.* **No. It is the opposite act and it took eleven years and I have never once had it named and I have written about four hundred things in four columns in this kingdom and nobody has ever asked me what it is for.** He tells her it is a thing that could be answered and the fourth column is for a thing nobody could answer, so it goes in the first one. She refuses to say it twice, **because she has said it to him about four things this month and is not going to make a habit of it in a county where she cannot hear half of what anybody says anyway**, and **takes the chalk off him, which she has done once, at a landing, about a year ago, and writes one word in the ninth line, and gives it back, and does not explain it.** The ninth line had read that a courier with a slate **said a name** out loud in the ordinary way in a market, at ten feet, so that about nine people heard it, and who has never once been asked what it was for. It now reads that she **said a sentence** out loud. He makes the change, writes the line out clean with the hours flat against it, and then does the thing his method actually is, in about four seconds, without enjoying it: **he rules the ninth line off from the other eight, moves it down the page, puts it in the third column, does not merge it, and writes the hour against it — because the third column is for words a person has said in their own mouth, and a name is not a thing a person says, it is a thing a person owes, and a sentence is a thing a person says, and that is the entire difference, and it has been in his hand for six days and it was handed back to him at the ninth hour of the evening by the only person it belonged to.** He counts the third time a person has read a thing back to him and changed one word — a woman in a market twelve days ago, a woman in a kitchen two days ago, and this one — and says **nobody in this county has ever once heard of a thing being got right by a person changing one word of it, and I have never once seen it happen in a building.** And she, putting her boots on: **it does not happen in a building because in a building a word costs something**, and that is the whole of what she says about it and she has not improved it since.
