The last entry, at about the eleventh hour of the evening, in the room, all four columns, nothing merged, the longest thing he has written in six weeks and the plainest. **The discovery of consent that the whole volume turns on, in the third column, because it is a thing a person said to him on a landing four days ago and not a thing he found out: an answer is a person and may be withdrawn; a duty is a mouth and cannot; the roll does not need anybody's answer, and the list does not need anybody's answer either; and it did not happen in a hall and it did not happen to an office — it happened in a queue, at a pitch, in about eleven seconds, for nothing, by a person who is owed nothing and is not an agent of anything and is usually right about who she is.** Then the hardest paragraph: **I have been building a method for six weeks and it is not a way of protecting anybody; it is a way of making certain that there is a document. I have been careful all my life and I thought careful was a habit about hands and about ink and about a person. It is not. It is a way of writing things down, and I found that out by writing nine people down, and the reason I could not stop is that a list of nine people is a list of nine people who are safe — they said a name in a queue and went home and forgot it; if it is on a slate in a room over a draper's shop then it is not forgotten, and then somebody may read it, and then it is a fact about them, and it is a fact about them because I wrote it, and I did not ask them. And a record is a sentence other people may read. I had that on the sixty-sixth standing against a wall and I thought it was a discovery about a clerk. It is a discovery about me. There is one kind of sentence in this world and it is the one that can be read afterwards, and I have been manufacturing them for six weeks because a man who has nothing to carry makes a document, and a document is the only thing he can be certain of.** And the second thing, which he cannot put down cleanly: **I said one true sentence in a room in this town seven days ago and it cost nine pitches and about thirty-six pounds a quarter and twenty years and nine years and a handcart, and it cost a household its front of a row, and the sentence was true, and nobody has been forgiven for anything and nobody has apologised for anything and I am not going to be told it was worth it. And I am about to write a list that will make it possible for a person to say a second one. I know that. I have known it since about the sixth hour of the afternoon and I have not been able to put the slate down, and I have written the nine down anyway, and about nine people in this town are not safe because of a man who cannot stop writing. A list of nine names is nine more mouths and I am making them and I am going to go on making them, because I do not have another thing, and I am writing it down here at the eleventh hour of the evening so that there is one place in this book where it is written that I knew and did it, and if anybody ever asks me what I was doing in this town since the fifty-seventh that is the sentence they get and not the other ones. I am not going to call it necessary. Nobody is going to come and tell me it was necessary. And I am not going to be a man who has only been a door.** Third column, ruled apart: **a woman at a fish pitch said a name and said it was the house's, in about eleven seconds, to a stranger, and I did not write it down — and a woman in a doorway said a hand against a line is the answer to a question, and I wrote it down, and it is a true sentence and it is in a book in a house and not in a hall and no clerk will ever read it, and that is the only good thing that has happened to me in six weeks.** Fourth column, one line, the second time in his life: **the list cannot be unrolled; it is in the memory of about eleven thousand people and it was made in about nine minutes a year and it can be walked out of, house by house, by one person on foot, in about four years, and it will take longer than that, and it is being made about eleven thousand a year faster than anybody is unrolling it, and there is nobody unrolling it, and there is no form for a list and there never has been, and I have not got a place to take a slate to and I am not going to look for one, because a slate that goes into a building is a column and a stamp and I know what those are and I have had thirty-three days of learning what a stamp is and it is not me.** And the last of it, flat and not improved: **a column a clerk can certify and a list that cannot be certified at all, and a name on a roll that begins, in about four years, to be a duty.** Nothing was rung in this town on the seventy-sixth and nothing has been rung anywhere since the fifty-third in another county; four bolts; no clapper; a receipt that forbids anybody to furnish one; the crate in a smith's yard with the sacking off and no claim of any kind in it and **no hand on it in twenty-three days**; **all five clauses stand, and he wrote the fifth one twice this week, once at a counter and once at a gate, and both times it cost him a question and both times it was the correct price.** And a woman who has said nine words on a landing four days ago and has not asked him for anything and is not going to be thanked by him for them, asleep in a room over a draper's shop with a slate under the head of the bed, and he is going to shut this book.

## Chapter 0091 — What A Bell Is In A Market

Day 77, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a room over a draper's shop, a market square of about nine hundred, a step. He writes **five sentences** on the sill with the hours, and reads the first four out to the woman on the end of the bed and does not read the fifth. **The resistance is a preposition: he wrote on the sixty-second *I am not going to ring it in this county at all*, in a room in this town, and *this county* is the county he was standing in, and the bar is in another one, and the sentence binds him in the place he wrote it, and nobody in this county can waive a sentence in a man's own day-book except the man.** He finds the bell in a sack — **fourteen pounds two ounces, a crate in a smith's yard since about the sixth hour of the morning of the fifty-eighth** — and sets out the fifth sentence's problem himself: **she reads it back in the flat voice in about nine seconds and says no.** It is the Larkins' sentence, *we are five and we do not agree, and we are not going to be sent for again, and we mean it, and any hand on that line is one of the five of us and not the house*, it belongs to a household of five who have refused him twice and refused him again on a third visit, and it is the only paper in his coat that is not about a document. **Tamsin Rook: that is not yours; a thing said in a market that a household would have said is a thing said for a household; the fifth clause of that receipt says you have no standing to speak for any household in Kells or in any ward, and you asked a clerk to add it at about the fifth hour of the morning of the thirty-third because you were right, and you were right, and it has cost you the only sentence in this town that anybody wanted to hear.** He rules it through, writes *not said* at the end of the line, does not tear the page out, and **she says nothing at all, which is not something she has done in twenty days.** The barrow goes to the kerb by the cross — **a barrow will not go at the speed of a question, and a frame out of its cradle on a road is a frame broken, and the barrow stands in the square with a frame in it four streets away from where the frame is** — and the market keeper makes him turn the handles round. **He does not write to Rowan Ketch and says why: a man who writes to a master and asks whether he may do a thing is asking a room for a permission, and he would have got a document.** At the top of the hour, in about nine hundred people, **he rings a bell he did not make, once**, and says four sentences: what he did on the sixty-ninth and what it cost nine pitches and that there is no part of it anybody else did; that he could not stop it and could not put the row back and has not said it was worth it; **that the rest of it was done by four people and every one of them was right, and the four are named, and one of the four is a clerk — Elias Nabb, who closed a line in dispute in the correct form out of the correct column in about four seconds;** and that those are the known facts, and here are the unknown ones, *I do not know how an office gets into a bell and I have got a column and a stamp and a habit, and I do not know where a name goes when it is registered and nobody has told me, and every word of these four sentences is about me*. Then about four minutes of silence, **and he does not improve them, the second time in a month and the first was twenty-four days ago.** **And the bell did not do what a bell does: the claim went to the end of the market street and stopped there, the way a voice stops; the booths at the far side heard nothing, the lane to the landing heard nothing, the smith's yard four streets off heard nothing, and about two hundred of nine hundred heard a note.** He does not know whether that is the metal, or the claim, or the crowd, and did not find out and is not going to in this county. **She came to the square with the slate and the four columns and the chalk out and did not write one word on it and gave it back blank, and would not explain it, and would not be thanked.** At about the seventh hour the market keeper tells him the price: **for about four days nobody in that hall will be able to shut a line, because a clerk can be named, and the notice on the wall means what it says, and I am not going to stop defending that notice because it is inconvenient.** Tomas's answer: **he put the name in on purpose, because if he does not then in about four days this town will have decided that the man with a book took a row off, or the district officer sent a cart, or the man in charge of the shelves did it out of spite, and two of those three are right and neither of them did it.** The day-book at about the ninth hour of the evening, first and third columns, second and fourth left: **a claim in metal is a thing a man cannot edit, and it is the first instrument he has ever had that is not a document, and there is nobody in any building who can ask to see it**; **and the fifth sentence is the only part anybody wanted and he is not allowed to say it and the reason is a clause he asked for because he was right**; **and he has never learned where a warning lands and is not going to be told it as a favour**; nothing else rung, four bolts, no clapper, five clauses standing, a bell in a sack in a room over a draper's shop, and a woman ten feet off with a blank slate.

## Chapter 0092 — The Officer Who Cannot Produce A Document

Day 78, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a room over a draper's shop, a bottom step, an outside stair. He writes to a district's two rooms over a dyer's: **not a person, not a name, one question about the work — there is a household of five at the end of the east lane with four lines open about four years and nobody has ever told that household what the question was; what question does the office put to a household, in the words the office uses.** He hands it to the woman who lets the rooms, who will put it in the district's box on the wall by the water butt and who tells him for the third time that he is not to thank her and that she keeps about nine hundred names in a book and has never been asked what it is. The answer comes back at about the sixth hour in a hand he does not know, with the hour, and says four things: **there is no document, because the question is not one of the four fields on the form and there has never been a place on any form in that office for the words the office says at a door**; the only record of a question in this kingdom is the answer; **a person may come and ask the officer in a room in words and this is the only way it can be had and it cannot be put in a form afterwards, because a form is about a household and a man in a room is a person, and a person is the only thing that can be asked a question and can refuse to answer it, and that is the whole reason a hall cannot be asked anything**; and, at the foot, **come to a room over a chandler's shop four doors down from the draper's, this evening — Cress Naylor, of the removals. I am not the district.** **He had been in this town twenty-one days and there was a Crown officer four doors down and he had not known, and the reason he had not known is that he had gone into a clerk's hall, a district office, a room over a dyer's, a yard, a counting house, a gate and a smith's yard and had not once walked up his own street.** Naylor comes at about the sixth hour and knocks, and asks him the question before he can put it: **what is the document**, in about two seconds, and Tomas has to put a hand on the door frame. Naylor's version is not the clerks' version: **a line is not a document, a line is a place in a book where a thing is entered, and I enter about nine hundred of them a year in removals, and I do not ask why anybody has gone and I am not permitted to ask and there is no box for it; and the reason you cannot have what you asked for is not that the office will not give it, it is that there is nothing to give, because the question was in a man's mouth for about nine seconds and the only trace of it in this kingdom is the hand that went down afterwards, and a hand is the answer and has never been anything else.** And then he says his part: **in the winter he closed a line, a household of four in a street off the market, the man went up the water four months before he came to a place in another district to a trade and wrote once; he came to the door at about the ninth hour of the evening with the book out and the woman told him before he had it open that he had gone in the autumn and nobody had heard since and she had told the last man twice; and he closed the line in the correct column with a mark, and she did not ask him a question and he did not ask her one, because there is no box for it and he did not want to know.** **That is my part in it. A man left this town and the only record in this kingdom that he was anywhere at all is a mark in a column, and a shut line is a man who has gone and answered, and so he is not lost. He is answered.** The name is in a book four counties up and Naylor will not say it in a room over a draper's shop, and Tomas does not learn it and does not ask. And Naylor adds: **nobody has looked for him, and I want it noticed that I did not do that to him — I closed a line that had been open, which is the only thing there is to do with a line that is open, and I was the fifth man at that door in four years and the smallest of the five.** Tomas asks whether he wants it written down. **No** — and the reason is not caution and not his office: **a thing that is written down goes somewhere, and I do not know where it goes, and neither does the district, and I have been eleven years in this district and I have never found the standing entry.** **Tomas Vale writes it down.** All of it, the street off the market, the hour of the evening, the winter, the four months, the mark, the four fields with none of them the words. Naylor watches and says nothing while he does it, and then: **there it is, and I could have stopped you and I did not try, and the reason I did not try is that I have asked you a question this evening and you answered it, and a man who has been answered once does not get to say the answer was not wanted. And I will not say it again, not to you and not to anybody, because if I say it a second time then the first time was a performance and it was not one.** At the door: **in eleven years, in four counties, you are the first person who has walked into a room and asked me what a document is.** Tomas says it is a complaint and he caused four seconds of it. **It is a complaint and it is eleven years and you may go and do the other twenty-four counties with it and I do not care.** Tamsin had the slate on her knee with the chalk and **nothing on it**, and nobody asked her why. Tomas says out loud, once, what he has just done: **I have taken a thing a man with a wife and a boy asked me in a doorway not to take, and I have made a page about it, and that is the third time in ten days, and the first one cost a household the front of a row of nine pitches, and nobody has been forgiven and nobody has apologised.** The day-book at about the ninth hour of the evening: **the truth does not arrive as a document**; **a hall is not a person and never was**; the third column holds the man's own words about a shut line and a man who is answered and not lost; **I asked him not to write it and he asked me not to write it down and I wrote it down, and I have now made four records in six weeks about persons who did not ask me for one, and the fifth clause says I have no standing to speak for any household and I have not broken it, and careful is not the reason I have not broken it — I did not break it because I wanted it not to be broken**; the fourth column stays empty in this county; and a clerk works four streets from this room whose name about nine hundred people heard yesterday morning and nobody has told him.

## Chapter 0093 — Fifty-One Pounds

Day 79, **the first hour, bare** (the yard is open at the first hour) to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a gate on the water at the west end of the town, a yard, a counting house, a step. He has the slate and the chalk and the four columns and does not use them, which is the first time in a month. He asks one question at the gate: **is the chore yours.** Marden Vey: **it is a chore and it is mine and it is not the firm's, and it has been mine since about the spring, and I have not done it, and the reason I have not done it is a man with a barrow.** Then he stands at the gate for about a minute and a half while a merchant explains why a man with a barrow is the last reason in the world, and says that the thing that is about to happen did not happen because of him. **And the hand went down. Will Preedy, in that yard since he was a boy, said not one word for the whole of the minute and a half, went through the counting-house door, got the firm's book, wrote a new line under the firm's line in his own name with the day against it, put a hand against it, and wrote his own name at the foot the way a hand is written, standing up, in less time than the talking had, and did not explain it and nobody asked him to.** Nobody in that yard was persuaded by anybody from outside it, and Tomas could not have stopped any part of it and did not try. Vey says four things: **that is a hand of a person of this firm, in that person's own name, against that person's own line, and the practice in that hall says that where anybody cannot make up its mind one hand is put against the line and the line is treated as answered, and it does not say whose hand, and that is a good rule**; **nobody persuaded him, and I have been in this yard thirty-one years and about nine things have been done in it that were not mine and he did not ask and nobody spoke to him**; **and it does not save us**; and the whole of it: **we can show it now, from about the fifth hour this morning, and the four of them are not going to hear about it, because a man who says no to you once is not going to say yes to you in the spring on the strength of a mark in a column, and he would be a fool to, and I would not lend to me either.** The bag goes upstairs at about the seventh hour and comes down and the total of the six claims is about fifty-one pounds, **read out one at a time before it is paid, a thing Marden Vey invented for the morning and said so.** The first is **Ruth Dall**, who stood at that gate in the winter with a man who died before the snow was off and had asked her to put it in and had not wanted to and had asked him twice what it was for; she is paid about nine pounds, counts it at the table, puts it inside her coat, says **two years**, and says it in that room because there is nowhere else to say it, has said it to her own sister twice and been told to be quiet, and asks for it not to be read out. He writes it down and does not read it out. And then Vey says the other thing, unasked, about about nine hundred people in the town who could each remember a thing Ivor Salter said to them at a gate: **they cannot be paid, and that is correct, and it is not a comfort I have invented for a bag of money in a room, and it is four hundred years of a rule doing the only thing it is for, and if I broke it this morning for nine hundred people who remember a gate then in about four years this county would be full of men who say a thing at a gate and come back with it and there would be no end to it and no way to say no to any of them. The six have shown a hand and the rest have shown a memory, and a hand is a record and a memory is a story, and I am not going to be a man who pays stories and I am not going to be a man who says the rest of them are owed it either, because they are not, and because that is the part that would rob him.** The day-book at about the ninth hour of the evening, three attempts: **a chore is a thing a man does for himself, in his own yard, with his own hands, on his own day, and it took four years and a dead man's yard-book and about eighteen words spoken in a counting house by the man it belongs to**; **a firm cannot do a chore and a household can, and a household is a person and a firm is not, and about four hundred years of a rule about dead men's money turns on that and on nothing else**; about fifty-one pounds into six hands; and **the thing I did — I asked one question at a gate and nobody in that yard was persuaded by me and a man who has been in a yard since he was a boy put a hand on a line in his own name and said nothing while he did it, and I was the instrument of one of the nine things that happened in this yard in six weeks and I am not going to pretend to be anything else, and a man with a barrow cannot do a chore for anybody and cannot persuade anybody of anything; the only question a man with a barrow can put to a yard is a question whose answer the man was going to arrive at on his own day anyway**; and the leash was not used, which is a non-use and the second in a month.

## Chapter 0094 — Six Households And Nine People

Day 80, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, a market of about nine hundred, a lane off the market, two pitches, a gate on the water, a stone landing. She has three questions and **does not ask one of them, and that is the whole of the first hour and a half**: a household is five people and a door and about an hour, a town is two thousand people and no door and no hour, and she is not going to start because a man stood on a block in one on the seventy-seventh. She does the one thing she has: **you say a person's own words back to them, out loud, at ten feet, in the ordinary voice, so that about nine other people hear it, and she has written since that this is not the same act as a woman saying her neighbour's name in a line, and that it is the opposite act and nobody has ever asked her what it is for.** **The first** is at about the fourth hour in a queue of about six at a pitch of butter: a woman of about five in the house says the name of her own house flatly to a stranger, that the line is the house's and not hers and has been since before she was married, that she is owed by it and has asked twice and been told the price of a pitch, in about eleven seconds, and does not mind who heard. Tamsin says it back **to the woman, not to the stranger.** And the woman turns round, **and the turning round is the part that does the work and nothing else does.** **The second** is at about the fifth hour in a lane: a man who has four years on a line, who took a second one out of a room like a man carrying a parcel he was not addressed for, and who said *take the kingdom out of it*, and who has not said a question about a mark in a column in four years. **The first time a person says a thing they have said it to a woman with a slate. The second time they have said it in a lane in the open, and a lane in the open is not a woman with a slate, and a thing that has been said in a lane in the open is a thing that happened.** **The third** is at about the sixth hour, back at the pitch of needles and thread — **it had not held**: a thing said back once on the seventieth is a thing said twice on the seventieth, and by the eightieth it was a thing said once again, because the second telling had been to one woman at ten feet and a woman at ten feet is not a place anybody has to keep anything — and the woman's hands go to her board and stay there, and she says *you are going to keep doing that* and Tamsin says **I am going to do it four times today and I have done it twice.** **The fourth** is at about the seventh hour at a gate on the water, and it is to a merchant, and **it is the first time in eleven years she has done it to somebody who is owed money.** She says back, at ten feet, in the ordinary voice, with about nine of his own men behind him, the sentence he said once on the seventy-fifth in a counting house and would not say again in another room to another man. He says *it is said once*. **It is said twice. You said it to a man with a barrow and I was not in the room and I did not ask to be, and I have been carrying it for three days and I have said it back to you at your own gate in front of your own men, and that is twice, and I did it on purpose, and I have not got a number for what it is worth and I am not going to make one up.** He asks the price and she gives it: **about a hundred and fifty pounds, and a third of the carrying, and the four households will hear it, and one of the four is a household of five, and a household of five that hears that a firm cannot show its own answering is a household of five that stops answering its own lines. And I have said it anyway, and I am not going to be thanked for it, and I am not going to be told it was worth it, and I have not got a reason you will like and I am not going to improve one for you. For eleven years I have read people's words back to them and it has never once cost anybody anything, because the people I read them back to were people who owed nothing.** And then she writes it down, **which she has not done in eleven years**, on the back of a slate bought for a penny and a half, the price side on the other face and the other way up, and **under the four columns and not in one, because the four columns are ruled and the rule is that a column is a thing somebody can certify and this is not a thing anybody can certify, and she has one line in her fourth column and she has not improved it and it is not on the back of this slate.** Her own statement of the whole: **a name said in a queue in one street and a name said in a counting house in another street are the same kind of act, and the reason is that both are a person telling a person a thing they have not been told, in the ordinary voice, and both take about nine minutes**; and the difficulty, which she has had since the fourth hour: **the six households and the nine men are about a hundred yards apart and neither is going to the other, and there is no room in this town big enough for both, and I could not be in both and I was not going to try, and I am the only person in this county who has a hand in both, and I am one woman with a slate and a deaf ear and no standing, and nobody has asked me to be in either place and I went to both**; and the price, in her own hand: **a thing said twice to somebody who owes nothing is free, and a thing said twice to a man who is owed a hundred and fifty pounds in a town of two thousand is a document about four households, and I have made it, and four of them have not been asked, and it is fourpence a day and I have never found a price on a sentence in a lane.** She asked nobody anything, nobody asked her for a reason, she rang nothing, nothing was merged, and the fourth column has one line in it.

## Chapter 0095 — The Room, The Bench, And The Copies

Day 81, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the eighth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a room over a draper's shop, a street, a yard. Two of them come up the outside stair: **an officer of this county called Curtis Ilsley** with a form folded in his coat, and **Ivo Trant, in charge of the shelves of a clerk's hall, who had not been asked to come and came, and who says so before they are inside, and who adds that he would be ashamed of the pairing.** Ilsley's three things: **this is not a seizure of the room and there is nothing in it about a room; it is not about the frame in the yard and I have not asked for the frame and I am not going to; it is about papers, and every paper on the list is a copy of a sheet belonging to his Majesty's service, and a copy of such a sheet made in a room is that office's from the moment it is made, and holding it is not a thing that any person may do, and there is no argument in it and it is about four hundred years old.** The ground is **a district instruction in the district's own hand, signed, of a day in the winter, folded into the form**, and Tomas reads it and the signature is **Pell Rill's** — *I do not know him. I have his name on a form. That is the whole of what I have got and it is enough, and I have got four more of these before the ninth hour and two of them are in this town.* And the officer notes that the sheet in his own coat is Rill's original and not a copy and is not on the list and that he would have taken it in about four seconds if it had been. Then the list, read out slowly, and it is five things: **the carbon of a form of the second series, entered at a district office in another county, with a reading-out on the reverse in a private hand — the carbon only, the office holds the original**; a fair copy of the hundred and ninetieth refusal in the book of refusals; a fair copy of the approving practice; a fair copy of the head of a round's schedule, the ruling and the last rule; and **a fair copy of one line out of an answering column, a household in the east row, taken on the sixty-sixth from a line shown at a counter.** He does not build a case, does not denounce anything, does not say a word, and asks the one available question: **who is the room in the form.** The form is read out entire and it is four lines long: *Papers of the district's business found in a private room in this town and copied in a private hand, being five sheets and no more. Found at the fourth hour of the eighty-first day of the month in a room over a draper's shop on the north side, and the room is over a draper's shop and the shop is named at the foot. Taken into the district's keeping and receipted. Signed: C. Ilsley, officer, this county.* **There is no room in it. There is a shop at the foot. There is no room. I have read that form out to four men in this county this month and not one of them has asked me a question about it, and you are the first, and the answer is that there is no room in it because there is nothing in it that a room is.** And then, in a street, at about the sixth hour, because Tomas asked the second half of the question and Trant chose where the answer was given and said so: **the fifth clause, read from memory, in about nine seconds, entire** — *that the receipt does not make the holder a keeper of any route, and no answer to any signal carried on or by the object is to be entered as anybody's answer for a household of this ward, and the holder has no standing to speak for any household in Kells or in any ward, and the ward's records are the ward's.* **Then it has taken your room off you and it has done it in nine seconds and neither of us had to do anything. I have been at that door twenty-two years and I have never once had to use that clause and I have never once had to know what it said, and a man from four counties has put it in a piece of paper to protect himself from an office.** And the paper is on his side: **there is one thing that paper can do to you and it can read you. It cannot enter a claim for you and it cannot answer for you and it cannot make you responsible for anybody, because you have no standing to be responsible for anybody. So the form has a man in it and nothing else. That is the whole of your paper being on your side and I would not build a fire on it, because the day a man with a standing in this county wants that room he will have it, and you have none, and that is the only reason he cannot.** And then the sentence that makes the day: **the same clause is the reason five sheets went out of that room, and I do not know that it is and neither do you, and I have been in this building twenty-two years and I have never once thought about who copies what, and a man from four counties has been in my hall twice and read four hundred sheets off my counter and the reason he was able to copy them is a clause about standing, and a clause about standing is not a clause about paper.** The five sheets are taken at about the sixth hour and receipted one at a time on a printed slip with a number, and he gives a receipt for each in his own hand with the hour and reads the numbers out twice. **The carbon is the third one in the satchel and he says unasked: after the fourth hour today there is one copy of that form in the world and it is in a district office in another county, and that is not a loss, and a form does not care what is written on the back of it, and nobody in that office will ever read the back of it. Tomas says *there is not*.** The bench goes at about the seventh hour because it belongs to the draper's shop, and the draper says it in one sentence, is not unkind, does not put it in a form, will not charge for the room to-day because he has taken the bench, and says **you are out on the eighty-second and not before, and a man who finds out on the morning is a man who has been told he can go tonight.** **The page of his own hand carrying the Larkins' sentence is not on the list, because a page of a man's own hand is not a copy of a Crown sheet, and it is the only thing in that room a form could not take, and it gives him nothing.** In the yard at about the seventh hour the officer stands four feet off the frame, does not go nearer, with the satchel shut, and says he has not asked for the object and is not going to and it is not on his form, that he was told by the man in charge of the shelves and believed him, and that he would like entered both halves — that an officer of this county did not take a bell **and that he did not want to**, because the half he did not want to is the half that will not be believed in about four years. The day-book at about the eighth hour of the evening: **the thing I have been building for six weeks had no owner and no claim and could be taken by a man with a form in about three hours, and the law that took it is a clause of a receipt I asked a clerk to add to in another county, and I asked for it because I was right, and I am not going to separate those two things for the rest of my life**; **the right I have felt about this room was not a right, it was a habit, and a habit of carrying is not a thing anybody can defend**; and **there was no room in the form, and the paper is on my side because it is empty, and I am not going to be told it was a comfort and I am going to remember it because I am afraid I will need it**; all five clauses stand, none broken by the losing and none by the keeping, nothing rung, the frame four bolts in the yard and he has not been inside the cradle.

## Chapter 0096 — A Fragment Of The Name Ledger

Day 82, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a yard, a passage, an outside stair, two shut rooms. He is out of the room six days early and it took one sentence and he is in a yard with a barrow, a frame with four bolts and no clapper, a bell in a sack, five receipts, thirteen documents, a slate with a ruled-through line, and about four hours before the rain. **The question is not where the names go — four people in four counties have answered that with a column, a stamp, a habit, a man who has never been upstairs, and a form with four fields and none of them the words. The question is whether there is a book.** He does not ask the woman who lets the rooms, and it is on the page before anything else, because he asked her once to put a thing in a box and she did and asked nothing and has told him twice for nothing what she keeps, **and a person may be asked and may refuse, and he has been refused six times since the thirty-fourth and not one of the six cost her anything, and the next one is not his to spend on a woman who keeps about nine hundred names and has never been asked what her book is for.** The man who owns the house is a dyer with a key to his own upper rooms and **about four years of a room over his own vat room that he could not let and about nine pounds a quarter he has had out of it and has not had a wage out of those two rooms in four years and has not got anybody to ask.** Tomas asks one question, and it is his own chore, and it is about nine seconds long: **if you went up those stairs and took a page out of one of those three books and put it in your coat, and nobody had asked you to and nobody had paid you and you were not going to be thanked, would you do it.** The dyer says **no — I would take the whole of the fire out and I would not touch the books, I have been up there four times in four years and I have not touched anything, and I would like to be able to keep saying that** — and Tomas says that is the right answer and he is not going to argue it, and then says the thing that took four minutes on a step: **what I want is a sheet out of a book that was not his, in a room that was not his, in a building that was not the Crown's, from papers belonging to an office that shut on the sixty-seventh; and there is no law against it and no form against it and no clause of a receipt that touches it; and there is also no way whatever to show that a man has not done it, which is the whole of why he could do it and also the whole of why he could not afterwards put it in a book and have anybody believe him.** The dyer goes up at about the seventh hour **on his own account**, and Tomas does not go with him and **does not ask her to come up the stair and she does not offer**, and the eighth refusal and the ninth are not spent on her. **And the man who came down did not claim to have any right, and said so before he was asked, and did not say what it was for: I have no right, I am the owner of this house and I have a key to my own rooms and there is a thing in them that is not mine, and I have taken nine lines out of a book that is not mine, and I have done it because a man with a barrow has been standing at the bottom of my stair for six days and because I have been paying for two rooms for four years — and I have been up there four times in four years and I have not touched anything, and I have just touched something, and I am going to be a man who touched something, and I could have been a man who did not, and I would have been able to say that at the end of my life.** **And the witness wrote the hour down, in her own hand, on a sheet of her own, and she was asked to do it by a man with no standing, and she could have refused, and he had about nine seconds to hear whether she was going to.** She comes out because a man is in her passage and there is a rule about that in a house with rooms in it, and she stands at the bottom of the stair and says say what you are about to do and say it where the person at the top of that stair can hear you. He does, on the bottom step, in about nine seconds, with the day, the hour, and the number of lines, and he does not say what he thinks it is for. **I have kept a book four years, about nine hundred names in it and a column for the nights and a column for what was owed and a column for the room, and I have never once been asked what it is — so I am going to write the hour down, and I am not doing it for you and I am not doing it for him and I am doing it because a woman of mine put her hand on a line in this town four days ago in a market and the district shut that evening, and there is not one written thing anywhere in this town that says a thing like that was done, and I have four years of people in a book and about nine hundred of them could be called a witness to something and none of them has ever been asked.** She keeps the sheet. **Do you want it. No. And I want it written down that I did not ask for it and did not try, and that the reason is that a person can be asked and a piece of paper cannot, and I have spent six weeks learning the second half of that and have never once been able to use it.** He reads the fragment in the passage in about two minutes: **a column of numbers, a column of days, and a third column empty from end to end, nine lines of it, ruled in the same hand and never once used.** **And a district number is a filing reference and not a man** — which a district officer in another county said in his own hand on the forty-second day of the month and refused to guess at, and which Tomas has carried for forty days and used nine times as a way of not asking a question. **The numbers run in a sequence. Two of the nine are the same number with two different days against them. One is struck through in a hand that is not the ruling hand and the striking has a day and there is nothing against the striking. And that is the whole of it: it does not say where a name goes, it does not say a name at all, it says a reference goes, and a reference going somewhere is not a person.** And then he writes down, immediately, that he is not going to join it to the six certified returns in a drawer two counties back with one name and one hand and five different dates, **because a person who joins them has joined them because they are both dates**, and that he had the page in his hand and felt the thing start. He tries to thank them both and is refused twice: the dyer — **you did not pay me and you did not ask me to and I would have said no to the money; if you say thank you to me twice I will begin to think the thirty-six pounds is coming back and it is not and I have made my peace with that and I do not want it unsettled**; and the woman with the book — **no, and you are not to say it to the man at the top of that stair either, and if you do I shall take my sheet out and burn it, and I would rather be a woman who has written one thing down than a woman who has been thanked.** Neither gives a name and neither is owed a visit and there is no form on which anything could be done for either. The day-book at about the ninth hour of the evening: **it does not tell me how an office gets into a bell and I am not going to write a paragraph about that, because a paragraph about that is a thing I would be able to carry**; **this is the first time in six weeks that I have got a thing that I did not have to be right about — I said a thing out loud on a bottom step and a man in a vat room had a grievance of his own, which was about thirty-six pounds and four years, and a woman who keeps nine hundred names had a grievance of her own about not being asked, and neither of them owed me a thing and I was the man standing in the passage, and the whole of what a man with no standing is for is standing in a passage while two people do a thing for their own reasons and then getting out of the way**; all five clauses stand, nothing rung, four bolts, the bell rung once and not certified for anything and he does not know where it went.

## Chapter 0097 — The Measure Splits

Day 83, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a room over a saddler's shop, an upturned crate in a yard. A bell is wanted in the town, nobody can say which bell, and about eleven men who work with their hands have hired a room over a saddler's shop for the afternoon. He gets in because **a bellmaker is a person with business in that room, which took about nine seconds at the bottom of the stair.** **Tamsin Rook is not in it, and it is the eighth time he has put a question on a slate for her, and she refuses in one word and gives the first reason in eleven years that she has ever given for wanting a thing: a room of eleven men who work with their hands is the only kind of room she has never been in, she has wanted to be in one since she was a child, and wanting a thing is not a reason to go and get it — and if I say anything in there it is the day I stop being a woman with a slate and start being the woman who was at the bell, and I have been the second for eleven years and have never been the first.** He asks one question and it is the wrong one: **is a man who has been barred in one county and has not stopped ringing a bell in another county a man the Measure can sit with in a room.** A wheelwright answers in about nine seconds: **no, and that is not the Measure's business, and not one of us has ever walked into a room and asked anybody to lift it, and you have brought us a thing about you and asked us to rule on it; we are not a court and we are not a parish and we are not a district, and if you want a thing ruled on there is a place about nine miles off and you have to go on a day you do not choose.** And Tomas: **because I wanted the room to have a position on it before I put my hand on the table, and a man who puts a position in a room first is a man who has already built something.** The wheelwright: **that is the first sensible thing anybody has said in that room in about four years.** **And then the room splits, and it does not split on the bar and it does not split on a licence, and Tomas has had both halves of the split in his own coat for six weeks without ever having them next to each other.** A miller: **if a bell can be traced then anybody can stand in front of it in nine years and use it, and the man who traced it is the man they use it against, and there is a maker in this county who traced one for me in the spring and he will tell you himself that he wished he had not.** A saddler on the licensed side, twenty-two years: **and if a bell cannot be traced then a man may put anything into anybody's town, and the town cannot show anybody where it came from, and that is a hazard to the public and not only to the trade, and there is a clause of the craft law about it in the fourth place and it is four hundred years old.** **And Rowan Ketch is in the room, at the back, reading a slate upside down, and neither man had seen him.** Ketch: **A traceable bell is a bell that can be proved, and a bell that can be proved can be stood in front of later and used against whoever proved it. I said that, on a market day, in a room over a saddler's shop in a town four counties behind this one, in the afternoon, and I have written it down twice since. And a bell made in a place under a declaration, out of a stock that cannot be traced, is a bell that can be used and cannot be traced, and that is a hazard to the public and not only to the trade. I said that too, in the same room, on the same afternoon, about an hour later. I did not see them, and I want it noticed that I did not see them, because I am a master and I have been in this trade longer than any man in this room and I have never once put those two sentences next to each other, and neither has the man who learned them off me.** And then the finding: **that is the split, and it is not a split anybody wanted and it is not about the bar and it is not about a licence, and it is two trades inside one word. A licensed central control says a bell that cannot be traced is a danger to the public, and it is right and it has to be, and if it says anything else then it is not protecting anybody. Independent craft says a bell that can be proved is a danger to the man who proved it, and it is right and it has to be, and if it says anything else then it is not craft, it is a trade union with a bell in it. And both are correct and they cannot both be the rule, and there is no room in this kingdom where both of them can be said, and the Measure is one of them and the other one has been in about four counties of workshops saying the same thing to each other since before the Measure had a name.** And then the cost, which is the only thing that costs Tomas anything in this chapter: somebody asks what he has, and he says **a certificate of metal and a note and nothing else** — Rowan Ketch certified that bell on the fifty-second day of the month at about the third hour of the afternoon, in his own hand, with the hour, on one sheet, no copy and none left behind, six witness names and one of the six written by the witness himself — **and it does not certify the man and it does not certify the making, and I have carried it in a coat for twenty-one days as though it were a passport, and it is not a passport, and I have known what it says the whole time.** Ketch: **do not carry it as a passport. You are not the first. It is the single most common way a man wears out a thing that was given to him in good faith, and when it wears out it wears out the man who trusted the giver, and not the giver.** And when he is asked about the bell in the sack he refuses in about four seconds, before he is asked: **I am not going to look at it. I have certified the metal and the note and I did not certify the man, and I am not going to certify the same bell twice for the same two facts, and if I did then in about nine years there would be two sheets in this kingdom saying the same thing about the same metal in two hands and one of them would be a copy, and a copy is how a sentence stops belonging to the person who said it. That is not caution. I have done it once in my life and it is the only thing in this trade I am ashamed of.** And: **you have a bell nobody can prove, out of a stock two shops from a Crown store, in a county with a bar on a different bell, and about nine of them do not care and two of them have just spent an hour telling each other why they cannot both be right, and neither of them has mentioned your bell once.** The day-book at about the ninth hour of the evening: **the Measure is two trades inside one word and it always has been, and they cannot both be the rule**; **a certificate of metal and a note and nothing else, and knowing what a thing says and carrying it as though it said something else is the whole of what I have been doing for six weeks**; **the split is not about me and I am not the injured party and nobody in that room was a villain**; **my bar is the correct side of a correct argument and I cannot argue the other side without being a man who has no licence to argue anything about a bell in any county**; **the eighth use of the slate, five of the eight worked and three of them cost me, and not one of the eight cost her anything**; all five clauses stand, nothing rung, **his own plain carrier is barred and in a sealed yard in Kells and it is going to stay there and I did not go near it and I am not going to**, the certified bell not certified for anything and untouched for six days.

## Chapter 0098 — What The Town Does With A Week

Day 84, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, a market of about nine hundred, a pitch, the east lane, the stone landing. She does not ask her three questions of a town, and then she watches about nine hundred people say the same nine-minute thing to each other about nine hundred times. **Because that is what a town does with a week, and it is not what anybody expects, and it is not a refusal and it is not a cart. It is a queue.** Two women in a line at a fish pitch say a name to each other, flatly, in about eleven seconds, and the woman in front turns round and says it back, and a third says it to nobody at all in the ordinary way; and a man at a pitch of nothing in particular said his neighbour's name and the neighbour said it back, and a woman with a barrow of onions said a name to a stranger and the stranger said it back before she had got to the end of the sentence. **Not the names. The act. A claim said twice in a market is a thing a market does now, and it started at ten feet on the seventieth from a woman with a slate, and nobody voted on it, and about a hundred people did it yesterday and none of them knew they were doing anything at all.** And: **that is what the road found. She has been on a road for eleven years looking for a place and a road does not go to a place, and on the eighty-fourth the road found a queue, and a queue is not a place and cannot be looked up and cannot be sent a rider and has no name on any map in this kingdom, and I have spent eleven years learning to go to places.** The figure she will not improve: **a bell in a market takes about nine days to change a town; she has got one bell and it is seven, and she is going to write the nine down because a rule wants more than one, and she is going to write the seven down because the seven is what she has, and I am not going to be the woman who improves a figure she has one of.** The one question, at about the fifth hour of the morning, is **who said that to you**, asked of a man at a pitch of cloth about four feet long: **my brother, at the well, in the spring; and I have said it here twice since and about nine people heard it the first time, because a woman with a slate stood ten feet off and said it back to me in my own words, and I have said it four times since then and I am going to keep saying it and I have not stopped and nobody has asked me to stop — and I will tell you what nobody has told you, because you have got a slate and you have not written anything on it in an hour and a half: I said it at the well in the spring and I said it here last week and a woman said it back to me at ten feet and about nine people heard it, and it is the same as saying it at a well, except that at a well it is gone in a minute and this time it is not, because nine people heard it and one of them is my neighbour's wife and she said it to her brother in a queue on the seventy-eighth.** And then: **I have not counted it and you are not going to count it for me, and I am not going to say a number, and you can go away now.** The nine names: **about nine people in this town know that their names are on a piece of slate, and she knows two of them, and this is how she knows — two women in this market who said a sentence and then said, in the ordinary way, as an aside, *and I have said that before, and I have said it here twice now***, which is the ordinary way a person tells you they have already been written down, and neither of them knew they were telling her anything. **Nine is not a large number. Nine is a number a market can hold and a town cannot forget and a clerk's hall cannot even see.** Then the east lane, at about the seventh hour of the morning, at the gable with the lean-to, **and she does not knock and the door opens anyway, and Bess Marrow says the thing that is the finding of the eighty-fourth before anybody has said one word: you have not come back to this door. There was a bell in the square on the seventy-seventh, about nine hundred people, a man's name in it, a clerk's name, and I have said so to about four people and I have not said it to my mother because she cannot read — and nobody has come to this door. Nobody has come to this door for the book. Which is in the drawer, where it has been for nine years, and which you did not ask me for and have not come back for, and I have been sitting in this kitchen for seven days with a fire in it waiting to be asked about the drawer, and nobody has, and I have had my mother's tea in here for seven days and I have not had to be careful of anything at all. And the market has started saying it back.** They stand about two feet apart for about four minutes and a half and it is not a conversation. **I am going to tell you the two things and then I am going to shut this door, and the first is that the thing I have been afraid of for nine years is a cart, and there has not been a cart, and I have not got a reason and I am not going to be given one and I am not going to ask. And the second is that the market saying it back is worth more to this house than the fire, and I am not going to say why, and I have not got a number for it and I am not going to make one up, and I have watched about nine hundred people in that square over seven days and I have counted the things that have changed and I have counted about four. Four. One, a queue says a name back. Two, a man with a barrow put a clerk's name in a square and about nine hundred people heard it and the clerk is right and everybody in this town now knows that a clerk can be named. Three, about eleven men who work with their hands met in a room over a saddler's shop on the eighty-third and about a hundred people have heard that they met. And four, and this is the one I have not got round: nobody has been told what to do.** And then: **a town has decided a chore. Not a policy. Not a practice. A chore. Somebody has to go and put his own hand on his own line, and that is the smallest thing a town can decide, and it is the only thing anybody has decided, and I do not think it will be enough and I am not going to be the woman who says it will not be enough, because the four things I have counted are the only four things I have got and I am not going to be told they are nothing.** She shuts the door on about the seventh hour and it is not hard, and Tamsin stands about nine feet from it and does not knock and does not follow and **does not say one word about anything else, and nobody has said a word to her about her hearing at any hour in this chapter, and what a woman in a market did say, in the ordinary way, is that she has not written anything on a slate for an hour and a half, and that is about a slate.** Her entry on the landing at about the ninth hour of the evening, first and second columns, third and fourth left, and the fourth has one line in it and she has not improved it: **the road has found something, and it is a queue**; **a list made in queues can be said twice, and saying it twice is the only thing in this kingdom that has ever moved a line, and it is four inches long**; **and it is not a cart — nobody has been sent for, nobody has had a man at a door, nobody has been rescued, nobody has been asked for anything, and there is not one refusal in this market that has cost anybody a pitch or four pounds a quarter, and the nine pitches that came off that market on the sixty-ninth are still off it, and about thirty-six pounds a quarter has not come back to nine households and is not coming back**; nobody asked her for a reason, she rang nothing, the four columns stand and nothing is merged, and the price side of the slate is the other way up.

## Chapter 0099 — The King Is Not Defeated

Day 85, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a yard, a street, a clerk's hall, a step. He has been in the town twenty-eight days and he has asked four people questions and been answered correctly four times, and he works out the thing that has been behind all four: **nobody has asked for the finding.** The district's finding of the twenty-eighth is a document; it was withdrawn on the thirty-third by a surveyor on her own paper; **three hundred people could ask for it and in seven weeks in four counties not one person has.** He gets to the front of the hall at about the fourth hour on a market day with the square full and **he does not go in and ask for it**, and that is the whole of the eighty-fifth up to the seventh hour, and the reason is at the foot of the entry and is not a good one: **a finding is a document, and I have made four records in six weeks about persons who did not ask me for one, and one of them cost a household the front of a row of nine pitches, and the man who cost it is right and the man who made it is me; and a man who has run out of things to do asks an office for a piece of paper, and a piece of paper is the only thing he can be certain of.** At about the seventh hour he goes in, because a hall is a building and the notice nine feet inside the door means what it says. Bryn Pethick, who has noticed three days of not asking and says he has not decided what to make of it and that deciding not to make something of a man is not a kindness: **I have come about the finding of the district.** And Tomas, inside about nine seconds, knows he has made a mistake: **say the rest of it.** The answer is **no**, and it is the only answer there is: **that finding is not in this building. It never was. It is about a ward in another county, in a district in another county, and about nine hundred bells in a district that is not this one, and there is about nine hundred lines a year coming into this hall and not one of the nine hundred is that document. You cannot ask me for it because it is not this hall's business, and if I went and got it for you I would be doing a thing nobody has authority to do in a building where anybody may ask a clerk about the work.** And where it is: **in a building in another county, in a file, on a shelf, in about a day and a half's road, and you can write for it, and there is a form for writing, and anybody may use the form, and the form will be answered inside a month, and it will be answered correctly, and about three hundred people in that county could have used that form since the thirty-third and not one of them has.** And then the sentence, which is the same one: **a document nobody has asked for is a thing that has not happened.** And the thing that is said afterwards: **you have had that since the fortieth, and you carried it thirty-three days before you ever came into this hall, and you made it into a key, and about four hundred people in this county heard that you had been told what a document is and every one of them heard it as a key — and it has been forty-five days and nobody has asked for anything, and you are not going to, and I have watched you three times now in this street not going in. And it is not a virtue. A man who carries a sentence for forty-five days and never once asks anybody to put it in a place is not careful. He is a man who has found out that a document is the only thing he can be certain of, and who has not found a household yet that wants to be certain of, and who is therefore still carrying it about, and I have been in this building about four years and I have never once seen anybody do that.** And then the thing he does not ask for: he tells the clerk he knows there is an answer of hers in that building, that it went down the water on the fifty-fifth in an auditor's own book in her own name at the head of it with the hour against it, that it has been here since about the fifty-eighth, and that he found out on the eighty-second from a man in a vat room that a fire had been out in two rooms for a fortnight — and that he is not going to ask for it. **And the reason I am not asking for it is not the fifth clause of a receipt I signed in another county. She is not a household. I am stopping myself, and the whole of it is five words: she did not ask me. And I am not going to go into a building in this town and ask for a woman's answer to a question she was asked in a room in another county, on the fortieth, by a man who has since put her name nowhere and her answer nowhere, and I am not going to do it for her and I am not going to do it to her, and if she ever wants to know whether it arrived she will have to ask somebody who is not me.** And then, unasked, in a corridor's worth of a counter: **a sheet went out of this hall on the eighty-first in a satchel on a form with five numbers on a slip, and one of the five was a carbon of a form of the second series with a reading-out on the back in a private hand, and there was a man with a barrow in the room and the man with the barrow did not say one word. I want it entered that nothing in this building has been altered because of that man's two days here, and that is not a favour to him. Because I have looked, and looking is not in my job — since the seventy-ninth. And I did not do it for him and I did not tell him and I am telling him now because he has just told me about a thing in this building that he has not asked for, and it seems to me that if a man can do that in a room then I can do this in a corridor, and neither of us is going to be thanked for it and I am not going to let it become a thing he is praised for, because I have been in this building about four years and I know exactly what a thing like that turns into in about a year.** And then Tomas asks the question, in the words a woman gave him on a road in another county in the rain on the fortieth, and the clerk gives the same sentence in about nine seconds, writes it standing up in his own hand with the hour, signs it and gives it across — **and Tomas does not take it.** **Do not give me that. I have already written it. I wrote it on the seventy-third and you gave it to me and I am not going to tell you where I have put it, and it is the fourth time you have said that sentence to me in about nine seconds and it is the fifth time it has been given in four counties and three of the five times it was said by a person who did not have to say it, and I am not going to take a fifth one off you and put it in a coat with four, and I am not going to ask you for a second copy and I am not going to let you offer me one, and here is why: two versions of the same position in the same hand can differ. You said that to me about a copy of a sheet and you were right and I have been thinking about it for a fortnight. And the seventy-third is one sheet, in one hand, in this county, and I am not going to write it a second time, and I am not going to let anybody talk me into writing it a second time, and if you want to know what is in it then you can read it, and if you want me to write it again the answer is no, and that is a clerk's whole trade and it is not mine.** And the clerk: **that is the whole of my trade and I have never said it to anybody.** The day-book at about the ninth hour of the evening, and the whole of the volume written out flat: **the Crown has not been defeated and I was never going to defeat it**; a finding withdrawn and three hundred people who could ask; **an officer relieved of nothing because she was never given anything to hold**; **a room taken in about two hours lawfully by a man with a form, and no room in the form, and the reason is my own fifth clause, and the same clause is the reason five sheets went out of it, and neither the officer nor the man in charge of the shelves knows that, and it is the only thing in six weeks that is mine and it is not an advantage**; **an office answered a question it did not have to answer, out loud, in a room, in a stranger's own name, and I made a page about it after he asked me in a doorway not to**; **and a set of rolls is on a shelf in a real building in this town**; **and a clerk of eleven years has not altered one thing in that building because of a sheet of mine, and he did not do it for me and he told me in a corridor, and looking is not in his job. That is the one thing in six weeks that nobody can take back and nobody has paid for, and I did not ask for it and I am not going to be thanked for it and I am not going to be the man who uses it**; **a hall is not a person and never was, and a habit of carrying is the only thing I have ever been able to do about a hall, and it took me forty-five days, four counties, four buildings, a receipt, a bell, a market, a room and a form to find out that I have been talking to a building**; **question one answered, and the second one not answered, and a district number is a filing reference and not a man and that is a smaller thing than I have been carrying**; all five clauses stand, nothing rung, the frame four bolts and not in a room, the bell rung once and not certified for anything and untouched for eight days, the bar on his own bell is a bar in another county and the bell is in a sealed yard in Kells, **my thirteen documents are thirteen**, and it is raining.

## Chapter 0100 — A Bell In A Bag, And Two Questions

Day 86, **the fourth hour of the morning** to **the ninth hour**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, with the last page reading Tamsin Rook's shoulder from behind him. The rain came in at about the seventh hour of the evening of the eighty-fifth and has not stopped. The barrow goes into the yard, the frame into its reinforced padded cradle, **the four retaining bolts made fast at every place the frame has been set down since the thirty-third**, and the bell of fourteen pounds two ounces into a sack against the cradle. **The draper: you can have the yard, and I am saying that a man with a barrow and a yard is a different thing from a man with a barrow and a lane, and I am not charging you for the outside of my own shop and I would like it entered that I said so, and if you thank me I will put the rent up.** She is at the gate at about the fourth hour with a bag and a slate and nothing else he can see, and he does not ask her what she is carrying or what she can afford or why she is here. Nobody comes out of a door to see them off. The market keeper puts a paper bag on the barrow — two loaves and a piece of cheese, about fourpence — and says **that is on the pitch and you did not pay for it and I am not going to say you did not pay for it, and I have said that deliberately.** They leave at about the fifth hour on the road down and along the water, **he had been in Miremarket twenty-nine days, he came down the water for one reason, he no longer has it, and he had not gone looking for another, and a habit of carrying is also a habit of not putting down.** The ledger, written under a hedge in the rain, the whole of what is on the page at the end of six weeks: a practice stopped in one district in writing and about eleven thousand lines a year behind it; about fifty-one pounds into six hands and a firm of nine that now knows why nobody would lend to it, **and the firm is answered and the firm is finished and the man who said so said it before the money moved**; a room, a bench and five sheets gone and the law that took them a clause in a receipt he signed in another county; **nine lines of a book of numbers in a coat, taken by a man who has no right and said so before he was asked, and the hour written down by a woman with a book of about nine hundred names who kept the sheet and would not give it to him, and neither of them is owed a visit and there is no column for either of them and no form on which I could do anything for them at all**; a bell rung once in a market in front of about nine hundred with no claim in it and nobody knows where it went; **a child on a carrier's cart on the seventy-second, not found, not placed, not named, not rescued, not brought back; nine pitches off a market; about thirty-six pounds a quarter not coming back; a handcart that did not earn; a mother who can say a number and the number is no; and nobody was rescued and nobody was told the refusal was worth it and nobody is going to be**; **and a clerk of eleven years who has not altered one thing in a building because of a sheet of mine, who did not do it for me, and that is the one thing in six weeks that nobody can take back and nobody has paid for**; **and a name at Ashlade, four counties up, is a name and not a place, and a boy of eight is a name, and there is no book of him anywhere in this kingdom and there is no form for one, and the only thing that has ever gone to a place to find out whether a person is in it is a person on foot with a bag, and I have got a bag and I have not got a place and I am not going to pretend to anybody that those are the same shape**; **and I do not know how an office gets into a bell, and I have not got nearer it, and I am not going to write a paragraph about that, because a paragraph about that is a thing I would be able to carry**; **the five dates are unaccounted for and I am not going to account for them and I am not going to call them a forgery and I am not going to call them a conspiracy, and I had nine lines of numbers in my hand on the eighty-second and I felt it start and I wrote the sentence down again**; and a service entry that has been genuine-or-borrowed for six weeks and is not settled. **The two questions, written under a hedge because a day-book gets wet.** One, how a Crown office turns one clerk's answer into a whole settlement's consent: **answered, and it is the whole of six weeks and it is a small answer: it does not need to. A roll does not need anybody's answer. A duty is a mouth, and a mouth is for a claim, and the claim is made in a queue, in about nine minutes, in the ordinary voice, by about eleven thousand people a year who are not clerks and are not agents of anything and are usually right about who they are. I do not need a better one and there is not a better one.** Two, where names are sent when they are registered: **not answered, and it is not going to be answered on this road and I am not going to guess at it. A district officer asked his district twice in writing and had no answer to either. A clerk of four years has never been upstairs. A dyer has a key to two rooms that are not his. A man took nine lines out of one of them and they were numbers. That is the whole of what six weeks has produced about it and I am writing it down in a hedge in the rain and I am not going to improve it.** And then he rules a space and writes at the top of it a numeral: *Three. **Which office is it.*** **He did not write it before she said it and he has written down that he did not write it before she said it, and the reason is that a question he has found himself is a thing he can carry and a question somebody has put to him is a thing he has to answer, and he has learned that in six weeks at some cost.** **And he did not ring anything, and he has said that in a book four times now and is going to stop, because a man who keeps saying that he is not doing a thing is a man who wants somebody to know he could have:** the Crown carrier is in its cradle with four bolts and no clapper and **cannot be rung and I did not furnish one and I have not gone near it and I am not going to**; the certified plain carrier is in a sack against that cradle with no wax and no dressed groove and only a caster's mark and a day, and **I have rung it twice myself, once on a sill in a town four counties behind this one on the fifty-second and once in a market square in a town four days behind this one on the seventy-seventh, and I have never learned where either of them went, I have never once in my life learned it, I wrote that in this book on the sixty-second, and I have not learned it since and I do not now, and nobody in that square was told where it went and nobody was told by me and I am not going to be told it as a favour and I am not going to go back and look**; and **he did not ring a bell he has made, and the reason is not a rule — the only bell I have made is in a sealed yard in a county whose bar I do not argue, it has been barred since the twenty-sixth from any public place in that county, and I did not go near that yard in six weeks and I am not going to, and a bell I made is not in this bag.** **And he did not write a fourth column entry, and why: a fourth column is a thing nobody could answer, and I have had two in my life, and the first was about a paper and a building and it cost me nothing and it taught me everything, and the second was about nine people in a market and about nine people in that town are not safe because a careful man wrote them down and he wrote down at the eleventh hour of the evening of the seventy-sixth that he knew and that he was going to do it anyway — so I know what the fourth column is and what it costs and I have had it twice, and I am not going to fill it in again in this county, and I am not going to fill it in on a road either, and a man who has worked out what a column is is not obliged to go on having one.** The inventory on the last page: the list that cannot be certified and cannot be unrolled; four columns and a slate with the price side the other way up; a witness in a corridor; the fragment; the receipt in five clauses, **and the second of them is the reason I cannot pay for anything and the fifth of them is the reason there was no room in a form and the reason five sheets went out of it, and I asked for it and I was right and it has cost me a room and a bench and five sheets and I am not going to separate those two things in my own head for the rest of my life**; a frame in a cradle he has never been inside; and a woman who has said nine words on a landing and is not going to be thanked. And then: **six weeks and I have got: a careful life is a way of writing things down. A document is what a person is read by, and I have been manufacturing them since the thirty-second, and a claim in metal is a thing a man cannot edit, and I have had one of those and I do not know where it went. And what I am is a man with a bar on his own bell, a receipt with a fifth clause in it, and a road, and I have not got a place, and I am not going to look for one in this county, and a town of about two thousand people has decided a chore and it is the smallest thing a town can decide, and about a hundred people in it say a name back to each other in a queue, and two of about nine hundred know that their names are on a piece of slate, and nobody has been told what to decide, and none of that is mine and I did not do any of it and it is not going to last longer than about four years unless somebody keeps at it, and I am a man with a bag and a barrow and no standing and I have never in my life been better at anything. Nobody asked her for a reason. Her hearing is permanent and it is hers and it was priced in a smith's shop on the fifty-first and she is not going to be rescued out of it and nothing has been restored to her in six weeks and nothing is going to be, and she rang nothing, and the bell is in her bag and it is hers. Nobody was rescued. Nobody was told their refusal was worth it. Nobody is going to be. And I have not learned where a warning lands. I have never learned it.
