I have said so four times in this book and I am going to stop, and if anybody ever asks me in any county in any year, the answer is in the third column of the sixty-second and it has not changed and I have not got a better one.** And then the last page of the book is finished, the day is not, and it is about the ninth hour on a road down off the water in the rain, and she is a step behind him and about ten feet off his left shoulder, **because ten feet is not near enough to be part of a thing and it is near enough to write down what it is, and she has been reading his left shoulder from behind him for eleven years and she does it with her chin and not her eyes.** He shuts the book and puts it inside his coat, **on top of the page of his own hand that carries a household of five's sentence and that is not his and that he may not give to anybody and that he did not give to a form on the eighty-first and that is the only reason he still has it.** And she says, from about ten feet off his left shoulder, in the ordinary voice, in the rain, without stopping: **you have written that down three times in six weeks, that you do not know how an office gets into a bell, and that you have not got nearer it, and that you are not going to write a paragraph about it. I never have. Then you have been asked it four times and you have not once been asked which office. Which office is it.** And that was the whole of the last of it — **not a promise and not a refusal, a question asked by the person who had the right to ask it** — and it was going to be on a page that night under a numeral, and he answered it in four words and did not improve it in the morning: **Nobody has told me.** And she said nothing at all, because that is her side of it, and the barrow went down the road with a frame in a cradle in it and a bell in a bag against the cradle, and the four bolts were made fast at every place the frame had been set down, and there was no clapper in it, and a receipt in five clauses was in a coat under a book, and a man who had rung a bell he did not make once in a market and had been told he was right, and had not been told where it went, walked on down a road in the rain with a woman ten feet off his left shoulder and a list that cannot be unrolled and four columns and a slate and a witness and a fragment and thirteen documents, and about eleven hundred people behind him in a town of about two thousand who had been told nothing at all about what to decide, and a market that had a chore in it, and a building with a set of rolls on a shelf in it and a clerk in it who had altered nothing, and a house at the end of a lane with a book in a drawer that was not going to be asked about, and a woman at a gate in a yard who had said no on the seventy-second in the ordinary way and was owed nothing, and about forty-four thousand names in four counties, and a boy of eight, and a road.

## Chapter 0101 — A Bell On A Post

Day 87, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a road four miles up off the water, a crossing, a notice-board, a low wall. The rain stopped in the night without him noticing and a barrow goes at the speed of a barrow. He has a barrow, a frame in a reinforced padded cradle with four bolts made fast, a sack with a bell of fourteen pounds two ounces in it, thirteen documents, five receipts, a fragment, four columns and a road, **and no room, because a yard behind a draper's shop was yesterday's.** **At about the sixth hour he stops under a bell on a post above a notice-board and it rings, and there is nobody within about forty yards of it and nothing to strike it with.** The bell is bronze, plain-crowned, with three winters of wax in a grey ring inside the mouth — a bell somebody has stood under and looked at — and a bracket and a socket and a wire going down inside the post, which is not a striker's gear. He does not touch it beyond the wax and writes that down as a rule he set for himself. The sheet under it is a **court date at the hall in Wendle, dated the eleventh of the month, four days forward**, and the bell rang today: **the sound came first and the sheet is what the sound was for**, and then he does not trust it and gives the second explanation — the bell rings the day before because a man with a book cannot get to about two hundred doors in a day — and writes that he has two explanations and one bell and is not going to write down the one he likes best. Tamsin Rook, at ten feet, says **that was not a keeper's bell**, and it is not a plain one either, and there is no box for it on any form he has ever seen, and **he is not going to coin a word for it in a book on a road**, and she says *because a word is a thing you can carry and you have got about nine of them already that turned out to be shelves.* Day-book at about the ninth hour of the evening, first and third columns, second and fourth left: **a sound is what a person is read by, and a sound is cheaper than a sheet and goes further, and the sheet is only there to say afterwards what the sound was, and I have been reading the sheet**; **which office is it — I still do not have the name of it, and a number is a filing reference and not a man**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung**, four bolts, the frame never in a building, the bell in the sack untouched since the seventy-seventh, his own bell in a sealed yard in Kells and he is not going to it, thirteen.

## Chapter 0102 — Wendle, And A Woman Who Did Not Come

Day 88, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a village of about two hundred at the fourth mile, a ford, a lean-to with a board across two barrels, a notice-board, a low wall. He goes to find the man who reads the notices and is told in about nine seconds what he came for. **Abner Tole, no office, about nine shillings a quarter, nine years.** The bell is not rung here and has not been rung in this village in his time; it comes from **Ashmoor, eleven miles**, where there is a room with a counter and a man and a sheet with a day and a place on it, and the day and place go on the sheet and the sheet goes on a barrow and the barrow comes round, **and the same day sets the bell.** The bell is a wire: eleven miles in the ground under a road into a wall in Ashmoor. Nobody has to be there for it. **Tole said in advance which of four questions he would not answer now and why — a stranger with a Crown thing in a cradle, about two hundred people on his board, and a man in a lane four counties off who put a thing on a table in a market — and Tomas does not ask it, because a man told in advance has not been refused and does not need refusing.** Nobody comes to a door about a court date; the clerk marks *not attended* and the line is closed. Tole counts, unasked and unthanked, on the back of a sheet between about the fifth and seventh hours: **about four in ten over nine years.** And he says the thing he has told nobody — **he has read out about four hundred notices in nine years and has never once been asked by anybody from Ashmoor whether the people were coming** — and has written in a margin on about nine of them that he said it out loud at the board, and there is no column for that either. **Salla Keene did not come on the fifth, a second notice went on the seventh by the same bell, the line is marked not attended twice and closed, and a third date is next week.** He does not see her and does not ask to. When he asks whether she is in the house Tole refuses to find out and gives the reason: **the moment he goes to one door he has to go to every door, and then the board is not a board — and that is why there is no form anywhere in this kingdom on which anybody could have found out whether a woman was in a house.** **Osten Keene comes down to the ford on his own account** and says one true unkind thing: he has swept under that bell for nine years and hung a washing line off its post, and a bell is a thing a man makes so that a thing can go further than a voice, and his daughter of nine cannot hear it from the far end of the village and can hear him shouting from the yard, **and it goes over his mother's door telling her about a room in Ashmoor, and there is no voice in it and there is no man in it.** Day-book: **a schedule does not have a face, and that is not a comfort and not a relief and not the good news I have been telling myself for a week it is going to be**; **I have been angry for eight weeks and could not be, and this morning I found out I was looking for a face**; **not being angry is not the same as it being all right — it is the difference between a thing I can fight and a thing I can only describe, and describing it is what I have been doing for eight weeks, and it has got me to Ashmoor tomorrow at about the ninth hour**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung**, thirteen, and a woman's name written on the page and **not in a coat and not in a book in a coat and not on a slate, and I am not going to write it down twice anywhere.**

## Chapter 0103 — The Same Hand

Day 89, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a smithy at a crossroads three miles short of Ashmoor, an open arch, a wall at the fourth milestone. **Doss Harrow**, about thirty-one years, works flat iron, and says before the barrow is in the arch: *put the barrow outside, I do not want that frame in here, and I want it entered that I said so.* He comes out to it. He asks one question and may be refused: **who carried it** — *an officer of a county I have left, in a watch house, at about the fifth hour of the morning of the thirty-third, and he read out five clauses and I asked for one of them and he wrote it in, and a man who asks a clerk to add a clause, I have heard of about two of those in thirty-one years and one of them was me and I got the wording wrong.* Then he names the work, standing at the arch, without going near it: **a coupling on the swing cut nearly through, and a thin copper jumper soldered across the cut, done from the inside, too neat for a patch in a hurry** — and Tomas has to put a hand on the barrow. **And Harrow has fitted the same hand of work to three bells in this county over nine years: a market bell at Marrow Bridge about five years back, a bridge bell at Pyle about two years back, and a water bell on a sill in a village about nine years back that he has told nobody he went out to.** Each came with a sheet with a number at the top of it; one man read the number out three times. He has no fourth in his head at night and says so. **He will not say what it does — he has had a working theory for about five years, has not tested it, and will not hand a theory to a man with a barrow who will write it in a book and take it four hundred miles** — and Tomas says no to it in about a second and a half and writes down that he said no and did not want it. And then the finding, in about four seconds, without weight: **there is a store. Every district has got one and ours is in Ashmoor and it is a room with a counter in it and a book on the counter and a man behind the counter, and you go in with a district instruction and he gives you the thing out of a shelf and writes what he gave you in the book, and if you are the sort of person who wants to know what a thing is for there is a column on the form for that and nobody has ever filled it in in my hearing.** And do not ask him what the column is for, because a man at a counter will give you a thing in nine seconds and will not give you a reason in nine minutes, and if you ask in front of other people he will be right and you will have spent the morning. Tomas reads the finding back to him off a scrap of slate off the wall, which is the fourth time in eight weeks. Day-book at about the ninth hour of the evening: **the same hand**; **I have been carrying that repair for ten days as a who, and it is not a who, it is a where from**; **a description of a thing is not a mechanism and a store is not an office and I do not know what a coupling and a jumper do and I have been asking who cut the coupling and not what the cut is, and Sabin Fell would have had my hands for it**; **a man who cannot be found is a different thing from a counter that can be stood at**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung**, four bolts, the frame stood in an arch for a minute and a half and did not leave the cradle, the bell in the sack untouched, thirteen documents and a scrap of slate that is neither.

## Chapter 0104 — A Bell On A Roll

Day 90, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a barn with about nine sheep, a market square of about eleven hundred people, a board under a roof, the edge of the square. He worked out overnight that **the thing he has been unable to find for two volumes is not the thing that is frightening.** The board has three columns — things entered for district duty in this hundred, **bell** / **other** / **withdrawn** — and he reads all nineteen lines of the first column because he has learned that a man who reads three of them has chosen three. Eleven market and work bells, four boundary bells, two school bells, a bridge bell, **and one water bell with the keeper's field empty, ruled past the end of the line.** **A second water bell, entered the same way, on the same form, in a second county — NOT Marta Vell's bell, and not her village and not her number, and Chapter 0104 says so in the plainest sentence it has — and he writes the district number down and not a name, because there is no name on it: there is a hamlet and a ford.** He wants, with a directness that shocks him, to join them, and does not, and writes out why: **two empties in a column is a clerk doing a form; if I cannot say what the third thing is then I do not get to keep the second one, because the second one is what I am holding the first one up with; a person who joins them has joined them because they are both empty.** **Ottmar Dace**, four years at the board, answers *the other column* (**about two hundred and forty things, about nine gates, about four bridges, about four years of the sheet, about nine withdrawn**), the keeper's box (**filled in twice without knowing what he was writing, never been told what it is for, and it is not on the sheet**), and what happens to a bell on the sheet (**nothing, until somebody wants it; a district can write to a hundred and say this bell is entered for district duty, and that is a sentence, and it is true from the moment it is in the book**). Then the sentence of the chapter: **a bell on that sheet is not a bell that is taken. It is a bell that is claimed, and a thing that is claimed can be given away to somebody else, and nobody in this hundred has ever had a bell taken off them because of that sheet and nobody ever will, because you cannot take a bell off a village, there is no form for taking a bell off a village, and if there were, it would be a form about a door and not about a bell** — and he asks for it not to be read out. Tomas then asks the second question out loud, in a square, in front of about forty people, and is refused a guess and given the true half: **a name goes on a line and a line goes in a book and a book goes in a barrow to Ashmoor and after Ashmoor I do not know and I have never known** — and then, in his own mouth, using the word *think* twice and asking for it: **a name does not go anywhere, a name stops.** Day-book: **I have been looking for a list of people for two volumes and there is a list of things, and it is the one that matters, and I have been carrying fright about the wrong object since the fifty-eighth**; **a name stops, at Ashmoor, in a barrow, in front of a queue with nobody at the front of it who wants anything, and that is the closest anybody in four counties has come in fifty-one days and I asked him nothing else and I am not going back**; **I did not join two empty boxes in three counties into a pattern and I wanted it more than I have wanted anything since the seventy-seventh**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung**, four bolts, thirteen documents and a bell on a roll with nothing in the keeper's box and nobody within about a day's road who has ever asked.

## Chapter 0105 — A Leaf On A Board

Day 91, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a market of about nine hundred on the Crown's maintained road at about the twenty-eighth mile, a board under a roof, the edge of the market, a lane behind a row of pitches. He reads the four printed sheets first, because he has read about nine hundred and he is not going to stop on a road because one of them is different — a work inspection eleven days forward, a boundary hearing nine days forward, the list of about two hundred and forty things **with the water bell on it, the same line**, and **a notice that the wire to a certain crossing is to be cut for repair and that a bell will not sound on that day** — the only one of the four that tells a person anything they can act on, and it has a number at the top of it and a man eleven miles off who decided on a day. Then the fifth thing on the board: **a leaf, folded once, pinned at an angle under a drawing-pin, and the only thing on that board anybody put there with their own hands.** He reads it four times. **Five words: *Ask the board. Not me.*** **Confirmed by comparison — the t she does not cross, the space she leaves after a full stop, the downstroke on the g that stops and starts — and NOT by copying wax, because there is none on it, and the chapter says on the page that he has been carrying a wax for fifty-one days as though it were a signature, that a mark the size of a thumbnail is not a hand, and that the leaf in Amos Trewin's stock book four counties away is a leaf of unknown hand and it stays one.** And the finding: **it is not an answer, and a board has no standing — you may pin a leaf to a board without a form, without a reason, and without anybody owing you anything, and it is the only way anybody who is where she is can be said to have said anything at all, and she worked that out before he did.** He does not take it, and the reason is Miremarket on the eighty-first at about the sixth hour, five sheets off a table in about three hours, lawfully, under a clause of a receipt he asked a clerk to add to: **a paper off a public board in a Crown town is the same act with a different name**, and there is no clerk within a day's road who would give him a form and nobody in that market who could say a word in his favour. The woman who keeps the board, nine years, about nine leaves in nine years and four put back up in about the same place, tells him for nothing that it is read every morning by about five people and not one of them will say a word, **and that this is not because nobody has seen it.** He does not say the name, with four people within about nine feet. **And he does not go in anywhere to ask after her**, and writes out the option and three reasons, the third and worst being that he would be doing it in order to be able to say he did not go and ask after her. Day-book at about the ninth hour of the evening, first and third columns: **she is the only person alive who can say a thing to me without owing me anything, and she has spent the whole of it refusing, and I am not going to treat that as a sign**; **I did not take the leaf, and that is the first thing in ninety-one days I have refused that I wanted**; **the fifth clause is why I had no standing to ask with, and I have been glad of it twice in four days, and the third reason is the reason**; **I have got a name for it and it is the wrong shape, and I am not going to write the name in this book on a road**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung**, thirteen, and a leaf on a board that he is not going back for.

## Chapter 0106 — The Box For The Person Who Repeated It

Day 92, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, a market of about nine hundred, a pitch, a stone by the water with her boots off. She does not ask him about the leaf and he does not tell her, and **that is the first four hours of the day and for once in a market she writes nothing down about him**; a woman at a pitch says *you have not written anything on that for an hour* and she says **that is about a slate** and the woman says *it is, and I have had one of my own for about eleven years*. The thing she came for is the **second column of a Crown slip: particulars of a repetition — the day, the notice, the words as spoken, *the person who repeated them*, the place, the persons present — six fields, four of which anybody in the room can fill in, and the fourth is the only one that wants a name.** She reads it in about eleven seconds and reads it again. She works out where she sits in it in about a minute and a half, and writes that it took a minute and a half: **a box is cheaper than a witness and a witness can refuse** — a clerk in a hall four days behind said the first half of that sentence on the sixty-second and she was not listening. The man with the slip says he would have written *a woman with a slate* and that it would have been right, **and that this is the trouble with a box — it is not wrong, it is just not a person.** **And then she rings it**, in the middle of the market, at about the sixth hour, in front of about nine hundred people, on her own account, unannounced, not looking at him: her own handbell, no wider than her palm, the crown re-cut inside with a riffle file mark at four points, once, and it is the loudest thing in that square for about nine seconds. She says one thing at ten feet to a man about nine feet off who has a slip in his coat: **you have got a box for the person who repeats things and I am the person who repeats things, and I would like it noticed that I chose a market, and a market is where I am known and a counter is where I am not.** And about nine hundred people look at her, **and that is the whole cost, and she paid it on purpose, and he did not try.** He comes over at about the seventh hour: *you did not tell me.* *Because you would have said it would be a good idea and you would have been right and it would have been on the page before the second stroke.* He does not argue. *I am not going to thank you for it.* *Good. And I am not going to let it be a thing that was for anybody else, and if anybody asks me in Sennet why I rang a bell in a market, the answer is that there was a man with a box and I did not want to be in it.* The entry on the stone: **a box is cheaper than a witness and a witness can refuse**; **a form can count a thing that a person did and cannot make anybody do it, and that is why the form wins and it wins against me every time, and I have been beating people at markets for eleven years with an instrument that costs more and lasts less and I have been calling that a victory because I have not been in a room**; **I rang a bell, mine, once, and about nine of them will describe me in four counties inside a year, and nobody has asked me why and nobody is going to**; **nobody has asked me about my ear in ninety-two days, written down twice now and not a third**; and **he has written that he has got a name for it and has not written the name, and I have not asked him for it, and that is the first thing in about two months that either of us has had and has not asked for.**

## Chapter 0107 — Clause Seven

Day 93, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a market of about two thousand, a trestle, the edge of the square. He has been carrying the heading of a standing instruction for ninety-two days — read once on the thirty-second day of the month, in a room in another county, standing up — and the heading is *Standing instruction of the district, in the matter of the coming Renewal, for the use of all officers and clerks in the district, and for the information of such persons as may lawfully be informed.* **The number is not the proof. The proof is a document and a date. So he goes and gets the document — off a table, in the open, on a trestle two women had left over from a pitch of needles, which he pays for, and about four hundred people watch — and puts out three sheets: the printed Crown instruction; a district's own form in a second county nine days after the instruction, referring to a clause by its number and not by its words; and a second district's own form in a third county, in a hand about a year younger, doing the same. **And he gets the order of the two dated sheets wrong in public, and a man in the market corrects him: that is the earlier one, and the paper in his other hand is a fair copy of the standing instruction made by a clerk nine days afterwards for a man who wanted to read it, and a fair copy of a Crown sheet is Crown property from the moment it is made, and the man who made it is not in the square and does not know he is holding it.** The officer is **Garvin Lyle**, district of Rill and Fen, who gives his name in a market of about two thousand because he would rather the stranger had it off his mouth than go and get it out of a building, and because in a fortnight he will be able to say he did not say it, and who asks for the stranger not to write his county's name down — **four things is a man and not a form, and I would like it noticed that I thought about that on a road for about a mile** — and who is here because a Crown thing in a cradle went down the road four days behind. **Nobody takes the sheets.** About nine people put a hand on a line — not a copy, a hand — and about four hundred say no in about nine minutes, and about nine say *that is the district's business and it is not ours*, and **about two hundred people do not go home and wait: about nine of them get any sheet at all and stand about nine feet off the trestle and say their own names and their own business out loud, about two hundred times in about half an hour, and he writes down the time and not one name.** At about the seventh hour he reads it out, flatly, with nothing on it that is not on the paper: the heading; that it is dated and numbered and Crown paper and that he got it from a man who copied it out for nothing and is not thanked; **that it has eleven clauses, that six of them are about things he has never heard of, that he is not going to guess at one of them, and that the day a man starts guessing at six in a market is the day the other five stop being any use to anybody**; **and clause seven: that in the course of the Renewal, such persons as are entered on no roll in the district, being children, be taken and held to the uses of the district, and their names be brought onto the roll**; and the two forms referring to clause seven by number, one of them dated about a year after the notice was pinned in his ward and about a week after his bell rang. And the last of it plainly: **a document is a thing I can be right about, and being right about it is the thing I have spent nine weeks learning to stop wanting; I have been right about a document eleven times and it has not once done anything.** Day-book: **a clause, and not a plot, and not a man** — it does not say who struck it, who holds the names afterwards, or what happens to a child once taken, **and I am not going to write down the sentence I would like and I am not going to go looking for it in the six I cannot read, because the six are a stranger's children too**; **a clause stops a form entering a household and it does not stop a person in a market, and a man with a form can stand nine feet off and say a true thing and not use it**; **about two hundred people did not go home and wait, and it is not mine and I did not persuade anybody and I am not going to be the man who says it was him**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung**, and **thirteen documents, unchanged, and a printed instruction I cannot put in a coat, and for the first time in ninety-three days that is not a loss.**

## Chapter 0108 — A Notice In A Man's Own Name

Day 94, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, a market of about two thousand on a market day with about two hundred people at the board, a board under a roof, a wall opposite, the edge of the square. He expected an officer and got a nail. **The notice: a hearing at the upper room of the Quiet Office in Sennet on the third day of the month following at the second hour, in the matter of a claim made in public in the hundred of Wendle and Ashmoor by a person named — *VALE, of the bellhouse in Kells, in the county of Kells, being a man who has no standing entered in this district or in any district of the Crown, and who is therefore named here in a capacity assigned to him by the office for the purpose of this hearing only, which capacity is that of a person who has made a claim.* Any person having in his keeping a paper relating to the matter is to bring it and produce it there. *A person having no such paper is not required to account for the absence of one, and nothing in his possession is to be taken from him on account of this notice. The office has not been able to serve this notice upon the person named, for the reason given above. It is posted.*** He reads it four times, steps away and lets about two hundred people read it, and then reads the last three lines out loud to anybody who wants to hear them, and about nine read it in pieces and a woman at a pitch of butter says *posted* twice. Then the finding, and it is the third time in ninety-four days that a clause he asked for has come back at him from a direction he did not think of: **a person with a standing can be served — you take the sheet to the man, he signs, there is a date and two hands; a man with no standing cannot, because a service needs somewhere for the answer to go, and the whole of the fifth clause is *there is nowhere*. So the office posted it, and posting is a nail and a board and four hundred people in a market and no hands at all. I asked for that clause because I was right, and a form cannot answer for a household because I have no standing to be a household, and a Crown sheet cannot be put in my hand for the same reason, and the same emptiness did both, and it has taken me ninety-four days to find out that the price of an empty form is that you also cannot be told anything quietly. The fifth clause is not a wall, it is a hole, and I built it.** And the second half: **a person who has made a claim** is the capacity the Office invented, and **he has thirteen documents and not one of them is about him** — a woman's return, four sheets from Fourmile, a deputy's answer, an auditor's sentence, a clerk's sentence, five receipts for sheets that are not his, a river-post receipt, a letter refusing him, a leaf of unknown hand, a page that belongs to a household of five, a question and an answer in two hands — and **the notice says he is not required to account for the absence of one, and a man who is not required to account for the absence of a thing cannot be asked to produce it, and a hearing is a room where a man is asked to produce things.** He sits on a wall opposite for about an hour and a half; at about the seventh hour a woman he does not know puts a cup of something beside him and does not say a word and goes away. Day-book: **the law that put it on a board is a clause I asked a clerk to write into a receipt because I was right**; **I have now paid the same price from the other side and I did not get a choice of the side and that is what ninety-four days is**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung, nothing taken, and no clause broken by any of it**, and **thirteen documents, and a printed sheet nailed to a board that I am not allowed to be given a copy of, and I have not asked for one and I could not have carried it, and I want it written down that I did not want it, which is not the same as not wanting it.**

## Chapter 0109 — Two Kinds Of Paper

Day 95, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, Sennet — a wharf on the flat, a market, the middle terrace, an arch at the foot of the top, a step. He comes in at about the fourth hour and the first thing he notices is the smell, the second is the noise, and the third is that **there are bells on the flat and none of them is sounding** — on wharves, on corners, over yard gates, on a gantry, on two net-loft frames — **and they are waiting, and there are about nine thousand people in this city and about nine hundred bells in it, and he has never in thirty-three years stood in a place where bells did not sound and were not broken.** The counter is under an arch at the foot of a stair, about nine feet long, three clerks, open from the fourth hour to the sixth, then a barred shutter. **The notice on the wall says *any person may bring a paper to this counter and ask what it is*, and it does not say any person may ask a question, and it does not say any person may ask for a thing, and the difference between those two sentences is four inches long and it is the whole of this chapter.** He asks the seventh time he has asked it — *what is the document* — and **Perrin Lowe**, about nine years, gives the same sentence in about nine seconds, because he has heard it four or five times a week for nine years: a record of the answer given, entered against the line, in the order in which the answers were given; not a household's answer, not a person's answer, not a copy of either; the office obtains an answer from a person and the entry is made by the office. **And then he gives the rest of it unasked, because four people behind them have brought papers and it is the sixth hour: *there are two kinds. The first is what I said. And the second kind is not kept in this building, it is not my business, it is not in this arch and I have not been upstairs and I am not going to be. The second kind is a thing that says it for you.*** It is a fitting, an instrument, or a box with a handle, issued out of a store against a district instruction with a number, and **when it is fitted the answer is made for you, and the answer it makes is in your own voice and it is not a lie**, and he has never had one in his hand and has never been asked about them four times in nine years and has never thought about them four times in nine years. **Where: a store, every district has one, ours is in the top terrace, four streets and four flights up, and it is not his building and he will not walk a stranger to it, because a counter takes papers and not people, and in about four months there would be nine strangers coming up and he would be the man who walked the first one, and he has not got the standing to be that man and neither has Tomas.** He sets down a printed slip — district, store's number, form — **and the form is the office's and has to be filled in by a person with a standing in a district, and he has not got one either.** **There is no box on the form for a question.** Then the last of it, with the shutter half down: about four thousand people a year bring him a paper, not one has ever asked for the second kind, and about four people in eleven years have tried to get a clerk to fill in a form for them and about two of them tried it on him — **and that they are all frightened of it, and that being frightened of it is the correct thing to be, and that nobody has ever said so out loud and that is why nobody has ever been able to write a form about it.** He comes round the end of the counter and shuts the shutter. Day-book: **I have carried a door for fifty-six days and it has a second leaf and I did not know, and a sentence that is true and incomplete is worse to carry than a wrong one, because a wrong one can be thrown out** — and **I have been walking about in this kingdom for nine weeks as though I had a thing nobody else had**; **a town of about nine hundred bells and not one sounding, and every one of them waiting for an hour and a man and a reason that is written down in a room**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung**, the frame in no building, **thirteen documents, and a printed slip that is the Office's, and a form I cannot fill in, and carrying an unfilled form is not the same as a plan and I know that.**

## Chapter 0110 — The Third Terrace

Day 96, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, Sennet — the foot of a stair, the top terrace, a yard, a gate, a board, a step. He leaves the barrow at the foot of the stair because a barrow is not a thing a man takes up four flights, and nobody asks him and nobody stops him. **The top terrace is four long buildings, a rail, a yard, and a bell about as big as a cartwheel on a frame over a gate about nine feet high, with a bench under it, and about two hundred people standing in the yard at about the sixth hour because it is the hour the bell goes, and he had not known they would come out.** **A man in a town of two thousand walks into a market. A man in a city of nine thousand comes out into his own yard at the hour, and that is not a fact about bells and he is writing it down wrong on purpose so that he has to look at it again.** It rings four times, the yard makes no noise, and a shutter goes up and a man turns a plate over on a board and about two hundred people read it. He gets up the last flight and reads it at nine feet: **a notice that by the fourth hour today a paper or instrument of which the person named is the keeper, of a kind used to repeat an answer, being of the kinds entered in the store list of the district, is to be produced; that the person named has no standing in this district or any district and this notice is posted and is not carried; and that the store list is open to inspection at the counter on the third hour of the day named in the hearing notice.** He looks for the name and it is his own, third line down, with nothing above it and nothing below it. **And the notice is the ordinary shape, it is correct, and every word of it is true** — and then the finding: **the reason there is no form about the second kind is not that everybody is frightened of it. It is that a form is a thing you fill in with a reason, and there is no reason anybody is allowed to write, and I have had nine weeks of finding out that a reason is the hardest thing in this kingdom to get anybody to put their name to.** And then: **and there was no such thing as the Office.** There are about nine hundred rooms — a store on this terrace, a sheet on a table and two sheets in a court, a man with a book and nine years in a village of two hundred, a board under a roof in a town of eleven hundred, a board at the foot of this stair with his name on it — **and the same six words on the same kind of sheet at the head of all of them, and a number, and a date, and a form with four boxes and none of them the words. A name is a thing you can be refused. A format is not a name, and a format does not have a door, and a format cannot be refused, and I have spent ninety-six days learning how to ask a person and be refused and I have got here and there is nothing at the end of it to ask.** And **he still does not know how an office gets into a bell** — a description of a thing is not a thing, and he is not going to write down the name of a fifth thing he has not seen. **And there is a second leaf on the Office's own board, about ninety feet from the gate: *Read the store list. Not the room.* Six words, her hand, and a date and a store number on the back — which means she knows the number of the store and the day of the hearing and has put both on a public board in a capital city, and the gate was not shut and the gate is not guarded and there is a man at the foot of the stair who would have walked a stranger up four flights and did not. He stands at nine feet for about four minutes and then goes down the four flights, and writes that he wanted to go in more than he has wanted anything since the seventy-seventh, and that a man who walks into a building at a quarter to seven in the evening with a hearing in seven days and no paper to produce, about a woman who has told him twice to ask the board, **is not doing anything to the woman** — and that in Miremarket he went back to a door after a household had refused him twice and it cost that household the front of a row of nine pitches. Day-book: **the Office is a format and it does not have a door**; **nobody in this city is wicked, and about four thousand people a year take a paper to a counter and are frightened of the second kind, and being frightened of it is the correct thing to be, and nobody has ever written a form about it**; all five clauses stand, **nothing was rung in ninety-six days**, four bolts, **the frame has not been in a building since the thirty-third**, the bell in the sack untouched since the seventy-seventh, his own bell in a sealed yard in Kells, **thirteen documents, unchanged, and a printed slip that is the Office's, and a form he cannot fill in, and a leaf on two boards that he has not taken and is not going back for**; and a woman ten feet off his left shoulder reading it from behind him, **and the fourth column has two lines in it and she has not improved them and I have not asked her what they say and I have wanted to every hour since the eighty-third and I am not going to, and that is the only thing I have got in ninety-six days that I have not had before, and it is the shape of a thing and not a thing, and I am not going to call it progress.**

## Chapter 0111 — The Flat, And Nine Hundred Bells Waiting

Day 97, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, Sennet — the flat below the water, the wharves, a net-loft. He comes down onto the flat because the flat is where you come down to when there is nothing to go up to, and the frame goes on the barrow in its cradle in the open under a tarpaulin bought for a penny, **four bolts made fast, and the second clause of his own receipt is the reason he cannot pay for anything anywhere in this kingdom.** **Nine bells in a walk of about a mile — four over gates, two on frames, one on a gantry, one on a granary gable, one on a net-loft frame with a rope nine feet long worn shiny in three places where a hand goes — and not one of them sounding, and not one of them broken, and he is eleven miles and a ford and a store he did not stop at from a county where bells sound.** He asks four people about the top terrace and gets four nothings that are not the same nothing: *nothing but offices*; a boy who runs; a carter with three horses, **no, not for nothing**; and a man in a doorway with a tally stick, **you could ask, there is nobody up there I would ask** — which is true and is not a refusal, and **the sum of nine thousand separate decisions is a wall and nobody is in charge of it.** He works a grain wharf about two hours, nine trips and then eleven, for nothing: **a man who can carry is free, free labour on a wharf in a city of nine thousand is not remarkable and gets no thanks, which is the correct arrangement.** The man is **Nevin Holt**, thirty-one years on the flat, who on the tenth trip — the trip on which a man talks — tells him about his own four bells. The granary gable one is not his and on a shut day it is iron on a frame. The gantry has a rope and a boy and the boy is not there today. The net-loft one is why he has a place to sleep and is struck by whoever is on the loft and had not been since the rope was new. **There are about nine he can see and he has counted them about nine times in nine years and has never written it down, and if asked how many bells this city has he would say he does not know, and he would not be lying.** And then the sentence of the wharf: ***a bell is a man standing next to it — the man and the hour and the reason; take the man away and it is iron*** — and Tomas writes it down and does not believe it and writes down that he does not believe it, and **does not know for about two days that he was wrong.** Then the finding: **it is about the hour.** A man in a town of two thousand walks into a market; a man in a city of nine thousand comes out into his own door at the hour. **About nine hundred of them are waiting and the count of them is a thing nobody keeps, because there is nothing at the other end of it to be counted against, and there is no roll of the waiting bells and no form and no column anywhere for what they are for.** He gets the whole of the Office out of Holt in nine seconds flat while counting sacks: **four buildings, about nine hundred rooms, a yard, and a bell over a gate that goes at the sixth hour, and the men go home at the sixth hour** — and then, unasked: ***everything in it is duller than the telling, a man up there decides a thing and it goes on a board and the board goes on a cart about eleven thousand times a year, and not one of the eleven thousand is a man anybody could be angry at.*** Day-book: **a city is not a town scaled up**; **I came here for a face and I have found the opposite of one and it is not a comfort and it is not the good news I have been telling myself**; **they came out for the hour, the bell is what the hour has instead of a man going round with a stick, and I have had that the wrong way round for two days**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung, and I slept under a bell with a rope within reach of my hand all night and did not touch it**, four bolts, the frame out of doors, the carrier in the sack untouched, twenty days, **thirteen documents, and a man's name and a place to sleep written in the same breath with them so that nobody can add them up and get fourteen**; and a woman ten feet off his left shoulder, **the count of the column is still eight and a thing that is not done is not a thing that has been done.**

## Chapter 0112 — A Counter That Takes Papers And Not People

Day 98, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, Sennet — the middle terrace, the arch at the foot of the top, the river courts, the flat, a net-loft. He goes up with a form he cannot fill in and **works out standing in the queue that he has not got a question: the one question he walked into this city with was *what is the document*, he spent it on the ninety-fifth, and a clerk gave him the whole of it in two halves unasked and shut the shutter afterwards, which is a thing a clerk does. You cannot walk into a building twice with the same question; by the second time it is a thing you are making people hear again.** The counter is nine feet long with three clerks and about nine clerks in the room over the arch, and **the difference between the three and the nine is the difference between people who answer and people who wait.** The notice has been repainted, and somebody has gone over the last word — *it* — twice, which is not a thing anybody does to a notice that has not been argued with. **Perrin Lowe is at the end of it and gives him nothing and is not sorry**: *I am not going to help you find a way round to the other thing; I told you it is a fitting, I have not got one and do not want one, and the fact that I told you is the last thing I am going to add to it*; and then, unasked, **the two halves of the volume's standing emptiness, both of them the fifth clause and both of them Tomas's own: a Crown notice about him is nailed up because it could not be served, and a form cannot be filled in for him and is therefore carried unfilled** — and *the man who named you invented a capacity for you so that he could order you to produce a thing, and he was right to.* Then **there is no box on any of them for a question, and the box for a reason wants a person with a standing in a district, and there is nobody in that arch who could put a reason in it for him even if he asked and he would not and he would be right.** And then he does the only instrument he has, which is the one he has had for nine weeks and has been throwing away: he does not ask a question, he asks **to read a board.** The river courts are up four steps through a door propped with a bucket, and **the board is not a notice-board: no number, no district, no date in a hand, three columns, the third a column of hours, and it is a list of what people have sworn to in open court, and anybody may read it.** About nine rows going back years, every kind of person — a woman who keeps a ferry, sworn to her ropes and the hour she counted them; a man sworn to a debt and the day it was struck; a woman sworn that a boundary stone had not moved; two men sworn to a horse — **and not one name on that board is a disgrace, and that is the finding, and it took him about four minutes.** **Coll Salkeld**, six years in that hall, gives it in four seconds: *a court is not an office; an office files; a court publishes, and the reason a court publishes is that a court is a room people are sent to, and a room people are sent to has to be a room they can be seen in — we have a board on that wall because a man is a person and not a line.* And the three requirements, unasked: **you say it out loud in your own voice; you say it about yourself and not about a household; and a person here hears it and writes it down. And there is no reason and no box for a reason, because a person swearing to a thing about themselves does not owe anybody a why.** **He found a door in this kingdom with no reason-box on it, it is four inches wide, it is open, and he did not go through it — because he has nothing true and his own to swear, and if he had gone through it with something false it would have been on a board and about forty people a week would have read his name against it, and that is the part he thought about afterwards.** And the standing reflex is named: whenever a building shows him a way of reading a person's own sworn word, he thinks about hers — **and he has not gone, in twelve days, and he is not going to on this trip, and nobody in this city has asked him for it and he is not going to hand it to a stranger who has been kind to him twice.** Day-book: **a counter takes papers and not people, and a court publishes and an office files, and the difference is four inches long, and neither building has ever heard of the other one**; **I have thirteen things that are true and eleven other people's, and a court wants a sentence about the man standing in the room, and there was no man in the room, there was a coat with thirteen papers in it**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung by anybody**, the frame out of doors, the carrier untouched, twenty-one days, **thirteen documents, and a public board with nine rows on it and not one of them mine.**

## Chapter 0113 — What A Wharf Knows About A Wire

Day 99, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the flat, a grain wharf, a loft floor, a net-loft. The wharf gate bell is on its frame, sound, unbroken, and cannot be rung from anywhere except under the frame, and the man who used to ring it from the granary loft is standing there with a hand on a rope that does not go anywhere. Holt: **a wire, cut on the eighth by the district's men, on the trench, lawfully, and I have not seen a paper and have not wanted one.** A trench has been along the wharf road about a fortnight and is going to be a drain; the district's notice said **wires** and not *wires belonging to anybody*, **and there is no form in this kingdom on which a person may object to a wire being cut, because a wire is not a house and not a thing anybody keeps.** **A bell is not a wire.** The bell on the gate went on being a bell every day since the eighth, did not know, and has not changed. Then the labour, stated flatly and without cruelty: **a man who has not slept and is holding a barrow is cheaper than a man who is** — nine tons, four hours, a hundred and eleven trips, **his hands stopped being hands somewhere about the sixth hour and he did not notice it happen, and there is no craft in this novel that is worth more than that.** He does not ask about the wire. He asks about the cut, and says what it is before he is asked, the way a maker does: **not a break — cut, from above, a clean face and a burr on the underside where it started to tear as it went, and the tool was not a chisel and it was not a spade.** Holt watched the man look up first to see who was watching. Then he is offered a soldered mend — two turns, a solder, a wipe of pitch, nine years' service — and refuses it, and gives the reason, and it is the finding of the chapter: **a wire that snaps is a fault and a fault is a thing that goes in a book; a wire cut by the district and joined by the district is a repair, and there is no fault in it anywhere, and the only paper in the world about that wire is the notice, and it said the bell will not sound on that day, and it was right, and I have not got one grain of complaint against anybody. If I put a solder on it myself then I have a bell with a mend in the line that is not in any book and cannot be entered in any book, and there is no form in this kingdom on which a man may say I have mended my own wire and here it is, and so I have got a fault nobody can see, and a fault nobody can see is a fault I have to be asked about some morning by somebody with a reason. So I want them to cut it. I want to be nine places down a list of about thirty, and I want it to be the ninth day, and I will stand here with nine tons on the floor and a cart on the road and a bell on a frame that will not do anything. And the day after that it is a wire in a drain, and I could not tell you in a court this afternoon whether it had ever been cut at all.** Then the shape of the whole of it, out of a man who has never been in it: **a bell you can blame. A wire you cannot.** About nine hundred bells with about nine hundred people near them who can be asked and can refuse; about nine hundred wires with nobody near them that can be asked nothing and have never had to. **Everything he has been afraid of in a hundred and seven days is a wire and a column and a habit, and none of those three is a person, and that is the relief and it is not a comfort, because a thing with no person in it cannot be answered, and he has come four counties and seventy miles to answer things.** He goes back over his own book and **corrects Day 97 in his own hand with nobody asking him to**: he wrote *a bell is a man standing next to it* and wrote that he did not believe it, and **he was wrong to disbelieve it, and the reason is a wire.** He does not read the foreman's list in the road and writes down that he did not, twice, for two reasons. Day-book: **a cut wire does not stop a bell, it stops a wire, and the bell on the other end of it does not know and goes on being a bell**; **he is waiting nine days for a repair he asked for and could do himself in an hour, and it is the most correct decision anybody has made in four counties in this volume, and he is not going to be thanked for it by me, because he did not do it for me**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung, nothing touched, and I said what the cut was and was not asked to and would have said it again**; **the printed instruction I carried off a trestle on the ninety-third is Crown paper and cannot go in a coat, and a fair copy of Crown paper is the Crown's from the moment it is made**; and the Office is **a format and it is also a drain, and I have got both halves now and neither of them is a room.**

## Chapter 0114 — The List, And The Man Behind The Counter

Day 100, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the top terrace, a store, the foot of a stair. He goes up four flights at about the third hour and is not stopped at any of them, **and has written down that this is the second building he has gone into in this city and that both times nobody stopped him, and that he is not going to make a habit of noticing it, because a man who keeps a count of how often he is not refused is a man who is waiting to be refused.** The room is about eleven feet by sixteen: a counter, a book open on it, a man, and a shelf up the back wall and another under the window, **and about nine hundred things in a city like this and a store like this, and he did not see one of them and was not shown one.** The man is **Osmund Tarrant**, nine years, and he is not slow. He reads the slip out of Tomas's coat before Tomas has it out. **A store is this: a district has one, somebody in a room brings an instruction, the instruction has a number and a district and a day, I go along the shelf, I find the thing, I give it to the man who brought it, and I write in this book what I gave out and the number and the district and the day.** And then, unasked and while he writes: **there is a column in this book between the fourth and the fifth, headed what the thing is for, and it has been empty every day I have been in this room, and it was empty before me and it is not going to be filled in after me, and I would like it written down by somebody, and I have been waiting nine years for somebody to write it down.** A smith two counties back has said the same thing in four seconds: **a column is a thing two men say the same thing about and never find out.** And Tarrant's reason for not filling it in, in four seconds: **I was told when I came into this room that the column was for a reason. Nobody has told me what the reason is in nine years. I do not fill in a column in a book I keep on behalf of a district for a reason I cannot answer for, and if a man came in here tomorrow and asked what is in this column I could not stand in front of him and say I put it there. It is not a secret and it is not a conspiracy. It is a man who will not write down a word he cannot read back.** Tomas puts that beside Abner Tole's unruled column in a village and writes down in his own head, not the book, that **there are two kinds of empty column in this kingdom and they are made of the same thing.** Then Tarrant **offers him a printed sheet** — free, about nine hundred a year in a drawer, going in the fire, nothing on it about him, and nobody would ever know — and Tomas puts both hands flat on the counter and refuses in four seconds and gives the reason out loud: **on the eighty-first, in a room over a draper's shop, a man in a form and a satchel took five sheets off a table in about three hours, receipted one at a time, and every part of it was lawful, and the law that let it happen was a clause of a receipt I asked a clerk to add. A paper off a table in a room and a paper off a public board in a market town with a drawing-pin in it are the same act with a different name, and I have got exactly one life, and there is nobody in this room who could account for that sheet for me.** Tarrant puts it back without expression: **you are the third person in nine years who has refused one; the other two were women and one of them was right and one was not.** Then Tomas asks for the shelf and is refused correctly in four seconds: **a store shows a shelf to an instruction and you have not got one, and if I opened that wall for a man with a barrow today then in about four months there would be nine, and I would be the man who opened it the first time, and there is a clerk four streets and four flights below me who will not walk a stranger up and he is right and I have heard of that and I do not know his name and would not use it if I did.** And the sentence of the hundredth: **whatever is on that wall came off it on somebody else's instruction and went into somebody else's hands, and the district's business in this room is to know what went out and when, and I have never pretended to anybody that I could answer the other thing.** Then the board-date question, which Tarrant flattens: **that list is open at the third hour every morning this store is open and has been for nine years, whether or not a district has written a day on a board. A list that is always open is not a thing a district can put a date against, and a form has four boxes and one of them is a day.** And the thing that is not open: **about nine hundred things a year, every one shut, and the way it is shut is that nobody may look without an instruction.** And the sentence he has gone over in his own head about once a month for nine years: **a man is not allowed to be angry at a list of wires. You can be angry at a man. You cannot be angry at a thing that came off a wall on somebody's instruction, because there is nobody in it, and if there is nobody in it then there is nobody to be angry at, and so the list is always open and the wall is always shut, and both of those are correct, and I have never once been able to make them both correct at the same time in my own head.** Then he stands at the column for an hour and reads it **empty from end to end, nine hundred times a year, for the whole nine years of the four books on that counter** — and understands that **the answer to a question he has carried fifty-eight days is a column, and it is not a secret, and it is not a conspiracy, and it is not a wall, and he cannot use it.** He notices he is inside a building and that **there is no clause about where a man may be and one about where a cradle may be, and the two do not have anything to do with each other.** Day-book: **a store is a room, a counter, a book, a man, a shelf and an instruction, and I have got all six in one room and have seen none of the six actually do anything**; **about nine hundred a year go out of that room into about nine hundred pairs of hands and not one of the nine hundred people carrying one has ever stood where I stood this morning**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung, nothing shown, nothing named**, **thirteen documents, and a printed slip that is the Office's, a form I cannot fill in, a sheet I was offered and refused in four seconds, and the total is still thirteen.**

## Chapter 0115 — The List Is Open At A Counter

Day 101, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, the top terrace, a store, a trestle, the foot of a stair. The list is on the counter at about the third hour. It is nine sheets and he reads every line of all nine in about two hours. **Nobody asked him who he was. Nobody asked him for a standing, a paper, a district, a reason or a name, and nobody refused him any of those either, because nobody thought of asking — and this is the first time in a hundred and one days that no office has asked him who he is, and he has written that down twice because it is the cheapest thing that has happened to him and the only one he cannot argue with.** The columns are the same five: number against instruction, district, day, the thing in a word, **and what the thing is for, empty.** He reads the fourth column end to end on all nine sheets: about ninety different words, and **he can tell you how many words are in that column and what a column is for and he cannot tell you what one single one of the ninety things is — not because the man behind the counter is keeping it from him, but because the fifth column is empty, and it is empty because a man will not write down a word he cannot read back, and he is right.** Then his one question, and it is not the question he came up four flights with: **how many of these go out of this room in a year.** About nine hundred, all of them, every line on those nine sheets, **every one carried out of that door by a different man, and not one of the nine hundred has come back into this room in nine years to say where it went — four came back, broken, out of warranty, entered as a repair, and those four are the only four lines in nine years with anything after them.** And then, standing at that counter, he understands the whole of what he came up here for: **the notice on the top terrace says he is the keeper of a paper or instrument of a kind used to repeat an answer, being of the kinds entered in the store list, and the bell in the cradle has a coupling cut nearly through and a jumper soldered across it, and a smith said there is a store and a clerk said there is a fitting, and neither of them said what it is — and here are ninety words and he can read every one of them and there is no way on this earth for him to find out whether the word for what is in that frame is one of the ninety, because the list does not say what any of the ninety is for, and the reason is a column and a rule, and the rule is a good one.** Then the chapter's real action: **he does not ask what the thing is for.** He writes down on a leaf in his own hand, with the hour, that he did not ask, and he writes down the reason, and the reason is not noble and is not a rule: **a man asked that has three answers and all three of them cost him something — *I do not know* is a thing he will have said in a room with a district's book open in front of him; *that is for the district* is a form of words with nothing in it that he has been reading off about nine hundred men in four counties; and there is no third answer, because there is no true third answer, and a man does not say that twice. So I would have been handed a sentence to carry, and that is the thing I have been refusing to be handed for fifty-nine days, and refusing it is a thing I can do on purpose and I did it on purpose and nobody knows except me and a woman ten feet off my shoulder who was not asked.** Then the three nines, written out in a row because it is the sentence he will want most in a month: **about nine hundred rooms, about nine hundred bells, and about nine hundred entries on nine sheets. They are three different nines and nothing in the kingdom says they are the same nine and nothing says they are not, and I have been writing about nine hundred for three days as though it were one number. A number that can only be told apart by which sheet it is on is a filing reference, and I have been told that about a man four days back in a market and did not listen to it.** He leaves with nothing: no sheet, no number on a leaf, no copy, no line. **A list of fittings is not a document he carries as evidence, and the number of non-bell documents in a coat is thirteen this evening and it was thirteen when he came into this city five days ago, and both figures are correct and neither is the other one, and a man who adds a store list to a coat of papers is doing the thing this whole business has been about.** Day-book: **nobody in this kingdom knows what those ninety things are for**; **about nine hundred objects and nine hundred people and about nine hundred wires, and not one person among the nine hundred could be asked about the wire — that is a sentence about a store and it is the seed of something I cannot see the shape of yet**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung**, four bolts, the carrier untouched, twenty-four days, **thirteen documents, and I looked at nine sheets and took nothing, and a list of fittings is not a paper, and a number is not a reason.**

## Chapter 0116 — Two People Who Will Write A Thing Down

Day 102, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tamsin Rook's POV**, Sennet — the middle terrace, a market of about nine hundred, a pitch, the river courts, a step by the river with her boots off. **The middle terrace market is on three streets and it is about nine hundred people on a good morning and it is on the ground, which is the first thing she noticed, because eleven years of markets have been on hills. You cannot see anybody from the top of it.** She is in it with her slate, price side the other way up, and she is not working. She is there to find out one thing: **can a person be asked to write down a true thing about themselves in a public room, and what does it cost — not the asker, the person who has it written down.** Then the leash is **tested in the open and refused and the refusal is on the page with a reason.** A woman at a pitch of needles and thread offers it to him, in front of about nine people, **and she tells him in front of all nine that he has gone up that stair three times in four days with a barrow and that his name is at the third line of the sheet nailed at the top of it and that there is a hearing on it tomorrow, **and he says no in front of all nine, and gives the reason out loud without stopping in the middle of it to see whether it was working, and makes it about him not having a right and not about her at all: he has no standing and she has nothing to stand on and he would be asking her to spend the only thing she has in a room where he would be the only person who heard it.** And then she says the reason, once, out loud, at ten feet, to nine people and not to him: **because she said it in front of nine people, and a question said in front of nine people is a thing I will be asked again in four counties inside a year by somebody who heard it offered and not answered. I have had one of those already. I priced it in a smith's shop on the fifty-first and it took eleven years of work and it is not a thing I am pricing again in a market because somebody thinks it would be useful.** And: **he said the reason out loud in front of nine people and he did not make it about me. He made it about him not having a right. Nobody has said a thing like that in front of nine people for me in eleven years, and I am not going to be grateful about it in a market, and I am not going to let it be a thing that was for anybody else. Nobody asked me about my ear. Not one of the nine, and not one of about nine hundred people between the fourth hour and the ninth. He did not ask. It was not a ninth use. The count is eight. A thing that is not done is not a thing that has been done, and I have written both halves down because I keep wanting the other half and the other half would be easier and would be a lie.** Then the instrument, and the finding: a court will write a true thing down and keep it, fourpence, forty years, and it moves nothing — **a thing said twice at ten feet in a market is heard by the people standing there, dies with them and cannot be produced by anybody; a thing written down in a court can be produced against you by a stranger you have never met. I have been doing the expensive one and the unproducible one and I have thought of it as the strong one, and I was half right, and the half I was wrong about is the half that keeps.** And then the harder half: **everything I have ever done needed me in it. My voice, my sentence, my hands, my slate, and a person to hear it, and I chose which person, and there has never been one single occasion in eleven years on which a thing I had said came back to me as a thing somebody else had said.** And then it happened. **Two people who have never spoken to her did it, on their own day, without being asked, and she was nine feet off and was not in the room for either of them:** **Ord Marren**, nine rows in that market for nine years, the rent put on those nine rows is the rent put on the wholesale stalls at the end of the row, and he has said so out loud at his pitch perhaps a hundred times in nine years and not one person in this city has ever said it back to him, including his landlord, who does not need to; and **Wil Brand**, who lets barrows out of a yard near the water, holds a licence eleven years, the licence says what a yard may not do, his yard has been doing the thing the licence says every day for eleven years, it is not a trick, it is his whole trade, and there is no room in this kingdom with a clerk in it where he is allowed to say so, and he has wanted one for eleven years. **AND HE IS NOW ON STAGE, which he was not before the review pass: four lines at the yard rail, he does not look up, he looks up about a second afterwards and says nothing, turns his left hand over on the rail, takes a stub of chalk out of his apron, writes *eleven years* on the back of his own hand, puts the chalk away and goes back to the barrow — which is not a document, is in his own name and his own hand, and will be off his own skin in about a week, and precedes his fourpence by three days.** And all she did was **say their own sentences back to them — four inches, at ten feet, in the ordinary voice — and then go and stand about nine feet further off and say nothing else to either of them for the rest of the day.** Her reason for asking neither of them: **a man who is asked to put his own true hand to a thing will say yes out of a market to a stranger he thinks has a use for him, and a man who is not asked, and who has just heard his own sentence come back at him in his own words at ten feet, has to decide on his own, alone, on his own day, in his own yard — and a decision a man takes alone is his and cannot be anybody else's.** On their own days, in their own places, both went to the court on the middle terrace and had a true sentence about their own business entered in their own names, in open court, on the same public wall; Marren's is the third row, Brand's the fourth; **each cost fourpence, moves nothing at all, and will be there when she is gone. Neither of them is a hero. Neither is owed anything by me. I did not ask either of them to do anything, I have not spoken to either of them since, and neither of them knows I said it.** She rang nothing today and has not rung it since the ninety-second, and **nobody asked her why and nobody is going to.** Day-book: **the road has found a place after all and it is a queue, and I worked that out on the eighty-fourth and did not act on it for eighteen days, because a queue is not a place a person stands in, it is a place two strangers can walk into at the same time, and neither of them has to be asked, and the only thing they need is to have heard their own sentence come back at them once, in their own words, in the open, in front of nine people who were not the man who said it to them. There are two. There are two, and I have their names, and I am not going to write their names in this book.** **It is not a system and it is not a network and it is not going to be a thing I hand to anybody.** And: **he did not ask, in front of nine people, and he said out loud that he had no right to, and he made it about him and not about me, and I have been on this road eleven years and nobody has ever done that in front of anybody. The count of the slate is eight. It was eight yesterday. It is eight tonight. A question offered in a market and refused in a market is not a question that was asked, and I have written that down twice now and I am going to stop writing it down, because the third time I write it I will be counting, and a woman who counts is a woman who is waiting to be owed.**

## Chapter 0117 — The Day Before A Room

Day 103, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, Sennet — a door at the top of a stair, a step at the foot, a yard, the arch, a counter. **The hearing itself is not in this chapter and the whole of what he says about that room is four minutes outside it.** He wrote five lines at about the first hour on a step and did not improve any of them: *One — what I know, made in public in a market in his own voice at about nine feet off a trestle in front of about four hundred people, and it is his.* *Two — what he does not know: what a coupling and a jumper are for, what any of the ninety words in a store's list is for because the column is empty and the man who keeps it will not fill it in and he is right, and who takes a name off a line.* *Three — his own fault: he has no standing in any district, he put a matter into a hundred's business that was not his to put there, in the open, on a market day, about two hundred people read his name off a board afterwards and nine of them will say it in another town inside a year, he knew that, and it is his and he is not going to hand it to anybody in that room.* *Four — what he is not here for: he is not here to have anything decided, he is here so that a thing is said in a room and entered in a book with the hour against it, and he would like to be told the column it went in.* And the fifth: ***I speak for the household of my father's house in Kells, being of it, and any answer given in this room is the answer of that household and not mine alone.*** At about the second hour he stands about nine feet off a door for about four minutes: **nobody came out of the door, nobody looked at him, nothing whatever happened, and that is not a story about a door and it is a fact about a door.** He does not read the nine sheets on the board inside the rail, and writes down that he did not, because there are four boxes on all of them and none of them is the words. Then at about the third hour, in a yard at the foot of the stair, a man he has met once — **Ormond Bly**, nine years keeping a yard of packing cases, with a gate and a bell of his own that he strikes himself — says, unasked, that he has been watched standing at that door and that a woman in a stall four hundred yards off watched it too; and then: **I have been in that room twice. Once, nine years back, as a witness. I was in it for about an hour and a half and nobody asked me a single question. A witness is not summoned to produce, and I had a bag of papers in my hand that would have answered anything anybody could have asked me, and I have never found out whether I was permitted to give them or whether I was not permitted to ask.** And: **a claimant is summoned to produce, and that is the difference, and it is not a difference of truth, it is a difference of what the room wants off you, and the word the office chose is *claimant*, and the whole of that word is *produce*.** Then the finding: **a man who walks into that room with nothing in his hands is a man who will be asked *where is it*, not by a man who wants to be cruel, by a clerk with a column and a form, and there is no sentence in this kingdom for the answer to that question and no box for it, because the box wants a thing. About four thousand people a year bring a paper to a counter at the foot of these stairs and not one has ever gone up them.** And then the half he did not expect: **his own notice says in four lines that a man who has no such paper is not required to account for the absence of one, and that nothing in his possession is to be taken. Every word of it is true and it is the best sentence anybody has written for him in a hundred days, and it is a wall and a door in one sheet and it works both ways at once. It means nobody can ask him where it is, and it means nobody can ask him for it — he will not be asked one question in that room about his hands or his coat or what he has in a bag four streets away, and there is not one hearing in this kingdom where a man with nothing in his hands is asked nothing, because the point of the room is what you brought.** Then, at about the seventh hour, **he strikes the fifth line out with a pencil in one stroke in about four seconds and writes *not said* in the margin** — the second time in a hundred and three days he has written those two words, the first being a fourth column of his own book on the eighty-second, and there is no connection and he checked. **The reason is clause five of his own receipt, the clause he asked a clerk to add because he was right, and it has now done four jobs: a form stopped at a door, five sheets off a table in about three hours, a notice nailed to a board instead of going into his hand, and this afternoon his own mouth, at the only moment in a hundred and three days when he had something worth saying and there was a room to say it in.
