The price of an empty form is that when you finally get into the room, the thing you most want to say is the thing you are not allowed to say, and the room does not know it is not allowed, and it will hear him not say it and it will think he had nothing.** And then the trade finding, which is the chapter: **a claim in metal is a thing a man cannot edit — it cannot be added to, taken back, have an hour put on it, or be shown to anybody on a page; it is the one instrument he has that is not a document and nobody in any building can ask to see it. And preparing a hearing is preparing a bell. He made one in about nine minutes on a step: four lines and a bar and a keeper, and then he edited it, which is the one thing he has spent thirty-three years not doing. A bell cannot be edited after it has been rung, and he is about to stand in a room and be edited, and the editor will be right, and the form has four boxes and none of them is the words, and he has never in his life handed a thing to another maker to finish. Somebody made a thing out of him in a room with a lamp and a column, in about four minutes, and he was not in it.** He does not ask Salkeld or Bly what happened in the room; the reason is nine words long and it is the same reason as the fair copy and as Lyle's own name: **a man who says a true thing in a recorded room and then says it to a stranger has said it twice, and the second one is the one that gets used.** Day-book: **a hearing is a room where a man is asked to produce things, and a claimant is a person the room expects to have brought something, and a witness is not, and the difference is not a difference of truth**; **I struck the fifth line out**; **I have been a maker in a kingdom that has a bell on every post and a form on every counter, and this morning somebody made a thing out of me in a room with a lamp and a column and I will not be able to argue with a word of it, and the fifth clause that stops the argument is a clause I asked for**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung**, four bolts, the carrier untouched, twenty-six days, **thirteen documents, and a sheet of my own hand with four lines and a fifth struck out, and a sheet of my own hand is not a document anybody may take off me and is not a fourteenth.**

## Chapter 0118 — The Man Who Was In The Room Does The Only Thing There Is

Day 104, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, Sennet — the top terrace, a yard, a gate, a bench, a corridor, a rail, the arch, the foot of a stair. He goes up at about the fourth hour and again at the fifth and again at the sixth and **does not go in, and nobody came out of the upper room, and he has written down that he does not intend to go in today.** The yard is the same yard: about nine people cross the bench between the fourth hour and the seventh, **a man turns a plate over on a board at the sixth hour, about forty people read it, the bell rings four times, and about two hundred people in the yard let it ring and make no noise, and he stood in the middle of that and was part of it and is not going to write a paragraph about it.** He asks four people whether they were in the room yesterday: three say no, and the fourth says, in the ordinary way, **I was in the yard at the sixth hour like everybody. That is not being in the room.** And that is true and is the correct answer, and **there is no version of this in which a person has decided to keep him out of an account of it, because there is no account of it: the room happened and has gone back to being a room, and that took him about two hours to believe.** At about the seventh hour, a man he has spoken to once, in a public room three streets away on the ninety-eighth, comes up the stair and goes along the rail and does not stop: **Coll Salkeld**, a clerk of the river courts, six years in that hall, with a sheet of his own in his hand, who says the whole of it walking in about nine seconds. **You have not been in the room, so I am not going to tell you what was said in it, and I want that in the right order: I am not being careful. I would have told a man in a market in a fortnight. A man who says a true thing in a recorded room and then says it to a stranger has said it twice, and the second one is the one that is used.** Then the finding, in about nine seconds: **a hearing is a room where one person's answer, once given, is written into a column, and out of that column it comes as the answer of the whole matter — not the answer of the room, the answer of the thing the matter was about. And it is entered in about four seconds, in the correct form, out of the correct column, by a man doing his job, and the man is not choosing, and the four seconds are not the point, and I have watched that be done in a town of about two thousand and I have watched it be done here and it is the same hand and the same column and there is no difference whatever except which building you are standing in. And that is all of it.** And then the other half, and the chapter: **a man came into the river court hall yesterday at about the second hour of the morning, which is the hour that room is shut, so he had a key, and he put his hand against a line on the sworn board. Not a slip. A hand, on the board, against a line, in his own name, in the open, in front of about eleven people who had come in to swear things they had not thought of swearing in front of anybody. His name is at the head of it. He is a clerk of the upper room on this terrace. His line says what he did yesterday morning in about nine words, and it is true, and it is the only true thing on that board in about four years, and I have been a clerk six years and I have never seen anything on that wall written by a man about himself on the day he did it.** Tomas asks one question, the only one he is going to ask: **what did it cost him.** **About four days of his working life: the upper room, the counter, and about a hundred yards of corridor, and he will be at a desk in a room with no door on it until a district decides otherwise, in about four days, and the district has not decided otherwise and is not going to. And there is a paper about it in a room four streets down. It is a district's paper with four boxes, one of them is a day, the reason is in the fourth box, and what is in the fourth box is *the district's business*, which is three words and not a reason, and it is the only reason anybody is ever going to have, and it is not a lie, and it is a door closing.** **He knew before he put his hand down; nobody persuaded him; a clerk who does a thing like that is not looking for a person to notice it, and if he was he would have done it where a district could not put a paper about it, and he did it on a public wall, which is the most expensive place there is, and he is not a fool.** Then the last of it, and Salkeld will not say why: **I am not going to say why he thought it mattered, because that is nine words on a wall in his own name and it is his, and he did not put it there for me to explain. If he was wrong then it is four days. If he was right then it is four days. There is nothing in this kingdom that turns four days back into nothing, and nothing that turns four days into more than four days, and both of those are the answer and I am going down the stair now.** And Tomas does not ask, and does not follow, **and was in the arch four streets and four flights below about an hour after the door shut, asking a clerk at a counter what a district's paper with a third hour on it is for, and was told in about nine seconds, and it was nothing, and had no idea while he was being told it that a thing was happening in a public room three streets away on his account.** Then the chapter's own finding: **I have spent a hundred and four days looking for a person who is right and doing something about it. I have found eight.** And then, in full: **the act is the same act as a yard on the seventy-ninth where six men put their hands against a line in a counting house. It is a chore. A chore is a thing a person does for himself, in his own yard, with his own hands, on his own day, and a firm is not a person and cannot do one, and a district is not a person. He did a chore for himself, in a room he chose, in the form that is the room's, out of the column the room uses, in his own name. It is the only thing in this kingdom that has ever moved a line, and it cannot be done by anybody else, and it cannot be done twice by one man, and it cannot be done at all by nine hundred people. It is not scalable. It is not a network. It is not the thing I have been looking for and it is the only thing that has ever worked, and a man who says the second is the answer is the man who says a format is a room, and I have been that man for nine days and I am not going to be him tomorrow.** And underlined twice: **I am the reason there is a hearing.** A notice in his name on a board four days and four hundred miles back, a capacity invented so that a man with no standing could be ordered to produce something, a fifth clause that meant he could not be served and was posted instead, a district that wrote back and named a day and an hour and a room. **He read the sheet, he entered the column, he did his job in about four seconds, and then he paid about four days and about a hundred yards of corridor out of his own pocket, in his own name, in a public room, and nobody asked him to, and nobody is going to tell him it was worth it, and I am not going to be one of the people who tells him, and I have written that down in a book that nobody but me has read for a hundred and four days and I am not going to stop writing it down.** Day-book: **a hearing is a room where one person's answer comes out of a column as the answer of the whole matter**; **the same hand and the same column as a town of about two thousand, and the machinery four counties found is the same machinery, and it has a room, and the room is not the point**; **it is a chore, and I am not going to be the man who says this is the thing I have been looking for, because I said on the ninety-sixth that a format is a room and that was wrong, and this is the same mistake in a better coat**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung by me and nothing rung by me anywhere in a hundred and four days**, the carrier untouched, twenty-seven days, **thirteen documents, and a record of a hearing somewhere in this city that is not mine and is not in my hands and I have not asked for.**

## Chapter 0119 — The Thing That Was For A Clerk And Not For The Man

Day 105, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, Sennet — the middle terrace market, a pitch, the river courts, a counter, a rail, a step at the foot of the top. **Nobody in that market had heard of a hearing in the upper room two days ago: he asked nine people between the fourth hour and the fifth and eight said *what hearing* and the ninth said *there is one, there is always one*, and neither answer was wrong, and there had not been about four hundred people in that market at the fifth hour on the ninety-second either. A thing that happened in a room in a capital does not travel to a market in a capital. It is a room, and it is four streets off, and a market is three streets off in the other direction, and the paper that came out of the room went into a file, and a file does not walk.** And about four hundred people are saying names back to each other in a queue anyway, in the ordinary voice, at about nine feet, and about nine of them have sheets, and it is the third time in a hundred and five days he has watched a market do that, and he stands at the edge for about an hour and does not go in and does not write down one name. Then, at about the fifth hour, **a market keeper with about nine rows and a book of his own and no office told a stranger what it costs to be named, and told it about somebody else: Ord Marren.** There was a man at a board in a market four days back with a notice in his own name on it, his name at the third line, and about two hundred people read it — *that is all I know and I have not been there and I do not know the town.* **About four hundred people read it and about nine of them will say it in a different town inside a year, and the price of that is he cannot un-name himself. There is no form anywhere in this kingdom by which a man who has been named on a board can be unnamed, and there is no clerk who can do it, and I have looked, and looking is what I do.** And then the half nobody says: **the reading is the cheap part. Two hundred people reading a sheet in a market costs a district about fourpence. What costs is the about nine. A man who says a name about four times inside a year in four towns has made it into a thing, and there is no undoing it, and every one of the about nine gets it right the first time and gets it more wrong after that, and in about two years there is a version of it that has a detail in it that was never on the sheet, and by then nobody remembers which of them added it.** And the arithmetic: **four in ten of a village does not come to a hall when a bell says there is a hall, and it is the same arithmetic and it is the only arithmetic in this business.** Tomas **understood every word of it, said not one word, did not correct him, did not thank him, and did not tell him that the man he was talking about was standing four feet off him with a barrow** — because a stranger who is told *there was a man at a board in a market four days back* can ask *was it you* and go and find out and be reported, and a man who tells a stranger a true thing about a man he has not met, in the ordinary voice, in a market, and does not point, is doing the only thing in this kingdom that can be done for a person at no cost to anybody, and it is on the list with everything else that cannot be done twice by one man. And he is going to be told, later, by somebody else, that a man in a market told him, and he is not going to be told it was a kindness, because it was not a kindness, it was a man talking in a market.** Then he goes and reads the record of his own hearing, **at a court on the middle terrace, standing up, in about eleven minutes** — possible only because a court publishes: **a record of what a court has done is open to any person who asks for it, at a counter, in the ordinary way, and about four hundred people a year go into that room and have a sentence of their own written down, and about four thousand people a year take a paper to a counter at the foot of the top and ask what it is, and not one of the nine clerks there has ever been asked by any of the four thousand for the record of a hearing they were not in. Not once.** And the batch's finding: ***an office files. A court publishes. The difference between those two words is four inches long and it is the only thing in a hundred and five days that anybody in this kingdom has got wrong in their own favour, and they have got it wrong on purpose, and I do not think anybody in the Quiet Office has ever been told it is a choice. A form has four boxes. One of them is a day. There is no box for *who may read this*, and the absence of that box is the whole of the coercion and it is not in a room, it is in a form.*** It is about four hundred words. It has a heading, a date, a room, and his name spelled the way it is spelled on an L-4 schedule in a county he has left. It has the matter: *a claim made in public in the hundred of Wendle and Ashmoor by the person named, that a certain child was taken.* It has four lines about the posting, correct, quoting the line about a person having no such paper. It has his answer, in the office's account of what he said. And the last line, nine words long: ***The person named produced nothing.*** And then the half he could not have got to in a year: **the record does not say why he produced nothing. It does not repeat the four protections printed on a nail in a market two days' road back. It does not say that he was not required to account for the absence of a paper, that nothing in his possession was to be taken, that the capacity was assigned by the office and not one he holds. Four protections, all printed on a nail in a market in a town two days' road back, and not one of them is in the file. The sheet in the street protects a man. The record in the file does not, and the record is the one that goes to Ashmoor in a barrow, and there is nothing in it that says the man in it was owed anything. And it does not say what happened either. It is four hundred words of the shape of a room and not one word of what was in it.** He does not copy it, does not ask for a copy, and leaves with nothing: **a record of a hearing is a public document, it belongs to the court, and a man who puts a court record in a coat has mistaken a thing he may read for a thing he has got, and that is the mistake this whole business has been about for a hundred and five days.** Then, at about the seventh hour, **a letter, eleven days late, four lines, and not a charge** — signed with a hand that is not his and with no name, from Jerm in Kells: the yard is as it was and nothing has been taken out or put in; **the parish has painted round the Crown notice about the originating workshop on the ward board, and it is the only painted thing on that board and the ugliest thing on it, and I have looked at it every morning for eleven days and I have not stopped looking at it**; a district asked for the making of that bell in the winter and he sent the certificate and nothing has come back, **I have not chased it and I am not going to chase it, and I want that in a letter to you and not to anybody else, because you would chase it**; and **I have not rung anything. I have not gone in. I will not without you.** It is the fourth person in a hundred and five days who has written to or spoken to him about a thing in a county he has left and asked for nothing. He puts it in the coat with the thirteen and writes down that it is not a fourteenth **because it is not evidence of anything and it is a letter to a man and not a record of a fact, and that is a distinction he is drawing for himself and has not got to the bottom of and is not going to tonight.** **Nobody has asked him to read a charge. A man in Kells reads a charge for himself and does not let anybody do it for him, and that is a habit of his and not a thing this city has been asked for, and it stays in the county it was made in.** Day-book: **a court publishes and an office files, and the difference is four inches long, and it is the only thing anybody in this kingdom has got wrong in their own favour**; **the record does not repeat one of the four protections that were printed on a nail in a market two days' road back**; **a man in a market told me what it costs to be named and told it about somebody else, and I stood four feet off him and let him and did not say one word — and I have said in this book that I will not ask him anything else this year and I meant it on the ninetieth, and the fact that I have now heard his own figure second-hand is not a reason to go**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung**, four bolts, the carrier untouched, twenty-eight days, **thirteen documents in a coat, and a court record that is the public's and not mine and that I stood at a rail and read in about eleven minutes and took nothing from, and those two figures are correct and they are two different objects.**

## Chapter 0120 — If There Were Three Of Me

Day 106, **the fourth hour, bare** to **the ninth hour of the evening**, **Tomas Vale's POV**, Sennet — a step at the foot of the top stair, the Wharf Road, a yard of packing cases, the top terrace, a yard, a gate, a board, a rail, the same step. He is **eleven days into a city of about nine thousand people** with a hearing behind him, and he is doing the one thing he came for: to find out **what a city of about nine thousand bells sounds like when it is asked a question instead of given a notice.** He writes the question down in about nine seconds because it is the only question he has, and sits with it. The rule is stated whole, in the plain form it was given to him in an arcade in a town he has left: **four parts — something a person can walk to and touch; a living keeper with a specific accepted duty, named, and not the whole town; the three answers; and a carrier who strikes it, how many times, and what it is not permitted to add. And the three answers are the hard part and they are not the metal.** A public signal needs three, independent, each free to say yes, no, later, or this much and not that. **And the rule that does the work is four inches long: you set the claim down once and you read the same words to each of them in the same order, and if you improve the words for the second one because the first frightened you, you are not collecting answers, you are arranging agreement.** **He had not counted anything into iron since the fortieth day of this month, seventy-three days.** Then, flat on a step at about the fifth hour, what he has: **one — a plain carrier in a sack against the cradle, fourteen pounds two ounces, no wax, no dressed groove, a caster's mark and a day, bought at Bramden for one pound fourteen shillings, rung four times in its life, and it is a real bell and he could put it on a leather at about the sixth hour in that yard and strike it and about nine hundred people in this city would hear it. Two — the frame in its cradle, four bolts, a coupling cut nearly through, a jumper across it, a child-sized wax handprint in the crown, a warped lip, and no clapper, and a receipt in five clauses saying in the first of them that no person shall furnish one: it cannot be rung, it is the only bell in this kingdom he cannot have, and it is in the open thirty feet off him. Three — a keeper, and he has none, and the fifth clause says the holder has no standing to speak for any household in Kells or in any ward, and he asked for that clause because he was right. Four — a carrier, and he is it, and a carrier who is also the only person in the yard with a reason to be in the yard is a carrier who cannot be counted, because a bell one man put there and one man struck is a man talking. And three answers, and he has one.** At about the fifth hour a carter with three horses stops at the bottom of the stair because a barrow is in the way of a cart, and Tomas asks him to carry a thing up the four flights, **and had the objection to every word of it worked out before he opened his mouth.** **No. Not for nothing.** And then, without getting down: **I have nine loads a day and a boy on the second cart and I have carried other men's things up those steps for about nine years and I have never once carried a thing up them that nobody could account for afterwards. And here is the whole of it, and it is the same as a man who mends his own wire: if I put a thing down in a yard in this city that I was not told to put down, and there is no form on which anybody wrote down that I was told, then I have made an entry in somebody's day that only I know about, and the only way that ends is somebody stands in front of me with a reason and asks me about it. I have three horses and a boy on the second cart and I have not got a son to put in that gap, and I would like you to go and find a man with a form and I will carry it up four flights this afternoon.** Tomas **did not argue, did not thank him, did not say that he understood**, and wrote down that he had asked a man who was right and been told no in about nine seconds, and that the man had not asked what the thing was. Then **he asked a man for a true sentence on purpose, knowing what he was doing**, at a yard of packing cases: **Ormond Bly** — whose openness on the hundred and third now has its bridge written on the page, because he told a stranger then that he had watched him standing at a door and that a woman four hundred yards off had watched it too, and neither has asked him for anything since, and a man who keeps a gate for nine years is not going to be the one who offers twice — gives him one in about as long as it takes a man to look at a gate he has looked at every day for nine years — *I have a gate and a bell on it and I strike it myself and I have done it about four thousand times in nine years and about nine hundred of them were for a cart, and I have never once struck it for a thing I had not seen with my own eyes. That is the whole of what a bell is and I did not learn it in a book. I learned it in a yard* — and then, unasked, why it is worth nothing: **I have said that sentence about four hundred times in nine years at this rail and it has never once been in a book. The day a man comes to this rail and asks me for a sentence, that sentence has a man in it, and the man is you, and the next person who asks me whether I have ever had a belief put in my mouth is going to be somebody with a column, and I am not going to be the first entry in it. If you carry it up a hill it is a thing I said to a stranger, and in about four years there will be a man who says I said it because a bellmaker with a barrow asked me, and that man will be right, and I will not be able to say one word against it.** Then the chapter's own instrument, used on himself: he goes back over **every** true sentence anybody has said to him in this city in eleven days and puts three under each other — **a bell is a man and an hour and a reason** (a wharf on the flat, the ninety-seventh, the tenth trip, asked by nobody); **I have a district's business in this room and no column anywhere in this kingdom for the rest** (a counter four flights up, the hundredth, asked by nobody); **a bell is what I have learned in a yard and it is not in a book** (this morning, the fifth hour, asked by me). **Three men, three rooms, three days, three different things, and not one of them is about the other two, and about nine hundred people in this city would each have believed the one that suited them, and between them they say nothing at all.** At about the sixth hour he goes up the four flights into the Office's yard. **There was nothing on the plate.** The district had not sent a sheet, about forty people who came for the reading stood about a minute and went in again, and **the man with the book struck it four times anyway.** He is **Marek Douse**, six years at that rail, a book of his own, no office, paid by the quarter, **not doing it because he was told to**, and he says it to nobody, in the ordinary voice, in the open, in a yard with about two hundred people in it: **Six years on that frame and four strokes and it has meant one thing since the frame was new: there is a sheet on the board. I have never known it to mean anything else. I do not know whether I am to strike it today. Nobody has told me not to.** Somebody at the back says *There was nothing on it.* **There was nothing on it, and I struck it four times, and I have done that on about nine days out of a hundred in six years, and about two hundred of you came out of doors for the four, and I do not know what to tell you, because I have never asked anybody and there is nobody above me to ask and if there were I should not know what to ask him.** And then: **that is the whole of what I know about that bell and I have known it six years and I have never said it out loud in this yard, and I have said it now because there is a man standing in the middle of the yard with a barrow who has not asked me a single question, and that is the only reason in this kingdom there is going to be.** At about the seventh hour **a man sweeping the yard stops about nine feet off, and Tomas says nothing, and after about nine seconds the man says it, to the boards and not to him: Piet Sarn**, who has swept that yard **eleven years** and has never been in the upper room and never wanted to — **I could not tell you what is in this building. I know the light goes out of one window at about the sixth hour. That is nine hundred rooms and I know the light going out of one of them, and that is all I know, and I have never said it out loud and I have never had the occasion, and I have just had the occasion, because you have been standing in this yard for an hour and you have not asked me one thing and neither has anybody else, and I want that in a place where somebody has written it down.** Tomas offers his own book and the man says **he can do what he likes with it and it is not about him**, and sweeps on. Then the finding, in four seconds, in full: **three people said one true thing each today, in the open, in their own names, in a yard, and two of them said it because he asked and one because nobody did, and he was nine feet off all three of them. And they were about three different things. And if they had been about the same thing, in three places, on the same day, that would have been a warning, and about nine hundred people in this city would have heard one thing three times instead of one man telling them what to think about it. That is the whole of what a bell is for and he has known it since the eleventh of this month and has not used it since the fortieth. And he has got one man. That is the arithmetic and it took a hundred and six days and a carter with three horses and a yard and a gate. A man can be one of the three answers. A man cannot be two of them, and the second one he is, the first one stops being an answer and becomes a man saying the same thing twice, and a thing said twice by one man is a thing said once louder. And there is no form in this kingdom on which a man may go to three men and say the same words to each and have it be three answers, and if there were, the words would be his and all three would be one, and he would be doing the thing he has refused in four counties and a city, which is arranging agreement and calling it a count.** At about the eighth hour he takes the sack out from against the cradle, unrolls it on the step, puts his hand flat on the crown of the plain carrier and leaves it there, **and puts it back.** And the reason, in craft law and not in feeling: **a warning names what it does not know and tells nobody what to decide. I have made that shape four times in my life and rung it twice in a market and both times a stranger rang it and not me, and a bell that a man rings for himself is a man in a yard with a bell. And if I strike this one tonight, about two hundred people will hear four strokes and come out of their doors out of nine years of habit, and they will be out of doors in about nine minutes, because a bell they have heard every day of their lives is not a signal to them, it is the hour. And I have no keeper and no bar and no third and fourth answer, and a thing said three times that is not the same thing is three things, and I cannot make them the same thing without lying to two of them.** At about the ninth hour of the evening on the same step, Tamsin ten feet off his left shoulder with a bell in her bag she rang once, in a market, for a reason that had not been him. **She carries the transit on the page and did not before the review pass: she had been four hundred yards off on the middle terrace on the hundred and third, watching a man not go through a door, and she came up off it on the fourth of this month and did not say why, and he did not ask, and it is the fourth day of not asking.** **He did not put a question on the slate, and wrote that down once, and then wrote under it that he is not going to write it down again, because she said in her own book on the hundred and second that the third time she writes it she will be counting, and a woman who counts is a woman waiting to be owed, and he is not owed and neither is she. The count is eight.** She has said about nine sentences in eleven days and been asked for nothing by anybody in nine thousand people, and tonight she says one — **his own sentence, four inches, at ten feet, in the ordinary voice, and he did not improve it:** *You have got a barrow and a bell and no keeper and no second person, and you have known that since you were nine feet off a door, and you have spent a hundred and six days finding out that the reason you cannot do it is arithmetic and not the Office.* And: **Then you are going to have to be three men, and there is one of you, and I am not going to be the second, and if anybody in this city asks me which one I am I am going to tell them nothing at all, and you knew every word of that before you asked, because you did not ask.** And then the two halves, and they do not meet: **on the hundred and third a man in a room with a lamp in it entered one person's answer into a column in about four seconds and it came out of the column as the answer of the whole matter, and there is a file four streets below this step that says in the office's own hand that the person named produced nothing, and it is four hundred words long and it is the only document about Tomas Vale that exists in the world. And on the same city, three days later, three people said three true things in three yards, and about two hundred people stood in one of them and let a bell ring four times for a sheet that was not on the board, and none of the three was asked to be about his business, and none of the three is an answer to anything, and none of the three can be joined to the other two, and he is not going to join them. Those are the two halves and they do not meet and I have been looking for a place where they do for a hundred and six days. The Office can make an answer for a person. It cannot stop three people answering for themselves. And neither of those is a victory, and I am not going to be the man who says the second one is the answer, because I have already said once this month that a thing I liked was the answer and I was wrong about it in a loft on the ninety-ninth with nine tons of grain on the floor, and I am not doing it twice. What I have got is three people and a yard and about nine hundred bells in this city, and not one of them has been rung tonight for anything anybody in it chose. If there were three of me this city could be told something today. And there is one of me, and one of me is what a hundred and six days buys, and it is the best thing I have ever had in my hands and I have written it down and I am not going to do a single thing with it tonight.** Day-book: **the answer is that it does not, and the reason is arithmetic, and I have got to the bottom of the road in both directions inside four days of each other and neither one has a door at the end of it**; **I did not arrange one word of it and I have written down that I asked two of the three and that the asking is the whole of the damage**; **and one of them has asked me for a piece of paper and I have given him my own book and not a copy**; **and there is no form in this kingdom on which a man may take the same words to three men and call the result a count. If there were three of me**; **I asked a man who was right to carry a thing and he told me no and I did not thank him and I am not going to be told it was worth it** — **that clause of my own receipt has now done four jobs and it will do a fifth one inside a month and it will not be mine**; all five clauses stand, **nothing rung, by me, in this city, in eleven days**, four bolts, the frame not in a building since the thirty-third, the carrier unrolled and put back, **thirteen documents, and a record of a hearing that is the public's and not mine, and a letter from Kells is not a fourteenth because it is a letter to a man**; **I read the store list a leaf at a time on two boards said I should, and I did not take either leaf, and the list said nothing about the third question and nothing about what ninety words are for, and I am not going to write to a woman about it, because she did not ask me**; **the five dates in a drawer in a county I have left are where they were on the ninety-second, and I have not touched them in a hundred and six days and I am not going to touch them this month, and I have not explained them to myself and I am not going to**; and the third question: **a format has no door and a city has about nine hundred doors and every one of them is a man.**
